LADY BY MARRIAGE

CHAPTER I
JOHN AMBROSE, just in at the end of a long after-
noon's rounds, heard the front-door bell followed by
the sound of voices, and scowled. If it was a call he'd
have to go, of course. With only seven years of private
practice between to-day and the taking of the degree
that allowed him to write the letters M.B. after his
name and put up his plate, he couldn't afford to turn
down cases even if he were tired and fed up and bored
to tears with life as a general practitioner in a small
village. And it wouldn't be anything interesting. That
stood to reason. Interesting cases, he told himself,
still scowling, didn't come his way. Panel patients were
his lot as a general rule; broken limbs, infected cuts,
coughs and colds, an occasional pneumonia or bron-
chitis patient, and confinements. They were the cases
that made up his daily round. People with interesting
illnesses, who could afford to pay, whose recovery
might and probably would add not merely to their
medical advisor's bank balance but to his professional
kudos into the bargain, went to Allenborough when
they needed a doctor, or even to London if things were
at all serious, rather than trust their valuable carcases
and internal mechanisms to the hands of a compara-
tively inexperienced young G.P. in their own village.
So the chances were that the bell wouldn't mean
anything more interesting than a premature baby or a
cut foot or a scalded hand, and yet he'd have to go.
Panel doctors, just starting-
3
His housekeeper put her head round the corner of
the door and at once he sensed disapproval. It was
written large on the upward lift of her long Scotch
nose and in the prim twist at the corners of her mouth.
"'Tis a message from the district nurse. She's want-
ing ye over to the fair-ground. Seems there's one of
yon
fair-women took sick there. I told the bairn to say
ye'd be over when ye'd drunk your tea, but I can't
say that I think fair-grounds right or fittin' places for
young doctors to be sent to. I was brought up to look
upon fair-grounds"
"I know," John Ambrose said. "As sinks of iniquity
and hotbeds of vice. You've said it all before, and I
dare say you're right, although people get ill in them
just as quickly as in nice respectable Presbyterian beds.
All right, Mrs. M'Queen, I'll go."
Mrs. M'Queen said grudgingly:
"I think the woman's bad, doctor. The district nurse
said she'd be glad if ye'd get over just as quick as ye
could. I said ye was having your tea
""
"I'm through," John Ambrose said, and picked up
the bag he'd flung down beside his desk only ten min-
utes earlier. "I'll be back for supper at seven, Mrs.
M'Queen."
Across at the fair-ground things were just beginning
to wake up. Half a dozen farm lads and their girls
were strolling about a field already bearing the dishev-
eled and unkempt appearance that characterizes fair-
grounds all over the world. In the middle a merry-
go-round composed alternately of unlikely-looking
piebald horses and midget motor-cars churned its noisy
way to the raucous wailing of a ten-year-old popular
music-hall ditty. There were gypsies offering to tell for-
tunes for threepence; tents that held mysterious "mar-
vels"-"Walk up, ladies and gents-walk up! Only one
4
penny. Just one penny to see the world's greatest
living curiosity"; a couple of cages wherein a tiger with
eyes full of a curious depressed resignation, and a sulky,
mangy lion, lay staring resentfully through the bars that
trapped them. There was a wooden platform on which
two men in grubby pink tights were throwing a girl in
an abbreviated spangled swimming-suit from one to the
other with, apparently, complete indifference as to her
personal comfort or even as to her bodily safety; a row
of swing-boats that were empty save for one holding a
couple of screaming village girls; stalls full of sticky,
vividly-colored sweets, of flat, indigestible cakes, of
gingerbread snaps, of peppermint rock that resembled,
more closely than anything else, the poles that desig-
nate a barber's shop, with, over it all, the hot sultry
heaviness of a lazy August afternoon; the faint unmis-
takable tang of humanity not too closely acquainted
with soap and water; the hoarse barking of voices,
"Walk up, ladies and gents-walk up!" and the threat,
carried in the steadily thickening bank of clouds over
to the westward, of thunder and rain that would as-
suredly fall before the night ended.
Leaving the car outside the gate, young Ambrose
went across the field to the corner where a cluster of
tents and untidy caravans marked the troupe's probable
living quarters. Once a man touched his hat, "Evenin',
doctor," as he passed; a girl ogled him shamelessly; a
couple of boys who played cricket in the village team
for which he was the only fast bowler-and indeed the
only bowler at all worthy of the title-grinned at him
sheepishly, and then he was amongst the heterogeneous
collection of dingy caravans and tents that made up the
living quarters of half a hundred people. A woman,
washing up dishes on the steps of a caravan said, "Ye're
the doctor, ain't you?" and jerked her head in the
5
'direction of a tent a little distance from the rest.
"Mary's over yonder. If she's still 'ere at all. You're
too late to be any use, I reckon, Mister," and then he
saw the district nurse. She had come to the door of the
tent in the far corner and was emptying on to the grass
a bowl of water dyed a deep ominous pink-a trim,
competent little person with about her the expression
that said as loudly and clearly as any words could have
said it: "I know my job perfectly, thank you, and un-
less I ask for help I prefer your room to your company,
so please keep out."
She saw young Ambrose and greeted him with a nod.
Brief and cursory. Wasting no breath in idle words.
Stating the case clearly and decisively.
"Premature confinement. Eight months. Baby's dead
and the mother's dying. I thought I'd better send for
you, though there's nothing to be done. She's been
underfed, I should say, for months, and the man's left
her. Come in, doctor."
He followed her, sniffing distastefully, into the
squalid little tent. A woman whose face was only a
shade less gray than the blanket that covered her, and
the same color as the once white nightgown that
clothed a body so emaciated as to be almost devoid of
flesh altogether, lay on a cot bed in one corner. A child
of perhaps six stared with huge terrified eyes from
under a thatch of unbrushed, unwashed red hair in the
opposite corner, and there was for furniture a table
and one chair.
John Ambrose, leaning over the bed, brisk and com-
petent, said:
"Send that child out, Nurse," and found his hand
gripped suddenly with fingers as thin as those of a
skeleton, but fingers that had, for a dying woman,
amazing strength.
6
"No I want her. Here. I want her to stop. Don't
let her go out there. Not alone. See? Out there"-she
caught her breath sharply, painfully-"they're bad. I
want her here."
"All right," John Ambrose said. He was surprised
a little by the voice, although lately he had learnt to be
surprised at so little, because it wasn't a voice that
toned in any degree with its surroundings. It might, he
thought, busying himself with stethoscope and ther-
mometer and his watch, belong to a woman who hailed
from some wind-swept, heather-covered moorland vil-
lage, was certainly not what he'd expected to hear.
"Lie still and don't worry! She shan't go out if you'd
rather she stayed here. Now keep still, there's a good
girl. I shan't bother you much."
"It's no use, is it?" the woman said. She was, per-
haps, thirty-perhaps a year or two less. "I knew,
all along, it wouldn't be any use. And the sooner it's
over-except for her." She jerked her head in the
direction of the child in the corner. "That's what's
worrying me. When I go- Mister, listen." Her eyes,
black as sloes, set in hollows that were like huge sooty
saucers, held him in spite of himself so that he found
himself unable to turn away, unable even not to listen
to what she was saying. "When I go, you take her.
Don't leave her here. If she's left without me—there's
nothing for her but an end like mine. D'ye see? And
she's a good kid. She is a good kid. She don't lie and
she don't steal like the other kids. Mister, listen-
99
"You're talking too much," John Ambrose said, and
she frowned impatiently.
"What does it matter? And I've got to make you
understand. Those kids-out there-they lie and steal,
and Sheila's not like that. I've seen to it she don't lie
or steal, but if she's left here-when I'm gone-
7
Mister, take her with you. You're kind. Take her and
keep her. Bring her up decently-
99
The district nurse interrupted briskly.
"That's impossible, my good woman. In the first
place, the doctor's not even married-
99
"I wasn't talking to you," the woman on the bed
said. There came into her eyes even then a flash of
spirit that made them suddenly beautiful. "Mister,
you'll do it, won't you? She's nobody, nobody at all.
Her father-we wasn't married and she's nobody. If
she's left here"
"You'll kill yourself if you keep on talking like this,"
John Ambrose said. "Just lie still-"
"I've got to know," the woman said insistently.
"If I knew she'd be all right I could die in peace. And
God knows I'd be happier dead. For six years-
She stopped short. "Mister, do this. She's only a bit
of a thing. Think of what it'll mean for her left here
alone without even her mother. I've been a fool. That's
what I've been all along, but I'm not bad. I swear to
God I'm not a bad woman, Mister. I've only gone
with men when I've loved 'em and that's not being
bad. There ain't bad blood in Sheila-only wild like
her father an' silly like me, but if she's left-say you'll
take her and let me die in peace, Mister."
Behind him at his shoulder, alert, competent, hard
as the enamel bowl in her hands, John Ambrose could
hear the district nurse muttering to herself:
"It's ridiculous. The whole thing. If he's any
sense" And then, to his blank horror and amaze-
ment, heard his own voice, steady, level, far gentler
and kinder than he'd ever heard it, saying words he'd
never had even the foggiest, faintest intention of
saying.
"I'll take her, girl. Don't you worry. I'll take her
8
and I'll keep her. I don't know what the hell I'll do
with her, but-if it'll make things any easier—I'll
promise you this much anyway. She shan't stay here
alone. I'll take her home with me and I'll keep her."
9
CHAPTER II
DRIVING home a couple of hours later John Am-
brose, for the first time in the last astonishing three
hours, remembered his housekeeper and wondered
what on earth he was going to say when he reached
home and had to produce his passenger. The district
nurse had made the matter clear enough, twenty min-
utes earlier. She'd said:
"Personally, if you'll forgive me for speaking
bluntly, doctor, I think you're crazy. Have you the
least idea what you're going to do with the child? Or
what you've let yourself in for? Now, for goodness'
sake, do be sensible about this. Give the child to me
and I'll take her over to the orphanage-
99
"Nothing doing," John Ambrose said, and tightened
his hold on the miserable little object by his side.
"You're very kind and thoughtful and all that, Nurse,
and I'm deeply appreciative, but at the same time a
promise is a promise. I gave that poor girl in there my
word, and, by gad! I'm going to keep it. I may have
been a fool, but there you are." He added, looking
down at the tousled head beside him, "I suppose she'll.
wash, won't she?"
"Washing," the district nurse said grimly, "will be
the least of your troubles, doctor. You can rest assured
of that anyway." She eyed distastefully and distrust-
fully the small child who'd been given away very much
as a doll or a puppy might have been given, and who
was apparently either too scared or apathetic or grief-
ΙΟ
stricken to take any part in the conversation or in the
transaction. "I don't think you've the least idea of the
size of the job you've undertaken, doctor."
John Ambrose knew that much at least without
being told. He'd realized it the moment that crazy
impulse had put into his mouth and on to his lips the
words that had made him the owner of the child her
mother had called Sheila, and knowing it didn't make
the job any less. He was definitely unhappy, too, as
to the result of the whole episode on Mrs. M'Queen
who, while being an excellent woman and a super-
excellent housekeeper for a young bachelor, was not in
the habit of leaving unsaid those things she thought it
advisable to say, and who, moreover, took good care
that, in the saying, they lost nothing of their original
value.
Mrs. M'Queen wouldn't, of course, approve of any
child being brought into the household, but she'd ap-
prove still less of Sheila. Who, in addition to being the
daughter of a despised and distrusted fair-woman, was,
also, everything contrary to her sense of what was
right and fitting and proper. Squinting at her sideways
from his place at the wheel, John Ambrose couldn't
help feeling that, if Mrs. M'Queen did disapprove-
and there was no possible reason to hope that she
wouldn't-she would, at least, have legitimate grounds
for disapproval from practically every known and rec-
ognized standpoint.
In the first place, she had for dirt in any shape or
form very much the same feeling as that actuated in
the devil by holy water, and there was no gainsaying
the fact that the child was dirty. She looked, John
Ambrose told himself, as though she'd seen neither
soap and water nor brush and comb for at least a
month, and as though she'd made acquaintance far
I I
closer than at all necessary with every available con-
dition of grime. She wore a tattered bedraggied gar-
ment closely resembling a sack by way of a frock, and
over it a pinafore so incredibly dirty as to be prac-
tically unrecognizable as such, a pair of ragged cotton
drawers, far too big for her, odd shoes down at the
heel and out at the toes, and nothing else. Her hair
looked sticky and practically uncombable, and some
one had evidently tried to console her for the impend-
ing loss of her mother by the gift of a stick of pepper-
mint rock, to the greater part of which her face still
bore ample evidence. Of conversation she had, ap-
parently, none at all. She had stood in the corner of
the tent in perfect silence, listening while her mother
panted out her last pitiful wishes, staring while she
died; she'd followed John Ambrose when he called her,
very much as a cowed and badly whipped mongrel cur
might have followed its recognized and admitted
master, across the field and into the car, and from start
to finish she'd never spoken a word. The woman who'd
been washing dishes on her caravan steps when John
Ambrose first arrived had come over and bidden her
good-by.
"Now you be a good girl, lovey, and do what the
young gent'man tells yer, see?" She'd spoken in a
wheedling, coaxing voice that had somehow irritated
John Ambrose unbearably. "Ye're a lucky gel, you are,
an' no mistake, you mark my words. I'll bet yer poor
ma in 'eaven's 'appy ter see w'ere ye're going, so mind
ye're a good girl, lovey." And on the way across the
field a man and two other women had stopped and
spoken to her. The man had included John Ambrose,
too, in the conversation. He was obviously the boss of
the show, a big florid man with shifty eyes and a
plausible tongue.
12
"You'll understand we couldn't do nothin' for her,
pore soul," he said. ""Tisn't even as if she was one
of us proper even, 'cos she ain't. Only picked her up,
pore thing, ten days or so back an' kep' her jest out of
kindness, you might say. Not but what she didn't try
to earn her keep a bit with the washin' up and cleanin'
an' that. I'll say that for her, but she was a pore thing.
No guts, if you'll excuse the word, Mister. She said the
man'd left her six months ago and she'd never tell 'is
name. But o' course, we'll see her buried decent, an'
all that. You can trust to us that way, Mister."
One of the women had wept, too, sentimentally.
"Ain't it crool the way things go, doctor? This pore
little kid, lef' all alone. You can say what you like, but
life's crool hard on women, s'welp me if it ain't. Say,
ducky, give us a kiss. Ye're the lucky one an' no mis-
take, being took off by a nice young gent'man this way.
Be a gran' lady one o' these days I wouldn't wonder.
'Bye, ducky."
The child had submitted to the kiss as she'd done
everything else: in utter and complete silence and
apathy, and then just as silently had climbed into the
car and let John Ambrose take her away. He said to
her once, more because he felt he'd got to say some-
thing, than because she needed reassurance:
"There's nothing to be afraid of with me. I shan't
hurt you or anything, you know, and I expect you'll
have rather a good time really. You're not afraid, are
you?" And she'd stared at him, dumbly, for a long
moment, and then when he repeated the question:
"You're not afraid, are you, Sheila ?" shook her head.
After which they drove on in silence, with the child's
thoughts only God knew where and those of John
Ambrose fluctuating uncomfortably between the pic-
ture of Mrs. M'Queen confronted with the latest ad-
13
dition to the household and the bewildering, utterly
unsolvable puzzle of what, now he'd got her, he was
going to do with her.
Mrs. M'Queen, however, left him little room for
doubt as to her intentions and no time at all. She met
him on the doorstep with the news that his supper was
cold-"Wasting good food, I call it, doctor, just hang-
ing around an' letting all yer meals get cold on ye this
way so they don't do ye any good at all"; and then saw
his passenger. "What in the name of the good God
have ye got there, doctor!"
A little apologetic now that the time to break the
news had actually arrived, he told her.
"She'll be different altogether when she's washed,
Mrs. M'Queen. I shouldn't be surprised if she's almost
pretty, and I'm sure you'll agree there was nothing
else I could do. After all, the poor little thing's lost
her mother-
"Are ye expecting me to wash her then?" Mrs.
M'Queen said dangerously, "because if ye are, I tell ye
now, this minnit, ye're makin' the greatest mistake
ye've ever made in all your misguided, senseless life,
Doctor Ambrose. I came here to take care of you and
to see ye get yer meals proper as your blessed mother,
God rest her soul, would have wished ye to have them,
and I did not come to do nursemaid to children picked
up just any which way off a fair-ground which, in any
case, is no place for children at all nor for respectable
men and women either, as I've made plain to ye time
an' time agen. And so I've told ye. If she stays in
this house I go, an' that's flat, doctor, so ye can now
choose your way between us, an' if ye've any sense,
which I badly doubt, ye'll know the side yer bread's
buttered an' send that child back where she came from
an' fergit ye've ever been so crazy as to think ye could
14
keep her here. Perhaps ye'll be good enough to say
which way ye'll be choosin', doctor?"
"I have chosen," John Ambrose said steadily. Des-
perately he was wishing that he'd had more sense;
that he'd stopped to think not once but twice and
three times before he'd made such a crazy promise to a
dying woman. But wishing wouldn't undo the mistake
he'd made or bring back, so that she could absolve him
from his undertaking, the woman to whom he had
given his word of honor, and there was nothing he
could do save abide by it. "I gave that girl my word,
and nothing you can say's going to make a ha'porth of
difference. I'm sorry, Mrs. M'Queen, but my mind's
just as firmly made up as yours is, so there we are. And
if you won't bath the child I'll have to go out and get
Mrs. Allister to do it instead. She's a good woman and
she'll come like a shot if I ask her." He held out his
hand to the dismal little creature on the doorstep.
"Come on, Sheila. Let's go and find Mrs. Allister.
She'll soon give you a bath and put you to rights, my
child."
Mrs. Allister agreed to the request, as John Am-
brose had known she would, like a shot, quickly and
efficiently taking over complete control.
"'Tis a splendid thing you've done, doctor," she
said warmly, and eyed, with no distaste in her face at
all, the child young Dr. Ambrose had brought in his
wake. "I'll be over this minute with some clothes and
things for the poor wee mite. Don't you worry yer
head any longer, doctor. I'll see to things all right,
and before you can say Jack Robinson, either. Come
over here, you poor little thing, and let's see what
we're going to do about you, shall we? You leave her
to me, doctor. I'll wash her here while you go and
15
have yer supper, and then I'll bring her over. She'll be
all right with me, won't you, girlie ?"
Back in his own house, eating the supper that, in
her wrath, Mrs. M'Queen had made no effort to
reheat, John Ambrose had time to look back and to
realize, aghast, to what lengths his inability to hurt
people more especially to hurt a woman-and his
absurd quixotry and good nature had led him. He was
thirty-five and a bachelor, and to ease the mind of a
dying woman he'd never seen before he'd saddled
himself with the responsibility of a peculiarly unpre-
possessing child not for merely a few days but for life,
and now that he'd got her, what on earth was he to do
with her? Mrs. M'Queen would go, of course. When
she said a thing she meant it, and it'd be months, per-
haps years, perhaps even the entire rest of his life,
before he found another housekeeper who would be
a patch on Mrs. M'Queen as far as her domestic
abilities were concerned. And the loss of Mrs.
M'Queen was only a drop in the ocean. A drop, so
small in so vast an ocean, as to be scarcely recogniz-
able, because the more one thought of what one had
done, the more vast became the ocean of one's folly,
the more overwhelming the knowledge of the responsi-
bilities that must, of necessity, follow in that folly's
wake.
He finished a supper that was cold and unpalatable
rather than ring the bell before it was actually nec-
essary to request that it might be reheated, then sat
back in his chair to think things out. He'd done a
crazy thing, of course. Adopting a child at the best of
times was a chancy speculation and one of which he'd
never really approved, but to take over the child of a
woman of whom he knew nothing beyond the facts
16
he'd seen for himself and her own dying confession
that she'd been a fool, was pure idiocy.
"I ought to be shot!" he told himself disgustedly.
"If the Medical Council ever gets to hear of this
they'll debar me from practicing, and they'll be right.
Congenital idiots ought not to be allowed to tinker
with other people's bodies and lives. They want put-
ting away, locking up. Kept where they can't make
damn fools of themselves; and in God's name what
am I going to do with her?"
Mrs. M'Queen, coming in to clear away the supper
things, didn't prove helpful. She said, facing him from
the opposite side of the hearthrug:
"I'm wishing to give ye a month's notice from
to-day, doctor, an' if it's all the same to ye I'll forfeit
my wages and get out to-morrow. When I came
here-
99
John Ambrose said abruptly, interrupting her:
"You can go to-night if you want to, Mrs.
M'Queen," with a great deal more dignity than he
felt. "There's not the slightest need for you to put
yourself out on my account. I can manage perfectly
well, thank you." He went over to his desk, wrote out
a cheque, and put it on the tray she was piling up with
plates and dishes. "Of course, I shouldn't think of
allowing you to forfeit your wages, and I think that
settles things, doesn't it?" He picked up his book
again, determined, whatever he felt, to carry off the
whole affair with a high hand. "I'm sorry, of course,
you've decided to go, but I quite understand your
feelings on the matter, and I dare say it's really the
best thing for both of us."
It was just then, while Mrs. M'Queen was still
opening and shutting her mouth in an endeavor to
find once again the wind he had taken from the sails of
17
her righteous indignation, that Mrs. Allister appeared.
She came in through the back door Mrs. M'Queen had
left open for purposes of air and possible disinfection,
and finding no one in the kitchen, came into the hall
and in the direction of the voices, and with her a small,
shy, adorably lovely little person that must, John
Ambrose told himself stupidly, be the child Sheila and
yet who bore, save in actual size, no possible resem-
blance whatever to the disreputable little object of
misery he had brought home with him from the fair-
ground an hour and a half earlier.
Because, washed from head to foot in more soap
and water than she had ever seen in her life before,
scrubbed and polished as only Mrs. Allister, who had
brought up and sent out into the world seven ultra-
successful and virtuous house-parlormaid daughters,
knew how to scrub and polish grubby little girls; with
her red curls washed too, and brushed and glowing
like burnished copper in the glow of a hot coal fire;
with the dirt that had hidden from view the loveliness
of her little red mouth and the deep gray pools of
her eyes no longer in evidence; clad in a blue smocked
linen frock that had once belonged to Mary Elizabeth
Allister, and shyly, deliciously self-conscious, the fair-
woman's child was the most beautiful thing John
Ambrose had ever seen in the whole course of his life.
18
CHAPTER III
IT came as a surprise to John Ambrose to learn that
even an unwanted, unclaimed waif of the fair-grounds
couldn't be adopted without a certain number of legal
formalities. Colonel Egerton, who was a magistrate
and prided himself on his knowledge of the law, took
the trouble to come in person after lunch two days
later to point out that, if he really meant to keep the
child, something must be done about it.
"Personally," Colonel Egerton said, repeating al-
most verbatim the words of the district nurse, "I think
you're a fool, Ambrose. What the hell are you-a
bachelor of your age-going to do with a child like
that? And when you marry what's the girl going to
say? Ever thought of that?"
"No, sir," John Ambrose said cheerfully. "I don't
intend to marry, you see, so that side of the matter
doesn't have to be taken into consideration. Doctors,"
he added a little sententiously, "shouldn't marry. They
haven't time. If they do they've either got to neglect
their patients or their wives, and neither's satisfactory
for long. As to the other side of the question, I'm not
sure yet. It's a bit early to make decisions, and Sheila
is very young. I'm also not at all sure that I've been
the fool you and every other man or woman of my
acquaintance thinks me. Have you seen Sheila, sir?”
"I've heard enough about her," Colonel Egerton
said, and John Ambrose got up and rang the bell.
"The whole place is buzzing. Can't talk of another
19
damned thing. My girls say she's not a bad-looking
child, take her all round, either."
"She's not," John Ambrose said. He sounded,
Colonel Egerton thought, complacent and a little
smug, and when the bell was answered he spoke crisply
to the girl who, produced from somewhere by Mrs.
Allister, and under the supervision of that good woman
herself was, pending the engagement of Mrs.
M'Queen's successor, "doing for" the doctor and at
the same time “giving an eye" to the child. "Bring
Sheila in, will you, Mary? Colonel Egerton wants to
see her."
Before her marriage Mrs. Allister had been a chil-
dren's nurse. It was her boast that she'd worked in the
families of a duchess and two countesses, in addition
to which she'd brought into the world, cared for,
clothed, and fed seven daughters of her own, and while
Sheila might be the illegitimate child of a down-at-heel,
out-of-luck, fair-woman, she was none the less a small
girl, and as such was entitled to the benefit of Mrs.
Allister's knowledge and experience. John Ambrose,
too, a little startled and more than a little pleased at
the success of the bathing operations, had given her a
cheque for ten pounds, which was far more than he'd
any right to spend, together with a free hand, and
armed with both Mrs. Allister had taken the train
and Sheila into Allenborough and spent the morning
there.
The result was far more satisfactory than even John
Ambrose had dared to hope. Mrs. Allister was by
nature a sensible, practical woman, and if a flair for
dressing children had been left out of her original
makeup it had been planted there very firmly and in- ·
eradicably by the duchess and the two subsequent
countesses. The clothes she bought for Sheila were
20
""
plain, devoid of frills and furbelows, unobtrusive in
color, excellent in taste, and dainty of texture. She'd
said to John: "With hair as red as hers, doctor, she
don't need much outside color, so to speak. Just blues
and whites and greens 'll be what she needs most I'd
say, so if you'll just let me get what I think best-
and John Ambrose had been only too glad to let her
please herself, and certainly he'd been justified in what
he'd done. Sheila, appearing in answer to his request
in charge of the "girl," and clad in one of her new
frocks, bore no resemblance whatever to the unhappy
little object he'd brought home two days earlier. Mrs.
Allister had put her into a little smock of green linen
that was short enough to show quite clearly a pair of
absurdly abbreviated green knickers that matched the
frock, and save for a pair of green leather sandals
her feet and legs were bare. Her hair, brushed and
shimmering, was redder, Colonel Egerton thought,
than any hair he'd ever seen, and her skin and eyes
were lovely. He said, staring and a little startled:
"Is this good Lord, Ambrose, the kid's a beauty!
Come over here, little girl. Well, well, you amaze me,
Ambrose. Upon my soul-aren't you going to shake
hands with me, little girl?"
Left inside the door Sheila stood exactly where she'd
been put. Her eyes, wary and watchful, on the alert
for the cuffs and blows that, for as long as she could
remember, had been associated in her mind with the
close proximity of men, slid from young Ambrose,
standing on the hearthrug, to Colonel Egerton staring
in amazement from the chair by the window. They
looked safe enough, almost, if any men could be, they
looked kind, but experience had taught her long ago
that with men, with all men, it was better and wiser
to go steadily and to watch one's step with caution and
21
care. So she stood, just where Mary had put her, look-
ing from one to the other till John Ambrose spoke to
her.
"Come over and say how d'you do to Colonel
Egerton, Sheila," he said, and then, explanatorily,
despite his obvious smugness, "She's terribly shy, poor
little soul. Can't get a word out of her, try as I will.
Scared, probably. But she's not bad to look at, is she,
sir? Come on, Sheila. No one's going to hurt you."
Six years' experience of life, however, had taught
Sheila wisdom where men were concerned. A lot of
them spoke kindly at first, even coaxingly. Not, cer-
tainly, in the voices used by these two men, who spoke
a language she'd never heard before; but one never
knew with any men, and it was far better, if one didn't
want to get hurt, to keep as great a distance as pos-
sible between one's own small body and hands that,
actuated by irritation and annoyance, could hit so hard
and hurt so much. So she stood still by the doorway,
her eyes unwavering and wary, and in complete silence.
And John Ambrose shrugged his shoulders.
"If we leave her alone she may thaw out a bit," he
said. "She's been hurt, of course. And frightened.
Beaten good and hard from time to time too, judging
by the marks on her, poor little brat. Still, I'm not a
bit sorry now that I took her, you know, sir."
Long, long afterwards, John Ambrose used to say
that the popularity which at forty-five made him one
of the most sought-after consulting children's physi-
cians in London began with the advent of Sheila into
the Corner House. Whether that is actually true or not
it is certain that he had never been so greatly in de-
mand, never had so many callers, as during those early
days that followed his crazy adoption of the little waif
from the fair-ground. They were, of course, mostly
22
women, and while some of them came out of frank and
shameless curiosity a few came because not to take an
active interest in "that poor boy with a baby of that
age, and no woman in the house except just a working
housekeeper," was deliberately flying in the face of a
Providence who had no use for people too apathetic
not to answer when he knocked at their doors.
As a general rule they found themselves confronted,
when they called, by Mrs. Warren, who was Mrs.
M'Queen's successor and who had the rare gift of a
sense of humor, combined with a very shrewd idea as
to the hopes that prompted the gifts and anxious in-
quiries and tender solicitude of most of the younger
women callers who came so often to the Corner House
to ask after Sheila. Occasionally they were luckier, and
finding John Ambrose himself, made the most of their
opportunities; but even Celia Gresham, who was really
quite charming and had two hundred a year of her
own, had to admit a little reluctantly that she couldn't
"get far" with young Dr. Ambrose. And certainly no
one got very far with Sheila, who stood perfectly still,
staring at them while they fondled her and petted her
and cajoled her, and who refused, point-blank, to take
a single forward step in response to their eager
advances.
The chances are that none of them, probably not
even John Ambrose himself, really had any knowledge
of the existence she'd led throughout the first six years
of her baby life. That she'd been beaten was obvious.
There were marks even now across her back and shoul-
ders that made him feel murderous every time he saw
them, and one never saw her eyes, furtive and wary,
without realizing, with a shock of remembrance of
one's own fear-free childhood, that fear had been the
predominating characteristic of her life. John Am-
23
brose, delving into legalities for permission to adopt
her, had learnt all he wanted to know as to her par-
entage. The big florid boss of the fair-ground had
brought him, the day following the fair-woman's
death, a tin box he'd found under her bed, and while
in themselves the contents had not told him much they
had at least given him a clew he could follow up, and
ultimately he'd been able to dig out the whole sordid
little story and learn the truth. It had been, of course,
just the usual thing. The headstrong, pretty daughter
of ultra-respectable Methodist farm-laborer parents,
who, refusing to go into "service" in the "big house,"
had gone instead as assistant to the barmaid at the
local pub and made friends of people her father and
mother would never have allowed inside their cottage
door. It was there she'd met Garry Morgan. He'd been
a bare-back rider in a circus, a splendid, colorful young
animal to whom all women were fair game, and Mary
Kavanagh had been easy. Who he was, apart from
the name he gave her, where he'd come from, no one
knew, and the chances are that, dazzled by his looks,
by his big laughing voice, by his strength and virility
and the gipsy vagabondage that lent more charm to
his little finger than she had ever seen before in a
man's whole body, little country Mary didn't even
care. She took him home to the cottage, and when her
parents refused him admission went with him back to
the show-ground and stayed with him. When, seven
months later, he was just beginning to weary of her
and to wish he hadn't let her pretty Irish eyes get such
a hold on him that he'd brought her away with him
in the first place, and especially to wish that hot,
passionate nights, spent body to body and lip to lip,
didn't so often result in the birth of unwanted children,
he was savaged by a horse of which he'd been warned
24
and of which he'd refused to be afraid, and died two
hours afterwards. Mary, stunned and sick of body and
soul and completely broken of heart, did the only thing
of which she could think and went back home. What
happened when she got there, heavy with the weight
of the circus-rider's child, John Ambrose could only
guess. He went to the cottage himself so that he should
know exactly where he stood, and neither old Kav-
anagh nor his wife would say a word. That they'd shut
the door in her face he learnt from a neighbor who'd
given the girl a shelter for the night, and then the
next morning, refusing further offers of a roof over
her head, at all events until the child came, she'd gone
back to the circus. There, amongst men and women to
whom birth and life and marriage and death mean
little or nothing, who take things as they come, asking
no questions, her child was born. In the tent of the
girl who'd been Garry Morgan's assistant in the circus-
ring and who, though Mary had never known it, had
been, too, for weeks past, the second string on his
emotional violin. After that, for a year, she'd gone
with the crowd, doing odd jobs for one and another,
earning her keep as best she could until the circus hit
a couple of bad seasons in succession and went broke.
John Ambrose, delving for facts, lost her after that.
She'd gone with the child, God only knew where, a
poor, feckless little thing, all her prettiness, her gayety,
her Irish fun dead and buried in the grave of the man
to whom she'd been nothing but a passing ship. There
were scraps of news now and then, but nothing reliable,
nothing that was a definite fact. She'd been seen here
and there and everywhere. Hanging, with the child, on
to the tail end of cheap, tawdry circuses and fairs,
snaffling a penny here and there. She'd gone to pieces
altogether, lost her looks, even what was left of her
25
pride. There'd been at least two other children and
they'd died. Nobody knew who the men had been. One
woman, wronging her cruelly in her ignorance, told
him cynically: "Women like us has got to live, Mister,"
though Mary had never given herself to any man for
money. Hungry for love, she'd jumped at anything
even distantly resembling the thing she'd found with
Garry Morgan, but money had never been a party to
any of her dealings with the men who, loving so lightly
and having had their fill, left the women they had
loved with equal lightness. And at last, when Sheila
was nearly six and she herself pregnant for the fourth
time by a man who'd alternately kissed her and beaten
her according to his mood and her willingness to fall
in with it, he'd left her, and the boss of the Oldest
Country Fair in the World had found her and let her
stay, giving her shelter at least and a meal now and
then until Death had, mercifully, let her go.
Piecing it all together wasn't easy. It took John
Ambrose nearly six months delving and digging and
probing before he got at the whole miserable story, but
the early bits taught him at least to speak quietly and
gently and not to lose his temper when, even fed and
clothed and warm, sleeping for the first time she could
remember between sheets and in a bed of her own,
Sheila was afraid of him. When she watched his hands
out of furtive, wary eyes and dodged, sharply, piti-
fully, if he moved quickly and unexpectedly. If she
fretted over her mother she never showed it. She had A
been brought up in a school where tears only meant
blows and curses and still more blows; where silence
and unobtrusiveness were the only recognized virtues
in any child, and six years' experience had taught her
the wisdom of keeping on the right side of grown-ups
and especially of men, whose hands were harder than
26
those of women and who knew, so terribly well, how
to use a strap so that, with the least number of blows,
it could have the greatest effect.
But if she never spoke to him of her own free will,
if her answer to practically any question consisted for
those first months of nothing more illuminating than
"yes" and "no" accompanied by a nod or a shake of
her flaming curls, the women who went out of their
way to make much of her for John Ambrose's sake
fared no better. She stood still while they talked to her
because Mrs. Warren told her it was naughty not to
listen when ladies talked, and because naughtiness spelt
trouble that might as well be avoided. She said "yes"
and "no" for the same reason, but to no one, not even
to John Ambrose, did she for one moment, in those
first months, relax her wariness or vouchsafe one
single glimpse into the workings of the baby mind that
dwelt and moved and grew behind the big gray eyes.
Mrs. Warren, produced through the machinations
of Mrs. Allister, looked after her with the assistance
of a girl procured via the same medium. Between them
they fed her, clothed her, bathed her, taught her not
to eat with her fingers, that a pocket handkerchief for
certain purposes was infinitely preferable to the sleeve
of her frock, that cleanliness is equal to, if not more
essential than, godliness, that somewhere not very
clearly defined there was a mysterious person known
as God, and that John Ambrose was His closest friend
and nearest neighbor. And there, because, without any
coöperation on her part, there was little else they
could do, they left her.
John Ambrose's part in her training lay in paying
Mary's wages, in providing Sheila herself with food
and clothes, with toys and dolls and books, with a bed
to sleep in, and with mental and moral and physical
27
safety. Once or twice a week he took her with him in
the car when he went on his rounds, bought her an ice
or a bar of chocolate to keep her company while he
paid his calls, tried to discover, without any success
at all, what was going on in her mind, and was on the
verge of giving up the quest as completely hopeless,
when one morning, from the seat beside him in the
car, she volunteered, for the first time in his knowl-
edge of her, a definite statement.
They had driven over to Colonel Egerton's place at
Coleshall. One of the maids had been stupid enough,
Mrs. Egerton told John Ambrose on the telephone, to
fall downstairs and hurt her ankle, and she thought
he'd better come over and see it as the girl seemed in
pain. So he'd put Sheila in the car, driven over and set
a white-faced little tweeny's broken ankle, and then
had turned the car and was driving back again through
the Park when the Colonel's groom and his seven-
year-old grandson and heir rode in through the gate
on horseback and passed them.
"Oh!" Sheila said, "there's a little boy on a pony."
Her accent was hideous-deplorable, but that, John
Ambrose told himself jubilantly, was nothing. If only
she'd speak, break through that ghastly wall of staring,
terrified silence, accents could easily be eradicated.
There were governesses who existed in the world for
the sheer purpose of teaching children how to speak
properly, schools by the dozen in every corner of the
country. The broad drawl of her native Midlands that
spoiled the baby voice was nothing. Nothing at all if
only she'd break through her fear enough to talk.
"I'd like to ride a pony like that little boy, Mister."
28
CHAPTER IV
IT took another three months to disabuse her mind
of the necessity for addressing John Ambrose as
"Mister," and all that time before Miss Aldridge,
installed for three hours daily as governess, could get
out of her more than that baffling "yes" and "no," but
after that things really began to improve.
There was, of course, no gainsaying the child's at-
traction. It stood out in flaming capitals all over her,
so that elderly ladies of narrow views, remembering
her origin, predicted, gloomily, her probable end, and
other little girls were frankly envious. She was in-
telligent, too, though she had no use whatever either
for Miss Aldridge or for the lessons Miss Aldridge
laboriously taught her; and when John Ambrose,
plunging recklessly, bought her a pony, she rode it
bare-backed, unconscious of even the meaning of fear,
before she had had it a week. She was thrown, of
course. Not once, but over and over again, and she
got up laughing but with a little set line to her mouth
that boded ill for the pony's tricks. He taught her
to swim, too, like an eel, and by the time she was nine
there was nothing left in her voice or in her speech
that resembled even remotely the twanging drawl of
her babyhood or the life she'd led prior to John Am-
brose's adoption of her. And for John Ambrose him-
self she had, instigated in the first place by Mrs.
Allister, Mrs. Warren, and Mary, and fostered by his
own treatment of her and his own real affection for
29
her, a blind slavish worship that sometimes really
troubled him. It made it, for one thing, doubly difficult
for him to correct her in anything, to find even the
smallest, simplest fault, because reproof of any kind
from him broke her heart. Miss Aldridge could jump
on her—and for that matter repeatedly did—a dozen
times in a week, Mrs. Warren could scold, Mary
grumble. All three of them left Sheila perfectly cold,
but a word of reproof from John Ambrose sent her
into a flood of heart-broken, terrified tears that upset
them both for days afterwards. He had to go warily
with her, too, because he could never quite get out of
his mind something that had happened when Sheila
had been at the Corner House a little less than two
years. She'd been naughty-definitely naughty-for a
week. "Feelin' her feet," Mrs. Warren described it,
and was probably perfectly right in her diagnosis,
because, for the first time since her arrival in the
house, Sheila was certainly making the most of her
position as its most pampered, treasured inmate. And
at the end of the week, against his will but knowing
he'd got to take a hand in quelling the rebellion, John
Ambrose had sent for her into the study and had
spoken to her sharply. He'd said, because he was tired
to death and hungry and cross, and because Mrs.
Warren had been laying down the law of his duty
rather more than he could bear:
"Things like this have got to stop, Sheila. You un-
derstand? If you can't behave yourself decently and
do what you're told, I shall have to send you away-to
school, anywhere. I can't possibly keep you here at
home if you're going to behave like this. Now, go
upstairs and go to bed, and you're to stay there for
the rest of the day. Understand?"
Sheila had understood perfectly, but, while the
30
whole diatribe had been disconcerting and upsetting
because it was the first time Uncle John had ever taken
sides against her, one sentence stood out in letters of
fire and terrified her. Probably if John Ambrose hadn't
been tired and cross he wouldn't have said it. Cer-
tainly, had he had the slightest idea as to how much
of her tragic babyhood still held so vivid a place in
her memory, he'd have cut out his tongue before he'd
even mentioned or hinted at sending her away. But
he had said it, and for Sheila the mischief was done.
Going away meant leaving everything that made life
so wonderful. Going back to the horrors of circuses,
of shouting men who got drunk and beat her, of rags
and horrible food, of bits and scraps picked up any-
how, eaten anyhow, of a heap of bags tossed into a
corner for a bed. She'd gone upstairs to her room re-
membering it all, which fact in itself would have hor-
rified John Ambrose, who flattered himself she'd
forgotten it long ago, and under the sheets and eider-
down of her own dainty bed had reasoned it all out,
shivering and sick with fear, in a baby mind taught, by
experience, to reason as logically as that of any adult
person.
You couldn't, she argued with herself, always be
good. Not unquestionably, unquestioningly good.
Things happened that made you disobedient and
naughty and rebellious, and you couldn't stop them
happening. Bright sunshine, for example, that got
inside you somehow and put ideas into your head. Or
the knowledge that you had found the weak spot in
Mrs. Warren's armor, which was her possession of
false teeth, and could tease her about them or, if one
were lucky, could hide them. The joy of swimming
without any clothes on that so shocked Miss Aldridge,
and your affection for all the things that were wrong
31
and tiresome and naughty. It was all very fine to be
sorry and to say you wouldn't do them any more. But
if you were honest you knew it wasn't true. You knew
perfectly well that, given time and opportunity and
the desire that couldn't be killed, you would do them
all again some time or other, and Uncle John had
said that if you did, you'd be sent away. He'd said:
"To school, or somewhere," but it all meant the same.
It meant going away, and while school wasn't a circus,
while it wasn't a place of which one knew anything at
all even by name or reputation, it was probably an-
other place like a circus; where you would live hor-
ribly and hideously, and have to give up all the things
that had suddenly made life and the world so beautiful.
She'd lain awake, reasoning it all out, for nearly
four hours, and then she'd made her decision. One
couldn't always be good. That stood to reason; and so,
if being sent away was to be the result of naughtiness,
it would be better-far, far better-not to run the
risk of it at all, because anything-anything in the
world, even being dead and out of the world—would
be better than being sent back to that place from which
Uncle John had rescued her. Or to another place
like it.
It would all, she told herself, be quite easy. Only a
week before she'd seen Mr. Giles over at the Mill
drown four little kittens in a pail of water, and, when
she'd cried and begged him not to, he'd assured her
they didn't feel anything and weren't being hurt.
""
'Tisn't nothing that hurts 'em, missie. You take
my word for it. In they goes, knowin' nothin' about
it, you see, and then-it's all over, and the poor little
things is safe in a better world and well out of a packet
o' trouble into the bargain. You can take my word for
it, missie, drownin' kittens don't hurt 'em."
32
The chances were, therefore, that if a kitten didn't
mind being drowned a little girl needn't mind it either,
and in any case-in any case, even if it did hurt a bit-
it would be better to be as Mr. Giles had said, “safe in
a better world," than to run the risk of being sent back
to that other world of tents and smells and curses and
blows, from which even the presence of her mother
hadn't been able to protect her. And now-without
even her mother there-
It was John Ambrose, coming upstairs to bed far
earlier than usual, who found her. She'd filled the
hand-basin in the bathroom to the limit of its capacity,
and when he went in for his bath she was standing on
a stool with her head half buried in the water. He said:
"What on earth!"—and then with pardonable ir-
ritation: "What the hell d'you think you're doing?
I sent you to bed and I told you to stay there. If this
is how you're going to obey the orders I give you, my
child, there'll be nothing for it but to send you away.'
He yanked her head with its sopping curls out of the
water and twisted her round so that he could see her
face. "Now will you be good enough to tell me what
the devil you think you're doing?"
And, half-sick with crying, she told him:
99
"I wanted to drown myself. You said you was going
to send me away, and I was afraid. And Mr. Giles
said it didn't hurt. I thought even being drownded
would be better than going back-there"-she jerked
her head in the direction of the village and the fair-
ground-"an' so-an' so-I tried an' tried, an' I
couldn't do it. I couldn't go in deep enough, 'cos when
I went right in I couldn't breathe, an' I had to come
up again. But don't send me away, Uncle John. I will
try to be good, but if I'm bad again don't send me
33
away. Please, please, Uncle John, don't ever, ever
send me away!"
Aghast, John Ambrose had picked her up, wrapped
her in a blanket and yelled for Mrs. Warren. To-
gether they'd dried her hair, warmed her half-frozen
little body, poured hot milk down her throat, cuddled
her, fussed over her; but for hours after she'd gone to
sleep, utterly worn out and exhausted, John Ambrose
had sat beside her bed, holding her hand, cursing him-
self for his unintentional cruelty; and he'd been ter-
ribly careful ever since in what he said to her and how
he said it.
But, on the whole, things went very happily and
smoothly. There were episodes, certainly, over which
it were better to draw a discreet veil; there were things
Sheila said and did even now that were not done by,
for example, Mrs. Egerton's little grandchildren, but,
on the whole, the experiment was proving a success.
Little by little, but definitely resentful, the child was
learning at least the rudiments of reading and writing
and arithmetic. Slowly and laboriously she was be-
ginning to realize that there was a clear and terribly
rigid line of demarcation between the things a child of
the fair-grounds might say and do under stress of
emotion and the things expected from the adopted
daughter of Dr. John Ambrose. The memory of the
circus was there still-the scar it had left on her soul
was far too deep ever to be completely healed and for-
gotten-and her slavish admiration for John Ambrose,
her anxiety to please him in every little thing she did,
her terror lest anything might disturb him or upset
him, was there too. But looking back, John Ambrose
knew he had never really regretted the thing he had
done. He was getting on, of course, now. People had
learnt, from experience, that youth in the medical pro-
34
fession need not, of necessity, spell ignorance or in-
competence, and panel patients were a secondary line
these days. He had, for one thing, pulled the Olivers'
only child through a really serious attack of pneumonia,
and the Olivers, who had both money and influence,
talked of him, ceaselessly, for months afterwards, and
in the scarlet-fever epidemic, which was a bad one, he
had worked like a navvy and had been publicly thanked
by the entire village at a social given especially for the
purpose of presenting him with a gold hunter watch.
In the neighborhood other people were beginning to
talk of him, too. He was called in, not once but several
times, by doctors with established reputations as a con-
sultant on children's diseases; his account at the bank,
from being constantly overdrawn, began to show a
steady and increasing balance, and it was nice-more
than just nice-to come home tired at night and find
Sheila waiting for him.
By the time she was ten he was a little surprised
to discover how essential she had become both to his
comfort and his happiness. Mrs. Warren was excellent
-he'd been lucky in his housekeepers-but she was,
after all, only a housekeeper who managed his house
and called him "Sir" when she spoke to him, and,
having seen to his material wants, retired, discreetly,
to her own quarters at the back of the house, whereas
Sheila belonged to him. Coming back for tea he knew
he'd find her there, fresh and clean and beautiful, on
the doorstep waiting for him, ready with cautious,
wary fingers to pour out his tea from the pot that was
really too heavy for her, to bring over the toast from
its place on the hearth where Mrs. Warren put it to
keep warm, to stand beside his chair watchful for even
his slightest need. It was nice to have her drive with
him sometimes, to listen while she talked, to watch
35
the light that flashed into her eyes when anything ex-
cited or interested her; to know that, whoever else
might fail him, Sheila at least was his and his alone;
to remember that no one-not even her mother now-
could ever have the right to step in and take her away
from him; to be able to look back and to know beyond
all shadow of doubt that whatever the future might
hold, at least the past four years had justified him in
the crazy thing he had done by taking her in the
first place.
And then, when Sheila had been at the Corner House
for just over four years, when Miss Aldridge's simple
teaching had given place to that of a really qualified
governess, when both he and Sheila knew that school
must be the next step, John Ambrose had a stroke of
luck. The Olivers had a house party that included,
amongst a crowd of other people, Dr. Lionel Tracy,
who was a famous children's specialist, his wife and his
daughter of seven, and one night Mrs. Oliver called
him worriedly on the telephone. The Tracy child was
ill. She had been seedy for two days-very seedy-and

and she couldn't breathe properly. And then, sud-

"Nan, tell him to come now. Now! Nurse thinks it's
diphtheria, and Sybil's choking!"
When Dr. Tracy got back just after ten it was all

sleeping-draught in her own room; Mrs. Oliver was

else in the house had been inoculated against infection,

Two days later Lionel Tracy came over to the
Corner House. He said, without beating about the

"I've come to make a suggestion, Ambrose. And,
while I owe you an enormous debt of gratitude, it isn't
simply gratitude that lies at the back of the proposi-

you. I want a man who's up and coming, and who, in
an emergency, knows what to do and isn't afraid to do
it. I've a big practice and a rich one. If it isn't an
impertinent question, what are you making here?"
"Roughly, a thousand a year," John Ambrose said.
His voice was quiet and devoid of all emotion, but his
eyes shone and his hands opened and shut twice very
quickly before he stopped them doing it a third time
by gripping the arms of his chair. “Quite that, I think,
but not much more. I don't suppose a practice of this
sort, in a small country village, is ever worth much

"I'll guarantee you that and another five hundred,"
Lionel Tracy said. "In addition, you'd have the right
to any patients you bring in off your own bat, so to

hospital work. Against it your expenses would be
higher, but if you'd care for the job-
" He stopped,
dropping his formal tone. "I hope like hell you'll
come in with me, Ambrose."

CHAPTER V
"WHEN I come back," Sheila wrote from a school in

John Ambrose was counting the hours, too. It was
astonishing how dull the house had been without Sheila.
Even now, when she'd been away nearly five years, first

"The house isn't the same without her," she'd said,

John Ambrose had been thinking of that side of the
38
question for some time. Things, of course, would be

That had been eight years ago, and they'd been eight
wonderful years. John Ambrose had started, of course,

People had said of him: "If Tracy has chosen him as

The flat that had been his first London home had given

40
of a house in Upper Wimpole Street. He had, in ad-

Amongst women he was intriguing and interesting,

CHAPTER VII
THE knowledge that he was no longer looking at

And, moreover, not as a pleasant shock. The whole

I hope you're not in the habit of letting young men like
this Armstrong chap kiss you," he'd said, and Sheila

"But that's nothing," she said. It was obvious, too,
by her voice that she spoke no more than the bare

it. It doesn't mean a thing. It's just part of the game
and it honestly doesn't mean anything." Then with
one of those swift flashes of insight that made their
relationship so dear to him: "You didn't like Derek,
did you, Uncle John?"
John Ambrose had answered her question with an-
other.
"Do you?" he'd said, and Sheila had hesitated a
second before she'd given her decision.
"Not terribly," she said, then: "He does things well
and he dances marvelously, and his car's a dream.
But that's all, I think. I mean there's nothing serious, if
that's what you're afraid of, Uncle John."
"You'd be upset, though, if I asked you to forgo

1
54
your evening with him to-night," John Ambrose said,

"Yes," she said. "At least-upset isn't, I think, the

sure to give me a marvelous evening-

"And to make love to you in the car coming home,"

Sheila said slowly:

John Ambrose had hedged a bit then.

"But I don't," she said. "Oh, Uncle John, don't

She'd gone, then and there, to the telephone, and

"I'm terribly sorry, Derek.... Yes, of course, but

I can't come to-night.... No, and not to-morrow,

but- Oh, 'night, Derek."
It was all, John Ambrose told himself, entirely
satisfactory then. Sheila's implicit obedience, her un-
failing readiness to fall in with even his slightest re-
quest, was admirable in an adopted daughter, but in the
girl one wanted to marry-and wanted it far more
than one had ever wanted anything in one's life before
-it might not be so good. One had a horrible suspicion
that, asked to marry one, Sheila would agree for the
simple reason that her refusal to comply with his re-
quest might hurt, badly, a man she couldn't bear to
hurt at all, and to whom she owed a debt of gratitude,
for the repayment of which she had no available
means other than gratitude; and the thought of being
married out of gratitude wasn't pleasant. John Am-
brose wanted her badly. It shocked him profoundly,
almost to the verge of actual distaste, to realize how
terribly badly he did want her, but he didn't, even then,
want a wife who preferred to marry him rather than
to hurt him by telling the truth and admitting that she
didn't love him.
The whole thing, he told himself angrily, was dis-
turbing but definitely illuminating. It explained, for
one thing, the annoyance and irritation he'd experiencd
when the Armstrong boy had kissed Sheila in the hall,
his fear of even the young man of the boat, his resent-
ment, stifled because it had seemed pure selfishness, at
Sheila's continuous absences. It explained-he faced
the truth with bitter self-condemnation-a whole lot
of things in himself over which, lately, he'd been wor-
rying more than a little, but it was none the less in-
tensely disturbing because it left only three paths in
57
front of him, any one of which would bring matters
to a definite crisis and none of which suited him. He
could, as he knew perfectly well, fling discretion and
common sense and all the things by which his carefully
regulated life had hitherto been governed, to the four
inconsiderate winds of heaven, and ask her to marry
him. In which event she would probably agree without
question and be profoundly unhappy for the balance
of her life. Thereby wrecking her own future and,
incidentally, his too, since her happiness must ultimately
reflect upon his as well. He could also ask her to marry
him and be refused, in which case, for the rest of their
lives together, there would inevitably be stalking be-
tween them the disturbing ghost of his own folly, so
that their present delightful relationship must inevi-
tably be completely and utterly ruined for all time. Or
he could take the third and last path by which he
could suffocate his love and his desire in the blankets
of self-respect and pride, and what, taking everything
into consideration, he really felt to be scarcely less
than sheer decency itself, and say nothing. By which
means things would be left to go on exactly as they
had gone on ever since Sheila came home from school;
he would stand by and see Sheila being loved by
other men and, almost certainly, loving them in return;
he would even-in all probability-be asked before
very long to stand at the altar steps of All Souls',
Langham Place, and to give her away in marriage to
some man who would make her an essentially satis-
factory husband in every detail save for the fact that,
as long as he himself lived, no other man save him-
self had, in his eyes, any right to be her husband at
all; and he would certainly be expected, ultimately, to
act the part of affectionate grandfather to her chil-
dren. Which was the rôle, he told himself with steadily
58
increasing bitterness of soul, for which he was ob-
viously best suited, both as regards age and the rela-
tionship which had, for twelve years, existed between
himself and Sheila, but in which, try as he would, he
could not see himself without a sense of revolting re-
pulsion that startled him afresh by the depth of dis-
like and distaste it evoked in his soul.
For a month, following the unpleasantly illuminating
mental searchlight that had brought into a prominence,
where it couldn't possibly be overlooked, the fact of his
love for her, he stood at the cross-roads his own
dilemma had created, and looked at the three paths
that stretched their uncertain ways ahead of him,
struggling to make up his mind as to which would prove
the least disillusioning and unpleasant, and afraid to
take so much as a step over the borderline of any one
of the three. He was so irritable and edgy that for
the first time in twelve years Mrs. Warren seriously
contemplated giving him notice and retiring from serv-
ice to live with her married daughter; his secretary,
who, despite her competence and her spectacles, cher-
ished for him a secret but passionate attachment, was
discovered on two separate occasions in a state of
sheer collapse and in floods of tears; Roberts, his
chauffeur, only tolerated the things that were said to
him because he knew he'd go a long way before he
found a better job; the butler left at a moment's
notice, muttering threats; and of the household the
only members who lived in any sort of comfort at
all were the tweeny, who took care never to let him
see her, Mrs. Owen who, as Sheila's chaperon, knew
when she had a soft job and wouldn't let things rattle
her, and Sheila herself, who was a little puzzled by
the sudden and inexplicable change in the mental make-
up of a man she had always regarded as something as
59
solid and unimpressionable as the Rock of Gibraltar,
but remembering he would be forty-eight next birthday,
and therefore elderly and entitled to moods, put it
down to liver and increasing age, and left it at that.
Then at last, when even Lionel Tracy was begin-
ning rather unhappily to wonder what had so definitely
"got" his usually even-tempered partner, the die was
cast for him instead of by him, and the game decided
without any volition on his part at all. Because, coming
out of hospital one foggy December afternoon and
deciding to walk home rather than drive, he crossed
Westminster Bridge without looking too carefully
where he was going and walked directly in front of a
ten-ton furniture van that, try as he would, the driver
hadn't a chance in a thousand to pull up in time. And
while he lay as a patient in the hospital of which he
was such a shining light, with half his bones splintered
and broken and his head swathed in bandages, Sheila
sat, white and terrified, beside his bed and heard him,
raving in delirium, tell the story he'd decided only that
morning never to let her know.

CHAPTER VIII
LATER that night, when a kindly house-physician had
insisted on her going home for a proper night's sleep,
Sheila consoled herself with the thought that, in de-
lirium, people often said things they didn't mean. She
remembered a girl at school who'd come back after the
holidays with a terribly gruesome story of her brother
who'd had pneumonia and, delirious for days, had said
incredible things that bore, as the whole of his family
knew, no resemblance whatever to his usual thoughts
about anything. Sybil, who had a flair for the dramatic,
had told the story with astonishing vividness and it had
all been a little shocking.
"He wouldn't have my mother in the room," she'd
said. "And it was dreadful because he simply adores
her, but the doctor said people who were delirious
often said just the opposite of what they really mean.
Sy my mother simply hadn't got to mind, you see, but it
is difficult, isn't it? Because, just suppose the doctors
don't know, and what people say when they're uncon-
scious is the truth they're too polite to say when they're
sane? I think that's an awful thought, you know, girls,
because it simply might lead you into any sort of a
mess.
"9
But the doctors were right, of course. Going home

61

with discretion, be said, not a touch wanting when

"" and she shook her head.

Upstairs in her own room, Sheila felt happier about
the scene at the hospital that afternoon. Doctors knew,
of course, and people in delirium made statements that
didn't mean a thing in real life, at which they'd be
shocked and horrified afterwards when they came to
66
hear of them. Even Uncle John himself had once said
that nurses and doctors were put on their honor never

and that there were even cases when the patient himself
mustn't be told. So that it stood to reason he couldn't
really have meant what he'd said, although she wished

But if what Uncle John had said were true, if,

imploring cry of a dying woman, had impelled him to
67
take a waif of the fair-ground home with him and

her, he would perhaps be terribly unhappy for all the
rest of his life.
The sheets were warm and cozy when she slipped
into them. Mrs. Warren had been upstairs herself to
see that her hot-water bottle was really hot, and to
bring her a glass of hot milk, and the fire was lovely.
It sang little tunes to itself on the hearth, and after

toast, but she was shivering so that her teeth chattered

try as she would, she couldn't stop shivering. Because,
lying there, alone and face to face with facts, she knew
that if what John Ambrose had said were true, if he
really wanted her as a wife instead of as a daughter,
the least she could do, after all his love and care of her
and all he'd done for her, was marry him and give him
what he wanted.

CHAPTER IX
WHEN, next morning, she went back to the hospital,
John Ambrose was still unconscious, but he wasn't
talking any more. Just lying there with his head so
swathed in bandages that only his eyes and mouth and
nose were visible, and absolutely still.
Dr. Symonds said:
"We're quite satisfied, Miss Kavanagh, I assure you.
Of course, with a crack in his skull such as he's got, it's
only natural" His voice trailed off into long-
winded explanations that, to Sheila, meant nothing at
all, ending with a kindly expression of hope that "they"
were making her comfortable. "If there's anything
you'd like, please don't hesitate to say so.
"Every one's terribly kind," Sheila said gratefully.
She was feeling much happier this morning. She told
herself: "It's surprising what a good night's sleep does
for one, because really, last night, I felt awful. About
everything. And now it's all completely different. Of
course, he was just delirious yesterday afternoon. He'd
be shocked and horrified beyond description if he ever
knew the things he'd said to me yesterday." And in
addition, Jimmy Bellamy had rung her up before eight
o'clock. He'd said:
"What time can I see you, Angel?" and when she'd
laughed at his urgency he'd been injured and aggrieved.
"I don't know," he'd said, "but it seems to me very
much as though I were making a colossal fool of my-
self, because this method of procedure isn't my style
69
at all. I've always gone along the lines that if you stand
still and let other people do the running, you're a great
deal less likely to get involved in a head-on collision
that'll hurt you. So that it's disturbing to my sense of
the fitness of things to find I'm doing the running my-
self. I'll be round about breakfast time."
"I'm going to the hospital," Sheila had said. It was
lovely to have Jimmy talking like that, because Jimmy
was well, it was difficult to describe Jimmy altogether,
because he wasn't good-looking or very rich or any-
thing really noticeable, and yet there was something
about him that was terribly attractive. It would be so
easy, Sheila told herself, sitting beside John Ambrose's
straight white bed, and remembering him so vividly
that she couldn't forget a single detail of anything
about him, so desperately easy to fall in love with
Jimmy. And so dangerous, because any girl loving
Jimmy was much more liable to be hurt than happy
in the process. There had been cases of other girls.
Sue Grant and Marjorie Overton and Phyllis Bond,
and lots of others. Mrs. Grant had actually had to
take Sue on a world-cruise before she could begin to
fall out of love with Jimmy; and Phyllis Bond had
taken an overdose of something and nearly killed her-
self. So that falling in love with anything as utterly
unstable and unreliable and fluctuating as Jimmy
wasn't a wise thing to do. And yet it would be won-
derful to be loved by him-very wonderful, but very
unwise. Even his mother saw the unwisdom of loving
Jimmy too much. She'd told Sheila once, just as she'd
told other girls:
"Don't take Jimmy seriously, my dear. He's like
his father, terribly attractive, but quite unreliable. I
was all right because, thank God, I've got a sense of
humor and I didn't marry my husband until all my
70
illusions had been already smashed for me. But you're
too young to have your life spoiled because a man
doesn't know how to spell the word 'fidelity' or even
the meaning of it when he has it spelt for him. Go out
and dance with Jimmy-he's like his father in that too;
there wasn't a man in the length and breadth of all
Ireland who could dance the heart out of your body
as my husband could; let him take you about and give
you a good time if you like, but don't ever take him
seriously. Not, that is, if you want to be a happy
woman, my dear."
But even remembering all that Mrs. Bellamy had
said, even bearing in mind all that one knew and had
learnt from other sources, one couldn't help remem-
bering, too, how wonderful it would be to be loved by
Jimmy. To be Jimmy's wife and perhaps, with great
good luck, the mother of Jimmy's children. But she'd
told him not to come round after breakfast just the
same: that she was going to the hospital and that she'd
see him later, perhaps for a cocktail around six or so,
and Jimmy had laughed.
"Oh yes?" he'd said derisively. "That's sweet of
you, darling. I'll be there all right," and half an hour

It wasn't so bad to-day though it was bad enough,

At five o'clock when Jimmy came, John Ambrose

"9
"He's got a grand constitution," he said, "and I

CHAPTER X
DOCTOR SYMONDS, meeting her at the door of a

CHAPTER XI
THEY were married at the end of January. As soon
as John Ambrose could trust himself to walk up the
aisle without the aid of sticks and in time for them to
combine a prolonged honeymoon with the sea voyage
he'd been ordered to take preparatory to starting
work again. It had begun as a small very quiet affair,
and then somehow it had grown and swelled until, by
the time it actually took place, it had developed into
the sort of crush that, in these days, seems the correct
and approved method of launching the matrimonial
ship. Sheila had wanted it all to be quiet. She'd said:
"Just us and no one else. After all, it's our affair.
Why should we have a crowd to see us married, Uncle
John?" But Mrs. Owen, who loved a wedding, and
especially a wedding in which her word would carry a
good deal of weight, had "got at" John Ambrose
privately and talked him into seeing things from her
own point of view, and in the end Sheila had agreed.
"I expect you're both right," she said. "And if you
don't mind, Uncle John-
99
He stopped her there, smiling at her.

81
cold and a little afraid. "Is it going to be terribly hard

Sheila hated herself these days. Despising herself

for Jimmy-

added a little wistfully: "Are you looking forward
