"I'm crazy about it," Sheila said, and almost jumped
at the sound of her own words, because for once the
89
modern slangy phrase was so nearly the truth. Lying
awake in bed at nights, when with darkness and silence
and solitude came, too, the thoughts and memories and
fears for which there was no time during the day, she'd
wondered sometimes if she really were crazy. If any
girl, sane, sensible, capable of any kind of logical, co-
herent reasoning, could ever contemplate doing this
thing she planned to do. Because girls with any sense
at all didn't do things of this kind. They were grateful
for kindnesses shown to them, of course: very grateful
often, but they didn't let gratitude stand in the way
of happiness or of the things they wanted. Not even
in the way of the men they wanted. There had been
that case of Stella Warner. She owed everything she
had, or was, to her elder sister. It was public property
that her people had been miserably, desperately poor,
and when Janie married Tim Buckley, whose stenog-
rapher she'd been and who was heir to his father's
American millions, she'd made her home with Janie
and Tim and they'd treated her simply marvelously.
Janię had grudged her nothing, making of her an
honored, treasured guest, never letting her even re-
member that she owed her lovely clothes and beautiful
home and even her pocket-money to Janie, and every
one knew what had been the result of that episode.
That one fine morning Janie had gone into Tim's bed-
room with a message about something and Tim hadn't
been there; that his bed hadn't been slept in, and that
a note, a pitiful little epistle that did its best, certainly,
to hide the truth and to be loyal to the girl who'd
trapped him, said that he'd gone away with Stella.
Who loved him and who couldn't live without him.
Gratitude hadn't meant much to Stella, Sheila told
herself bitterly. It didn't really mean much to any one
of them if one stopped to analyze it properly, because
90
Stella wasn't the only one. There were lots of others
who said they were grateful for big things or for little
things, but who never, for one moment, let gratitude in-
terfere with the things they wanted to do, so that it was
crazy really to let this debt she owed to John spoil every-
thing. Come in between herself and everything that
made life worth living. And John would understand.
He'd be hurt and terribly upset, but he'd know; he'd
understand instantly that while one could never forget
all he had done, never fail to appreciate the goodness
and kindness and generosity he'd shown one all these
years, there was Jimmy and love which bore no re-
lationship to gratitude. And youth which, more than
anything else in the world, needed youth as its rightful
complement. It was all very fine, too, to pretend to
oneself that in any case one wouldn't have married
Jimmy. All very fine, as John's promised wife, to ex-
plain to Jimmy all the excellent reasons why one
shouldn't marry him; why he couldn't ever make one
a good and satisfactory husband, because if John
hadn't existed and the debt of gratitude one owed to
John hadn't existed either, one would have jumped at
the chance of marrying Jimmy. He might, as a hus-
band, have been all one had pointed out that he would
be. Unstable, unreliable, probably, before the end, un-
faithful too, but there was an end to everything. There
was always an end to everything, and before the end,
in between the beginning and the inevitable end, there
would be something so wonderful, so breath-taking, so
beautiful that even the end would be justified. One
would be willing to risk almost anything, loneliness,
dishonor, disillusion, anything, just for the joy of
being, if only for a little while, Jimmy's adored and
adoring wife, and having the chance to look back and
remember things that, even when one had never had
91
them, made one go hot and cold and shivery even to
think of them.
But Jimmy wasn't there any longer. He'd gone
away—abroad, without leaving an address even. With-
out a word. And John was left. Who for twelve years
had been Uncle John and the only father one had ever
known. To whom one had gone for consolation in
trouble, for punishment in sin, for advice in bewilder-
ment. Who had dosed one with syrup of figs or with
castor-oil; who had dictated one's diet, paid for one's
clothes and one's school bills, ordered one's entire life.
Who'd been one's guide, philosopher, friend, but who
one had never-never even in one's wildest, most in-
considered moments-pictured as one's lover. Who
would be very soon unless one became suddenly sane
and sensible and practical, unafraid of the truth-
one's husband. Sharing one's life so intimately that one
could never be quite free and independent any more;
with the right to come in and out of one's bedroom
without even knocking, and with other rights too. The
right to sleep beside one whether one liked it or not,
the right to one's body, the right, it seemed, almost to
one's very soul.
Lying awake in the darkness, Sheila could see it all,
reason it out quite clearly. So clearly that in the morn-
ing she'd get up with her mind full of excellent and
very sensible resolutions. She'd go to the hospital and
tell John the truth. She'd put it very gently and con-
siderately and thoughtfully, sparing him all unneces-
sary pain, but just telling him the plain truth. Which
was that she loved him dearly, dearly-as an uncle, as
a father, as even a big, splendid brother, but not as a
husband, and that there was Jimmy, and that youth
and love screamed at her for attention and consider-
ation, and that she couldn't, could she, remembering all
92
these things, actually marry him. That always she'd
be ready to obey his slightest wish, to carry out his
smallest order but that she couldn't-'please, Uncle
John, darling, please-I can't marry you. It isn't only
just Jimmy, it's everything. I'm eighteen and I want
love. The love that eighteen has for eighteen, that's
wild and reckless and passionate and hot-headed. That
hasn't anything in it of reason or common sense, but
is just a love that can't be killed. That's got to have
its way. Whatever happens. Oh, Uncle John, darling
Uncle John, I owe you everything I've ever had in the
world, everything I am, everything I ever will be, but
don't-please, please, don't want me to marry you.'
She'd go down to the hospital with all the arguments
arranged all tidily and neatly right in the foreground
of her mind. And then she'd go into John's room-
John, not Uncle John—and see his adoring eyes fixed
on the door waiting for her coming, hear his eager
voice: "I thought you were never coming, my darling,"
feel her hands taken in his and held there, his lips
on hers, the thud-thud-thud of his heart as she stooped
over his bed and all the good resolutions died. Face to
face with John, seeing his eyes and hearing his voice
and remembering, always remembering, what one was
and what, but for him, one might have been, one
couldn't say the things one had come to say. One simply
couldn't sit there and knock out with one blow a man
who couldn't hit back.
So that there was nothing for it but to go on. To put
out of one's mind once and for all the things that,
henceforward, must have no place there. To plunge
into the buying of clothes, into engagement parties,
into cruising plans and wedding arrangements, so
deeply that one simply hadn't time to think, and if even
the fact that John had to pay the bills for one's
93
trousseau was in itself a separate and individual slap
in the face, there was nothing one could do about it.
One had one of two ways from which to choose, and
if gratitude stood with a flaming sword to bar the en-
trance to one, only the other remained, and one might
just as well be sensible and make the best of it.
And certainly the plans John was making were
rather lovely. Because he wasn't well enough to go to
the shipping office and arrange things there, an ob-
sequious, deferential agent had actually come to the
hospital. He'd brought with him a whole portfolio of
papers, colored pictures of ships; of intimate, luxuri-
ous cabins; of wonderful coastal cities; of white beaches
and waving palms; of snow-clad mountains under blue
skies; of smoldering volcanoes and native dancers.
"I take it you'll be covering the entire cruise, sir?”
he'd said, and then had proceeded to enlarge on the
comforts of Cabin D.4, "with private bath" over those
of Cabin D.6, which although with private bath lacked
apparently so many of those things that made for the
real pleasure of a long cruise, and was, incidentally,
nearly £100 per head cheaper.
Ultimately John Ambrose had booked Cabin D.4
for "myself and Mrs. Ambrose." It had, as far as one
could gather from the pictures available-and they
were legion-everything a cabin could have. Real beds
instead of berths, with sumptuous green eiderdowns
and green silk-bound blankets. "We make a specialty
of our beds, Dr. Ambrose. You need have no anxiety
on that score." Cupboards and wardrobes and chests
of drawers in glowing walnut, a fireplace that was
electrically heated but so cleverly contrived that the
logs looked actually real, walls paneled in palest apple
green and lights everywhere. Concealed bulbs that shed
a light exactly where it was most needed, over the
94
dressing-table, at the head of the bed, over the per-
fectly equipped writing-table and beside the big luxuri-
ous arm-chair that held a reading-desk. And the
bathroom was a sheer joy. Small certainly, but with
everything one could possibly need for comfort right
at one's finger-tips. John said complacently, writing
the check that was to pay for all this luxury:
"I think we ought to put in a fairly comfortable
trip, don't you, darling?" After that he'd gone on to
talk of her trousseau "or whatever is the modern name
for a trousseau," had added that other half-wistful
question, "Are you looking forward to all this as much
as I am, Sheila ?" and Sheila had answered him:
"I'm crazy about it," with what was after all only
perfect truth.
Well, she was crazy according to the laws of most
of her own set. But if she was, at least she was paying
her debts and that meant something. And with money
and youth and John, who was so generous and so
kind, one might, she told herself, still pushing into the
background of her mind those things that, for the
future, must never again be brought out of it, have
quite a wonderful time. And in any case, there was
no sense in whining about what, after all, had been
one's own choice.
So she went with Mrs. Owen and bought clothes.
Linens, and muslins and organdies for sunny tropic
days; filmy crêpe-de-chine underthings fringed and
flounced with frail, cobwebby lace; formal evening
gowns for gala nights on board and parties ashore;
dainty informal frocks for ordinary dinners and danc-

countries; half a dozen bathing suits and wraps and
95
beach-suits; night-dresses in pale and loveliest shades

They filled every available corner, every available space,
and were accompanied by dressmakers and tailors and

one had to be.
And on the fifteenth of January, with the wedding
fixed for the end of the month, John Ambrose came
home. He still walked with two sticks, and his hair
was far grayer than it had been prior to the accident,
but he looked extraordinarily well, taking everything
into consideration, and was obviously delighted to be

"I can't believe it's true. I've sat here so often.
Thinking of you and wanting you. Telling myself not

96
a fool after all-I can't believe it. In the hospital it

It didn't seem possible to Sheila either, but it was.
At noon on the thirtieth of January she went out of the
Wimpole Street flat as Sheila Kavanagh for the last
time. She drove with Lionel Tracy, to give her away,
to All Souls' Church, where she walked over a strip of
red carpet and under a striped awning past a battery of

Ambrose with Dr. Symonds waited for her. She lis-

meant nothing at all, repeated after him, like a well-
brought-up little Sunday-school child and quite levelly
and unemotionally, the words he told her to repeat,

again by bridesmaids, groomsmen, guests, walked

under a great bell of white and gold chrysanthemums,

97
women who were John Ambrose's best and closest
friends. They came up one after another all saying the
same thing. Phrasing it a little differently perhaps, but

"Dear John, I do so hope you'll be happy."
"The best of luck, Dr. Ambrose."
"You know we're wishing you both the best of every-
thing, Mrs. Ambrose."
"Darling, you looked so sweet. I don't wonder Dr.
Ambrose is crazy about you."
And, standing under the big yellow and gold bell,

set smiles, framing polite stereotyped phrases.
"Thank you so much."
"Thank you so much."
"You've all been so kind."
"Thank you so much."
Until at last it was over, with the last suitcase
strapped on the luggage grid of the car, the last hand-
ful of rose-petals and confetti dodged more or less
successfully, the last good-by said, the door of the
car closed behind them both, and Stephens, sounding

Southampton, the ship, Cabin D.4, the open sea, and a
new life full of hopes and fears. Behind them was the
past. A past, Sheila told herself, staring out at black
wet roads, at dripping pedestrians struggling with um-

against the car windows and beat savagely on the roof,
that was finished. Done with and buried. Together
with everything that past had held. Jimmy and love
and youth, all the things that once, so short a time ago,
had meant so much and must never be thought of any

98
wife. Mrs. John Sinclair Ambrose. Who was the wife
of a popular, successful doctor and had nothing to do
with a past that held Jimmy Bellamy, for example, or
young wild, passionate love, and had certainly nothing
to do with fair-grounds. And suddenly, quite suddenly
and unexpectedly, Sheila laughed.
"You've always wanted to make a lady of me,
John," she said, "and now you've done it. It's true,
isn't it, that a wife always takes her husband's social
status, so that whatever else I am, at least I can always
call myself a lady by marriage."

CHAPTER XII
SHEILA would have been less than human had she
failed, even with Jimmy in the background, to enjoy
her honeymoon, because if, as a husband, John Am-
brose was quiet and unromantic and years too old, as a
traveling companion he was delightful. There were
times when, for one short fleeting second, she actually
found herself wondering whether, after all, her wed-
ding hadn't been some bad nerve-shattering dream;
whether it could be possible that Uncle John-dear,
delightful, unselfish Uncle John-had changed into
just "John" who was her husband; whether she wasn't,
after all, Sheila Kavanagh, having a wonderful time
on a world pleasure cruise rather than Mrs. John
Ambrose on her honeymoon. Because all John wanted,
apparently, was for her to enjoy herself. He went out
of his way to see to that all the time.
"Because I'm still too much of a crock to sit up late
is no earthly reason why you should have to turn in
early," he said. "You go ahead and have a good time,
darling. Dancing bores me stiff, anyway, but you like
it and there's no reason on earth why you shouldn't
enjoy all you can get. I'm not such a wreck that I have
to be babied, Sheila."
Or he'd send her ashore, when they put in at the
various ports of call, with the gay pleasure-loving
crowd who were only too glad to have her as one of
them.
"Here's a fiver, Sheila. Go and spend it and have a
100
wild time. If your conscience pricks you, it needn't.
I'm a damned sight happier sitting on deck with a book
than trapesing through a lot of dusty hot streets and
arguing with a set of robbers who want me to pay ten
pounds for a thing I can get at home for fifty bob. You
go ahead and enjoy yourself, child.”
So she went. Every time. Wearing her pretty trous-
seau frocks, her gay trousseau hats, the expensive shoes
and gloves and frail silk stockings that were all part
of her wedding outfit. Laughing and loving every min-
ute of it all and forgetting, away from him with the
young heady crowd who were so ready to help her to
forget, that she wasn't Sheila Kavanagh and a free-
lance any longer. But was instead Mrs. John Ambrose
who was a married woman with a husband and
responsibilities.
But there were times, of course, when she couldn't
forget. When, kind and gentle and lovable as he was,
the remembrance that John Ambrose was her husband
came home to her with a shattering, devastating shock.
She'd been quite firm with herself right from the begin-
ning, utterly honest.
"There are things I'll hate, but he mustn't ever
know, and I've got to be decent about them. It's no
good paying a debt with money you grudge, and while
no one wants particularly to pay debts-ever-at least
they can pay them gracefully. And I know what I'm
doing. I'm not a mid-Victorian fool who doesn't know
the meaning of marriage. He'll want to sleep with me,
to make love to me, and I'll have to let him. And not
just to tolerate him either but to be decent about it.
To meet him half-way and, after a time-one gets used
to everything in time. It isn't possible that-for ever
and ever and ever-one can go on remembering Jimmy
if one really makes up one's mind to forget. And John's
ΙΟΙ
kind. He's terribly kind, so that the least I can do is
to be kind back again."
She'd lived up to her own resolutions too, could look
back and feel quite happy on that score. Which in
itself was disconcerting because one had been so sure,
so utterly certain that one could never, for one moment,
tolerate being made love to by any man who wasn't
Jimmy. Lying awake, on that first night, long after
John Ambrose, in the little bed beside her, was asleep,
she was shocked, almost humiliated, to realize that
whatever might be her reactions later, she'd given him,
in that first hour of their married life, kiss for kiss, and
that, if she couldn't give him love, at least the love he
had for her had waked in her a passion that was, for
the time being, an excellent substitute. A woman who
was older, more experienced, could have explained that
quite easily. Could have told her that only in the rarest
instances can passion fail to beget a passion with which
love may have little or nothing to do. John Ambrose,
of course, could have made it clearer still. He could
have reminded her that she was Mary Kavanagh's
daughter and that passion had been the ruling factor
of Mary Kavanagh's life; could have drawn for her,
had he wished to, a cruelly-vivid picture of the inci-
dents that culminated in her own birth; could have
given her, clearly, certain biological facts of Nature
against which neither convention nor propriety nor
common sense have any armor whatever. But, even had
she wanted to ask, there was no woman to whom she
could go, and certainly she could never ask John Am-
brose. It was all, she thought wearily, lying awake in
the soft, sibilant darkness, with only the muffled thud
of the engines and the slap of the water against the
ship's steep sides to break the silence, a hopeless propo-
sition. If it had been Jimmy-who wasn't worth
I02
one of John's little fingers, who couldn't, where loyalty
and goodness and steady unshakable values were con-
cerned, be mentioned even in the same breath with
John-but if it had been Jimmy. If it were Jimmy who
lay beside her in the other little bed; who an hour
earlier had said to her the things John had said; who
had held her in arms that shook with a passion even
his iron control couldn't master; who had taken her
body and soul for his own-she caught her breath in a
little choked sob. Oh, Jimmy, Jimmy!
But next morning the memory of last night had died
more or less. One remembered-things-but in the
daylight, with the sun streaming through an open
porthole and making patterns that danced on the car-
pet, with the sound of gay young voices all about one,
with a flat, calm sea, and a band that played, with a
holiday spirit dominating everything else, life wasn't
so bad. After all, at eighteen-and John was terribly
kind. It was astonishing how wonderful life could be
-even without Jimmy-when one had so many things
with which to make it good.
And so she had a marvelous time. Money, frocks,
parties, everything she could possibly want. With John
asking for nothing save her companionship when she'd
nothing else to do but to give it to him and the few
hours of what was left of the night. And being nice to
John in return, after the first plunge, was very easy.
He was such a dear, so unselfish, so generous, so kind.
He said again and again, amazed still at his own good
fortune, a little incoherent even now because to have
her soft perfumed body in his arms was still to him one
of the seven wonders of the world: "You're so sweet,
my darling, so adorable." And she had nothing to do
but to lie still and let him adore her. To let his eyes
drink in the soft, sweet daintiness of her, his hands
103
follow the curves of her throat, her lovely young

when that other thing caught them up, sweeping

of them to battle against, to give him back kiss for
kiss, passion for passion, and to slip, spent, exhausted,
incapable of regret or even of thought, into the sleep
that was simply the forerunner of another marvelous
day.
There were incidents, of course, that upset every-
thing badly. That jerked one back with a jolt to the
recollection of things as they were and not as they
might have been. John was kindness itself, generous,
unselfish to a degree, but he didn't stand for any non-

on board. He was staying at Malaga for ten days,
and in an English paper he'd read an account of the
wedding and of their honeymoon trip, and had come
across especially to see them. He'd been in excellent
form too; had been charmingly deferential to and
openly envious of John Ambrose; had ragged Sheila
on her newly acquired dignity; had taken them both
ashore to tea, and then, accepting a really cordial in-

Shipping Company knew well enough how to keep its
guests in good humor-and John Ambrose had aug-

"You're sensible, aren't you, Sweet?" he'd said.
His words were a little blurred but they were distinct

thickly, rather tipsily. "More sensible than I'd ever
given you credit for, because with an old husband you

cabin, Sweetness ?"
Sheila said:
"You're talking rubbish, Derry." It was no use, she

"Not so tight that I can't see on which side my

"I don't like that young man, Sheila, and I like his

He'd never referred to the matter again, even by

ended where it did. Derek had been a fool, of course,

there might have been a hideous row, because John

with another girl next morning. But with John-
there'd been, of course, nothing for him to really get

heard what Derek had said to her before he'd kissed

Annesley boy and girl, a young married couple named

"Personally," Pete Barton said, "I like women. All

my staying on board to read, and for a rubber of

Things were settling down comfortably now. Far more
comfortably than she'd ever thought possible, and for
the greater part of her life John was quite happy with-

that no other man could, by marrying her, rob him of

and it was a life so set in its ways that, even traveling,
even cruising on his honeymoon, there was little or no

self gave to John Ambrose such joy as he'd never
thought it possible to find. As a boy he'd been brought
up strictly and rigorously by a father who believed
rigidly in Hell and in Hell fire, and by a mother to

the smaller public schools, to the University, to keep

been nothing for luxuries. Nothing with which to go
looking for trouble, to make it worth while for women
ΙΙΟ
to love him, to help him along what his father always

He had excellent ways of spending these long lazy

III
ralysis for example, to combat which all manner of

John Ambrose had been giving a good deal of time and

Later that night-very much later-John Ambrose
lay awake in the darkness and silence of his cabin. He
was conscious of a pleasant sense of contentment; of
satisfaction with life and with himself; of pride in the
excellent judgment and acumen that had guided him
to choose for a wife this girl who so exactly suited him
in every way. He asked himself, quite reasonably, what
other woman save Sheila, certainly what other woman
of Sheila's age, would fit so exactly into his scheme of
life; would be content to go off on her own, leaving him
to his own devices and in the peace and quietness he
loved, and would come back in the mood Sheila had
come back? Not many certainly. Probably not one in
a thousand. Because Sheila had been adorable to-night.
He thought, remembering every detail of her sweetness
and softness and daintiness, the touch of her warm,
eager mouth, the feel of her arms about him, that never
had Sheila been sweeter, more utterly adorable than
she had been to-night.
Life, he told himself a little drowsily, had been very
kind to him. He'd no kick coming anywhere really,

CHAPTER XIII
THEY left the ship at New York on the homeward-
bound journey. At Honolulu a new passenger boarded
the Aurania, and John Ambrose came excitedly to find
Sheila.
"It's the most amazing bit of luck, darling. That
chap we took on at Honolulu is Shepherd." He saw
her puzzled look and plunged into explanations. "You
don't know, of course, but Shepherd's done more to
help on research work in the discovery of the infantile
paralysis germ than any man living. He lost one of
his own children with the damned disease fifteen years
ago, and there's no length to which he won't go to run
it to earth. If I could get into touch with him—it
doesn't interest you, of course, but to me-I'd give a
lot to get into close touch with Shepherd, darling."
Sheila said:
"But it does interest me, John. I don't understand
it, of course, but if it's anything that helps you-you're
so terribly good to me. If you're happy and interested
that's the only thing that really matters."
"You make me happy," John Ambrose said simply.
"So happy, my darling, that if I were a superstitious
fool I'd say it couldn't last." He stooped to pick up a
book, tucked it under his arm, prepared to go back on
deck. "You're going to be busy, I suppose?" he said,
and Sheila nodded.
"Up to the eyes. There's a fancy-dress ball to-night
and theatricals to-morrow. You don't have to bother
119
your head about me, John. Run along and make
friends with your Shepherd or whatever his name is.
I'm quite all right."
So he'd gone up on deck, and there he and Simon
Shepherd had met and fraternized. Sheila saw less of
him after that than she usually saw. Simon Shepherd
was traveling alone and he had been only to glad to
find on board a luxury cruise-ship a man whose inter-
ests were almost identical with his own. He and John
Ambrose spent hours together, and if their conversa-
tion would have bored to tears nine-tenths of the pas-
sengers, to them it was enthralling. And at Kingston
on the return trip he'd given John Ambrose the invi-
tation to Colney Head.
"We're only beginning," he said. "When I think
of what's still ahead of us I don't seem able to get on
fast enough, but I guess we've made a bit of headway
these last ten years, and I'd surely like you to see what
we're doing, doctor. If you care to leave the ship in
New York and bring your wife down to Colney Head
for a couple of weeks, I dare say we'd be able to show
you a few things that'd interest you, and we could give
you both quite a good time in a quiet sort of way, you
understand." He added with real cordiality and the
hospitality that stamps every son of America's sunny
south: "I surely hope you and Mrs. Ambrose can see
your way to coming, doctor."

Sheila, as usual, had been perfectly willing.
"I think it'd be fun, don't you, John? Do let's go

And so, when they docked in New York she and

new was worth while. New things made one think,
I 20
absorbed, tremendously, all one's thoughts and one's

was the sort who'd never have tolerated having his

But when they reached New York, Sheila was quite
ready to go to Colney Head. To get out of an atmos-

make friends with new people. And of course America
was marvelous.
Simon Shepherd, apparently, had powers that
weren't granted to every one. Methods of dealing with
Customs authorities, with Immigration officials, with
baggage men and ticket agents, that simplified every-
thing. Both John Ambrose and Sheila were to learn
later that he was even richer than the millionaires
commonly associated with New York and Newport
and Long Island; that the chances were he didn't know
and had never known exactly how much money he had
got. When he wanted a thing done he gave an order
and, incredible though to an outsider the order might
have appeared, it was always carried out. When he
traveled it was in a private car attached to the fastest
train on the system, with his own cook in the galley,
his own men-servants to minister to his comfort, his
secretary always at his elbow, his stenographer in-
variably "standing by" for orders, a spare "drawing-
room" beautifully fitted up and equipped with every
luxury for the guests he wished to take with him.
Later, too, Sheila was to learn many things about this
man who, as a memorial to the child he had lost, had
devoted both time and money and all the influence
122
money had given him, to the discovery of the germ
that had defied all the care and skill money could pro-

rule against which his Church set the mark of her dis-

for the social life practiced by practically every man
and woman of their own financial standing.
99
"We're simple folk," he told Sheila. "You'll find

though I guess we can make you comfortable all right.'
And then seeing her incredulous eyes run round the

My wife's a rich woman, but she's a good one. And
she's simple. We both are. We've got money and we
spend it, but we 'think' simple, if you follow me. You'll
find out what I mean when you get down home better,

And at the colossal white mansion he called "home"
Sheila had sensed at once what he'd meant by sim-
plicity. Simon Shepherd and his wife lived in the lap
of greater luxury than she had ever dreamed possible
a man could live. But at heart they were utterly sim-
ple, abiding by a code of morals as rigid and inflexible
as those governing any Quaker household, tolerating
neither gossip, nor malice, nor uncharitableness; setting
straight, impervious backs against every form of law-

man. Prohibition then was law in America, and no
123
occupant of Simon Shepherd's house, no visitor, no
chance caller was offered a drink or, taking one from
his own private' flask, was ever asked there again. On
Sundays they went to Mass with unfailing regularity,
and with them every member of their domestic house-
hold. Simon Shepherd didn't believe in the inter-
marriage of Catholics and Protestants, and recogniz-
ing the power of propinquity did all in his power to
forestall it by employing in his home only Catholics,
and on Sundays the whole household went with him
to the church he had built, founded, and endowed, and
by whose rule of life he so rigidly and conscientiously
measured and formulated his own.
There was nothing in the dinner served on his first
night at home that even hinted at the sybarite, or was
conspicuous for its wealth. It was a simple meal, per-
fectly cooked and perfectly served by servants who
were obviously devoted to him, and after dinner they
all four sat in the big lovely lounge and listened to the
wireless, and at half-past ten went to bed; Sheila and
John Ambrose in a suite of rooms big enough to house
a really luxurious London flat, and waited on by a staff
of servants whose sole and only duties lay in the care
of their master's guests.
The clinic, to which next morning Simon Shepherd
himself took them, would have fascinated a layman
who knew nothing of what underlay the spotless per-
fection that characterized its every corner, who had no
interest in anything save in its sunlight, its airiness, and
its inherent cheerfulness. But to John Ambrose it rep-
resented the realization of his wildest dreams, the
concrete picture of all his hopes and the aim of his
highest ambition.
Simon Shepherd said:
"We're doing our best. Come in here and see for
124
yourself," and opened, with a key he carried on his
watch-chain, a door made of a solid block of silver
birch. "Mind you, I'm a layman. I don't know the ins
and outs of the job. I put up the money and I run the
business end of the show, and there I stop. Inside this
door are the men that matter. This is their block. They
have their own staff of servants, and they're law to
themselves. They abide by no rules save the ones they
themselves make. If they think they're on the track of
something and don't want to break off for a meal
there's a cook whose duty it is to see that when they
do break off, what they want is ready for 'em, day or
night. I guess, doctor, you with your knowledge'll be a
little more than interested in what the chaps inside
here'll be able to tell you and show you."
Later, in charge of the Medical Superintendent,
John Ambrose went over the wards.
"We're doing what we can," Brand Overton said,
"but God knows it's little enough we can do. We don't
lose as many patients, it's true; since the use of the
serum I suppose the death-roll has been reduced by
seventy-five per cent., and those left crippled by the
damned disease are probably less than fifteen per cent.
of what they used to be. But that's as far as we can
get. We don't know what we're looking for, you see,
or how to recognize it when we find it. And half the
trouble's fear. Pure, unadulterated fear. The very
name's enough to send a whole community into a panic
that lays a good solid foundation for any germ, as you
know just as well as I can tell you. Once we find that
germ, once we can come out into the open with some-
thing that's tangible instead of merely theoretical, and
say 'here's the germ, and here's the antidote that's
going to kill him' after a patient's developed the dis-
ease, fear's gone, and half the battle's fought in our
125
favor. In here," he said, and opened a door that led
into a huge glassed-in veranda, “are some of the poor
little devils in whom we're trying to cure the after-
effects, Doctor Ambrose."
John Ambrose reveled in those two weeks at Colney
Head. They were just starting experiments then with
the instrument which has since been called the human
lung, and he saw that, examined it, tested it, marveling
at its ingenuity. He had the run of the laboratory, was
given a key to the sanctum sanctorum behind the silver
birch door and made welcome there by the men who,
with death in their hands, pitted their wits against the
ingenuity of the devil of disease; he had the entrée to a
library stocked with every known book written on the
subject that interested him more than any other subject
in the world interested him, and with Simon Shepherd
he set up a friendship that—while he knew nothing of
its results then-was to lead him to heights that, in his
most optimistic moments, he'd never once thought him-
self capable of attaining.
And Sheila was happier at Colney Head than she'd
been since her marriage. Perhaps the atmosphere of
the house, something in its quiet simplicity, in its cheer-
fulness, and in its complete happiness, got into her soul
during those days she spent with Simon Shepherd's
wife. Perhaps something Jean Shepherd said: "You're
a lucky girl, Mrs. Ambrose. It isn't many girls who
have a husband as fine and splendid as yours is," helped
to show her more clearly even than her twelve years'
knowledge of him had been able to show her, the solid
worth of the man she had married. Perhaps, with the
memory of the ship and all its petty little intrigues, its
scandals, and its unhappinesses still in the forefront of
her mind, she was learning to put security and safety
and honor before Romance, and even before Jimmy.
1
126
Perhaps, though she was still unconscious of it, the fact
that under the heart she thought had been given to
Jimmy for all time she was carrying the seed that in a
little more than eight months would be John Ambrose's
child, all helped to make life easier and happier and
less turbulent. Because she was happier, and when Mrs.
Bellamy, writing one of her usual long gossipy letters,
said casually that Jimmy had met an old friend of his
schooldays who was rubber-planting in the Malay
Straits, and that he intended to stay there and to join
Dick Palmer in partnership, she was almost glad.
John was so good, so kind, and so unselfish. Asking
so little, so easily pleased, so readily grateful. With
Jimmy out of the way-without him continually and
for all time reminding her of life as it might have been
-surely it couldn't be difficult, or, at all events, very
difficult to forget him altogether, and to make of her-
self John Ambrose's happy and contented wife.

CHAPTER XIV
BACK at home again in the old familiar surroundings
of the Wimpole Street flat it was difficult sometimes to
realize one was actually married to John. There were
times, of course, when realization came home to one
rather sharply. One no longer slept, for example, in
the pretty blue and silver room which, six months
before, had been one's own particular property. After
the wedding Mrs. Owen had stayed on for a week put-
ting things to rights, arranging everything, and she'd
superintended the removal of Sheila's things into the
big room at the front of the house that, hitherto, had
been sacred to John alone. And it was queer to be in
charge of the house, to be its mistress in practice as
well as in theory. She'd always been, officially, its mis-
tress, of course. Ever since, with school over and done
with, she'd come back from Belgium for the last time,
but Mrs. Owen had shouldered all the real responsi-
bility. When there were parties it was Mrs. Owen
who'd interviewed Mrs. Warren, who'd made up the
menu, who'd laid down the law quite nicely but very
firmly to refractory tradespeople, who'd given the final
touch to table and flowers. And now Mrs. Owen wasn't
there any longer, and Sheila herself was responsible.
But Mrs. Warren was a help. She knew all about quan-
tities and the difference between shoulders and loins
and legs, and while she never for one moment forgot
that Sheila was the real mistress of the house she could
128
always be trusted to hold out a steadily helping hand
when necessity arose, as it so often did.
But apart from responsibility, life for Sheila was
very much the same following the return from her
honeymoon as it had been before she went away. There
were parties just as there had been before her mar-
riage, where one met the same people and said very
much the same things as one had been in the habit of
saying. One went to dress-shows, to theaters, to the
movies, to race-meetings, to charity balls and matinées.
One gave formal dinner parties which were a bore and
to which one invited influential patients, and informal
parties which were fun and to which one asked the
people one really wanted to see and liked to have. One
sat on hospital committees, put one's name to appeals
for this, that, and the other, was deferential and atten-
tive to rich mothers whose children invariably had dis-
eases and physical idiosyncrasies unheard of and
completely unknown to other children. All of which
was comparatively easy and straightforward, present-
ing little or no real difficulty, and was, on the whole,
rather amusing and entertaining. And mixed up with
it all was, of course, the excitement incidental on hav-
ing a baby which, while it had its drawbacks and in-
conveniences, was at least an entirely new experience
and as such, if for no other reason, tremendously
thrilling. A baby, Sheila told herself, would be fun.
One looked a sight, of course, for a time, which could
not fail to be disconcerting, but it was rather wonderful
to think that within oneself, by oneself almost, one could
produce anything so wonderful as a real baby. And
John was delighted, almost childish in the exuberance
of his satisfaction. There might be a great many things
missing in one's marriage, as, of course, there were,
but it was tremendously satisfying to one's self-respect
129
to realize how important one was to a man of John's
age and position. One couldn't help comparing, too,
things as they were and as, under other circumstances,
they might have been, so that things as they were
became suddenly even more desirable than those that
might have been, because Jimmy would have had no
use whatever for an ailing wife and a prospective baby.
Jimmy, told of the impending arrival, would have said:
"Oh, Lord, Angel, d'you mean that? What a hell of a
nuisance," and he would have meant it. There'd been
lots of cases among the younger married members of
their own set, and always he'd said the same thing. "I
should think George is pretty sick, shouldn't you?" or
Dick or Henry as the case might be. "A baby's a bit
apt to cramp your style, isn't it?" Jimmy's idea of
marriage was more exciting than John's, perhaps, more
thrilling and a thousand times more romantic, but not
nearly so satisfactory when it came down to brass
tacks, and if one had felt out of sorts and moldy and
couldn't go with him dancing night after night or had
been too sick to lift one's head from the pillow he'd
have been charming and sympathetic, but he wouldn't
have thought of staying at home. And the chances were
he'd have found another girl, who wasn't cramping his
style by having a baby, to go with him. One loved
Jimmy terribly with a love that had in it neither sense
nor rhyme nor reason, so that at the very thought of
him one's heart turned over inside with a jerk that
nearly made one sick, but one knew none the less his
limitations and his weaknesses. As a companion he was
sweet, as a lover so wonderful as to be almost unreal,
but as a husband, faced with the natural results of
marriage, of which a baby was certainly one, he'd have
been a failure. And John, as a husband, was marvel-
ous, taking nothing for granted at all. He hadn't
130
wanted Sheila to have children unless she wanted them
too.
"I think," he'd said, "children should be wanted-
premeditated-not be just the result of a passion that
a few words in a church has made legal and unless you
want them...
99
"Do you?" Sheila had asked, and he'd nodded.
"Yes. Until I married you I'd no idea that I did.
Perhaps I'd no time to miss them. Perhaps I'd never
met a woman I wanted for their mother. Now-if
you're willing-I should like my children to have you
for their mother, my darling.'
99
Sheila hadn't minded then very much, one way or
the other. If John hadn't wanted a child she probably
would never have given the matter any serious thought,
and if children had come as with marriage they so
often did whether one wanted them or not-she would
have accepted the irrevocable and made the best of it.
But with John, somehow, things were different. He
never, for one thing, took her, or the right to her
which marriage gave him, for granted. Her body to
him wasn't simply a source of pleasure and gratifica-
tion and sensual satisfaction, but was instead a temple
to which she and only she had the key and to which he
had access by virtue of unquestioned generosity on her
part. And the knowledge that she was willing for his
sake to carry in that body the burden of his children
made him astonishingly happy and very grateful.
And so, on the whole, if married life wasn't exciting
or romantic, if from time to time one's thoughts slid
with a wrench to Jimmy growing rubber in the Malay
Straits, one was oddly content. Not happy, as one had
pictured happiness, so that life became something
transcendently wonderful, but at least content. And
Mrs. Bellamy, understanding as ever, had commented
131
on it, taking the bull by the horns as she always did
and plunging into a china shop only she would have
dared to enter.
"You were in love with my Jimmy, weren't you,
Sheila?" she said, and had been so sure of the reply
that she hadn't even waited for it. "You probably
are still in love with him or think you are, because
Jimmy's like that, but you're much better not married
to him. I married his father, my dear, and I speak
from bitter knowledge. The sort of happiness that
comes from marriage with a man like Jimmy, Sheila,
isn't worth the pain, because for every ten glorious
moments-and there are glorious moments, I'm per-
fectly willing to admit that, because that kind of man
makes the world's perfect lover-there are so many
hours of misery and pain that in time one forgets the
happiness altogether. You've been wise, my dear.
You've married a man worth a hundred of Jimmy,
for all I love him so much, and if you want to be wiser
still you'll forget him altogether. Or if you want to
remember him at all, think of him as just a pretty
yacht built for sunny days and calm seas but incapable
of standing heavy weather. You've got a grand hus-
band, Sheila, and every chance of a happy life. Don't
let imagination and dreams butt in and spoil it, my
dear."
And with the baby coming it was easier not to think
so much of Jimmy. There were all sorts of fascinating
things to see about for one thing. The repainting and
refitting into a nursery of the blue room that had been
her own; the buying and making up of flimsy materials,
of muslins, laces, of flannel incredibly soft and fine;
the selection of a name; the trimming of the cot, and,
on the whole, one was so well that there were all the
usual run of things to be done as well. Dressmakers
132
were clever in these days, so that one didn't mind going
out, and in any case having a baby was different, ap-
parently, than it used to be, so that one didn't care in
the least who knew. Mrs. Owen, coming over from
her new job in Oxford one afternoon, had been quite
shocked to find Sheila trying on an evening gown and
making plans, over the telephone, for a party that
night.
"When my baby was coming..." she'd begun, and
Sheila had cut in:
"I know, darling. It was a great secret. No one
must be told, and when it became so obvious that
people knew without being told, you stayed at home
alone wondering just how bad the pain would be and
remembering all the girls you'd known who'd died in
childbirth. And at night when your husband came home
from the office you went for a little discreet walk with
him after dark, with your nerves going to pieces and
your general health to the dogs. I've read all about
that. And when the baby came every one was suitably
surprised and said: 'How thrilling!' Well, that's all
my eye and Betty Martin. Having a baby's a perfectly
natural function and what's the good of shying at it?
Besides, the day's gone when you didn't discuss things
like that. I don't care who knows about mine or who
sees me, and Jack Wrightson brought me an ivory rat-
tle this morning. He's going to India to-morrow and
wanted to be sure the baby got a present from him,
so he brought it along. Everybody knows that the
natural sequence of marriage is a baby unless you do
something to stop it, so why bother?"
And John liked her to go out. It did her good and
kept her from worrying.
"No cocktails," he'd said. "Three cigarettes a day
and no more, and one late night a week. Otherwise-
133
carry on as usual. I wonder if you're as happy about
this as I am, Sheila ?"
"I'm terribly happy!" Sheila said, and was sur-
prised to find herself speaking the truth. "It's going to
be simply marvelous to have a baby, and I am happy
about it, darling."
It was a busy winter taking it all round, because in
October Lionel Tracy died quite suddenly from an
attack of angina that no one save himself had even
known he had, and the whole of the practice, which
was still growing, devolved upon John Ambrose. And,
in addition, his laboratory work took up more and
more of his time. There was scarcely a night when he
went to bed before two, scarcely a free hour that didn't
find him studying in the silence of his own little study
or messing with tubes in the laboratory he'd built for
himself on the roof of the house. Again and again he'd
come to Sheila on the brink of the discovery that was
going to rid the world of a disease that refused to be
banished, and had gone back next morning to find that
after all it had only been another will-o'-the-wisp. He'd
taken on an assistant there now, and between them
they kept the nose of the spectacled little secretary
held firmly to the grindstone. There were, amongst
other things to be answered, long letters from Simon
Shepherd at Colney Head. The head of his Research.
Department had found this or that or the other. Had
Dr. Ambrose come across it too? And improvements
had been made in the human lung. A few more experi-
ments and discoveries. And even if they couldn't trace
the germ, at all events they would be able to fight it
more or less successfully. Had Dr. Ambrose read such-
and-such a book or an article written by so-and-so? If
so, what were his criticisms? In addition, he was ap-
pointed Senior Consulting Physician at the Palace Hos-
134
pital for Sick Children, and that, too, took up some of
his time, though it added at the same time to his repu-
tation. In February he found it absolutely necessary to
take a young partner as, years earlier, he himself had
been taken on, with the difference that the new plate
read "Drs. John Ambrose and Ralph Littleton, Con-
sulting Physicians," with his name coming first and he
as the head of the firm of which for so long he had
been junior partner.
It all meant, of course, a great deal of work, al-
though the work brought with it both fame and kudos,
and he was very happy. For the first time he could
honestly look back and know that what he'd done had
been good; that the sacrifices his mother and father
had made for his sake had been justified, and he was
making a lot of money. More than he'd ever thought
it possible he would make. Sheila had her own car now
-she'd driven it herself until he'd thought it wiser for
her to let the second chauffeur drive it instead-and
he was negotiating for the purchase of the house in
which they had the flat, because already just the flat
was beginning to feel crowded. In June, if everything
went according to plan, the house would be his alto-
gether, and there'd be ample room there, so that young
Littleton could have his own quarters without over-
lapping, and there would be a floor for nothing but
nurseries. He didn't approve of only children. In two
years they would with luck have another child; pos-
sibly, if Sheila were willing, still more as time went on.
It was wonderful, when one had a moment to spare
for the luxury of personal introspection and anticipa-
tion, to look forward and picture himself and Sheila
with children about them, and a man at forty-nine
wasn't old. There were days when he felt so vigorous
and young that it was difficult to realize he was past
135
middle age, and of course life had been very kind to
him. One day passing a church he'd turned almost in-
voluntarily and had gone inside. It wasn't the sort of
church he'd been brought up to attend, and there were
candles and flowers on the High Altar, a Calvary slung
from the roof, a statue of a woman he knew must be
the Blessed Virgin in one corner. But acting on that
same uncontrollable impulse he'd knelt down at the
edge of a pew and from the bottom of a heart that
was honestly grateful he'd thanked God for the pleas-
ant place in which his lot had been cast.
And then in January life was kinder still. He was
up at Cambridge lecturing to a class of young medical
students when a servant brought him a telegram.
Sheila, the message ran, had given birth to a son at
four that afternoon, and both mother and child were
doing splendidly.

CHAPTER XV
BABY STEPHEN was eighteen months old when
John Ambrose bought for Sheila the cottage on the
edge of Dartmoor. Driving back one night together
from Tavistock, where he'd been lecturing, to Chag-
ford where they were spending the week-end with
Lionel Tracy's widow, they had seen the notice-board,
"To Be Let or Sold," and Sheila had insisted on
stopping.
"It's such a dear cottage, John. Do let's go inside
the garden and have a look. Only one, darling."
But once inside the garden they had found the
owner, a little white-haired woman, who insisted on
their coming in while she gave them a glass of her
own wine.
"It's too lonely for me these days," she said. "I'm
seventy, you see, and my daughters say the Moor's no
place for a woman of seventy to live alone. It's half
a mile to the next neighbor, and it is lonely now and
then, I suppose. Anyway, I'm leaving it. My girls say
they can't sleep in their beds for thinking of what
might happen to me here alone, and though I tell them
it's just their fancy, I'm going to humor them and let
it go." She looked into Sheila's eager eyes and smiled
at her. "You'd like to see over it, wouldn't you, my
dear?"
"I'd adore it," Sheila said. "Oh, John, look-it's
all so sweet. You can wait, can't you, while I go over
it and see it all?"
137
"Of course, if you really want to," John said, and
sat in the ingle-nook of the room that, half-lounge, half-
sitting-room, ran the whole width of the house, smoking
a pipe and waiting for her. He thought, tolerantly,
'If it gives her pleasure why shouldn't she go over the
place?' and of course it was attractive. Small, but, yes
-definitely attractive. This room, for example, with
its low latticed windows and overhanging thatch, its
ingle-nook and glowing fire. One might be very com-
fortable at night in a room like this. One could come
down here for week-ends-to study and work. Away
from all the noise and confusion and bustle; and in the
summer... He got up, went over to the window,
stood there looking out over the pretty flower-covered
garden, the trim little lawn, the orchard on the other
side of the tiny stream. In the summer it would be
marvelous for Stephen and Sheila. They could come
down here together with Nurse and a maid or two and
the second chauffeur to keep a watchful eye on them,
and be as happy as the day was long. It would do them
good too not that Stephen needed much improve-
ment, thank God. He was a splendid specimen of
healthy childhood, but Sheila had been doing too much
lately going out too much, having too many late
nights. Admittedly she deserved to have a good time
because she'd been really wonderful about the baby,
making all the sacrifices demanded of her without a
murmur and apparently perfectly willing to make
them. It wasn't every young mother, John Ambrose
told himself complacently, who was willing, at nine-
teen, to nurse her baby. Bottles were the first inquiry
of the modern young woman if she bothered to make
any inquiry at all. The majority of his patients' mothers
seemed to think they'd done a little more than their
duty in having the baby at all, and that it certainly
138
wasn't their business to bother about the food it had
to have. After all, there were nurses, weren't there,
and what was the good of paying high wages to ex-

too glad to nurse Stephen. For six months she'd given
up her whole life to the baby-to his baby-John Am-
brose reminded himself a little smugly. Stephen had
taken precedence over everything-over parties, food,
drinks, over, in fact, all the things Sheila had really
liked and enjoyed. She'd even been ragged about her
devotion and she hadn't minded. Stephen was, of

children to whom it was easy to be rather absurdly

membering all she'd given up for Stephen's sake, one
couldn't grudge her a few late nights now that Stephen
not only drank milk from a cup but was also begin-

and spinach, and had even been promoted to an occa-
sional egg. But she was looking tired now and was
apt to be highly strung and a little nervy. To make
mountains out of molehills and to imagine things.
Especially with regard to Stephen. Magnifying things

nesses of terrific importance which, of course, wouldn't

he'd wanted and hoped for always, but she mustn't get
fussy. John Ambrose didn't like callous mothers, of

be nothing but fresh air and yellow gorse and purple
139
heather and flowers in the garden, would be really good

wonderful time, take it all round, in a little cottage
like this.
Upstairs, following her hostess from room to room,
Sheila was thinking very much the same thing. She
hated Stephen to be in London amongst all the dust
and grime and air that must, of necessity, be soaked in
infection of one sort or another. It was all very fine

rather horrible and certainly thoroughly uncomfortable

dear and lovable and adorable. Never, she told her-
self, remembering him in sheer ecstasy, never had there
been any child quite so perfect as Stephen was. And
he had done something for her that neither friends
nor fun nor time nor even John Ambrose himself had
been able to do, because, in some way she couldn't

had helped even where Jimmy was concerned. One
could think of him somehow, with Stephen beside one,
with less pain, with less sense of actual loss. One would

140
and it made everything different. Happier and more

It was an accepted fact-accepted, moreover, by every
one-that doctors' children were invariably neglected
by their own fathers. Or, if neglected were perhaps

Whereas if Stephen sneezed and one began to explain
John said briefly and competently: "Don't fuss, dar-
ling. The child's as right as rain. You really mustn't
get nervy about Stephen, Sheila."
But even if the child were as right as rain, it would

like this, a hidey-hole where she and Stephen could

Finishing the journey back to Chagford, Sheila
talked of nothing but the cottage, making plans, bub-
bling over with thrills and excitement.
"It's going to be simply heavenly, John. To get away
from all the rush and the noise and the heat. Not to
live away from them for always, because I'd hate that,
but to know there's somewhere to go to with Stephen.
Oh, John, it is going to be fun having the cottage, and
of course Stephen'll love it. We can teach him to ride in
a little while. Buy him one of those tiny ponies we saw
on the Moor. I can't think of anything more wonder-
ful than having that cottage, John."
John Ambrose was glad, too. He loved to see her
happy and gay like that, and, of course, the cottage
would be nice. He could go down on Friday nights for
long summer week-ends; he might be able to build a
small laboratory there as well and work at some of his
experiments, and the garden-away from telephones
and bells and calls-would be a marvelous place in
which to read and concentrate. But he hoped just the
same that, left alone with Stephen, Sheila wouldn't fuss
too much over him and pamper him. He didn't want
Stephen to be pampered and babied, and with an only
child...
His eyes slid round to Sheila, still babbling happily
beside him. She looked splendidly fit now. Better than
she'd looked for weeks. With a complexion that owed
nothing to rouge, eyes brighter than any drug could
143
make them, and terribly happy and thrilled about the
cottage and all the plans she was making for the future.
Well, he'd put Smithson & Roberts on to the job to-
morrow. He'd write and tell them to have the place
inspected and thoroughly overhauled and to have the
title-deeds looked into, and if everything was satisfac-
tory he'd buy the cottage and give it to Sheila as an
anniversary present. She'd like that tremendously, was
still young enough to appreciate the importance of own-
ing real estate, and he'd enjoy giving it to her as much
as she'd enjoy receiving it. She loved presents even
now just as she had always loved them, and the present
of a house of her own... He could hear her still
babbling on.
"It'll be so splendid for Stephen, won't it, John?
Out of all the heat and dust, because all that grime
can't really be good for little children, can it? Of
course, Stephen's splendidly fit, but even he loses some
of his color when it gets really hot."
He wished, illogically and quite unselfishly, that
Stephen didn't mean so much to her, that he didn't
occupy so much of her mind. Which in itself was ri-
diculous, because he spent quite a lot of his time en-
deavoring to make young mothers, with a lot of social
responsibilities on their shoulders, pay a little more
attention to their children.
But if anything happened—and things did happen to
children-even to the healthiest and strongest of them.
Something went wrong and there was an end. He had
hoped over and over again that by this time the
brother or sister he and Sheila both wanted for
Stephen might be in evidence, but there was no sign
yet of a second baby, and it wasn't because Sheila had
been unwilling. She'd said quite truthfully, sincerely:
"I'd love another baby, John. You don't have to worry
144
about me, darling." But although this was July and
Stephen would be two next January there was no sign
of any other baby. He said, as he'd said over and over
again with a queer driving note of urgency in his
voice:
"You mustn't let Stephen occupy too much of your
thoughts, Sheila. Of course with all your eggs in one
basket I know it's only natural, but you mustn't..."
He hesitated, stumbling a little because it was so diffi-
cult to put into words that wouldn't frighten her to
death, the fear that, in the case of an only child and
an idolizing mother, was always uppermost in his mind:
"You mustn't fuss over Stephen, Sheila."
Smithson & Roberts sent in their report three weeks
later. They had been very thorough, and the report
dealt with everything it could be expected to cover.
The cottage was, of course, lonely, although as Dr.
Ambrose had seen it personally, he did not need to
have its loneliness pointed out to him. The water-supply
was better than adequate, the drainage reliable and
satisfactory, the roof in a good state of preservation,
the damp course functioning properly, the title-deeds
quite straightforward. If Dr. Ambrose felt inclined, on
receipt of their letter, to instruct them to make an offer
of nine hundred pounds for the property they felt quite
sure it would be found entirely acceptable to the pres-
ent owner, who genuinely wished to get away from
the place, and they would, of course, be only too will-
ing to do everything in their power to get the deal
through as speedily as possible.
It was "through" by the end of September, and
Sheila, with Stephen and his nurse, went down to Chag-
ford to buy furniture.
"I know so exactly what I want, John, so please let
me do it all myself. It's got to be just 'so,' you see.

So, at the end of September, when the moors are
at their purple loveliest, Sheila and Stephen and the
nurse went together down to Chagford. Stephen and
the splendidly competent, capable young woman whose
duty it was to look after him, to spend their days
among the yellowing bracken and the heather and the
soft September winds, and Sheila to spend rapturous
hours delving in antique shops, unearthing a table here,
a chair there, bringing to light from incredibly dusty
corners bits of precious mahogany, of walnut, of Chip-
pendale. Every now and then John Ambrose himself
came down to join them, bringing, inevitably, his books
and microscope, the tools of his trade from which he
could never bear to be separated unless absolutely
necessary.
He'd found, a couple of weeks back, another way
by which the human lung Simon Shepherd had shown
him could be made even yet more human, and he was
always and for ever on the verge of making the dis-
covery that to them both meant so much. With so
much at stake, with thousands upon thousands of
childish lives at the mercy of the disease, one couldn't
waste time just doing nothing about it. Every moment
was of value, every second, because one never knew
where one stood. One might be in the blank utter
darkness of ignorance one minute and then a minute
more and the secret might be an open book. So he
brought his books and his microscope, and both he and
146
Sheila were utterly content with their respective jobs
and quite happy. Sheila continued to unearth tables
and chairs, tall-boys, old chests of drawers, mirrors
that had long histories behind them of fair women and
gallant men. Their rooms at the hotel were a bewilder-
ing sea of patterns for curtains, carpets, bedspreads,
loose covers, but little by little the cottage was being
furnished. Already the sitting-room was finished and
one of the bedrooms, and soon the whole thing would
be ready for occupation. Sheila said:
"I've found a woman who'll go in as caretaker, John,
until I can come back again. We'll have to be in town
over Christmas, but I thought I could get back about
February, and then in May the place'll be ready, and
Stephen and I can be down here all summer. It's going
to be the darlingest place when I've finished with it,
John."
So, in November, the caretaker was installed and
furnishing operations suspended over Christmas. One
had to be in town, of course, over Christmas. There
was all the shopping to do for one thing, and there
were parties one after another on which one couldn't
possibly turn an ungrateful back. And one was looking
one's best. Smugly, facing a radiant reflection in the
mirror, Sheila knew she had never looked prettier or
better or more alluring than she looked at the end of
the third year of her married life.
She didn't see much of John, of course. She had
never seen him as often as most women see their hus-
bands, because, in addition to their ordinary work,
doctors are at the mercy of an inconsiderate public and
have no regular hours to call their own. And added to
the calls and consultation and hospital work that made
up the daily routine of his "trivial round," there was
still the laboratory that, with every day that passed,
147
occupied more and more of his time, more of his
thoughts, more of his enthusiasm.
Sheila used to tell herself these days it was a mercy
she'd had Stephen, because with a husband so wrapped
up in his work that he hadn't a moment to give her save
occasionally in their own room at night, having Stephen
to think of and play with kept her out of mischief.
Women who were still young enough to want admira-
tion and attention and had husbands like John and no
children were always in trouble of one sort or another.
There was Judith Lester for one. Every one knew the
sort of game Judy was playing, because Tim Lester
was too busy to be able to take her about, and Judy
wasn't the only one who killed time in the same way.
Lots of the younger married women did it, especially
young attractive wives of busy doctors who'd nothing
else to do, so that Stephen was a godsend. With him
to play with, to be amused by, one couldn't be dull ever.
And with Jimmy away-one thought about Jimmy dif-
ferently but just the same, with John so desperately
busy, so unavailable, getting yet more unavailable with
the passing of every day, one couldn't help telling one-
self that loving Jimmy as one did and always would,
it was perhaps just as well he was growing rubber in
the Malay Straits. If Jimmy had wanted one to go and
play and John didn't mind-was too busy to mind or
perhaps even to know-it would have been terribly
easy to slip back, and that wouldn't do. Wouldn't be
even safe. Other men made much of one certainly; were
ready, at a single beckoning finger, to go further still
at a moment's notice, but they weren't Jimmy, and
they hadn't Jimmy's grip on one. Useful if one were
lonely, but not essential to one's happiness, and with
Stephen one wasn't ever lonely. There was never, it
seemed, time enough in a day in which to see enough
148
of Stephen, so that one could never be lonely and one
never had time to kill-not, at all events, enough time
to make one do the silly things so many women did.
And just now there was all the Christmas shopping
to be done. Wonderful toys of every size and kind and
