where on every side one was greeted as an old friend:
"Hullo, Sheila."
"Darling, where have you been? I thought you were
dead."
"How's the adored Stephen, Sheila ?"
One had scarcely time to breathe over Christmas-
time, what with one thing and another. There was so
much to be done, to be looked after, things one must
do oneself and couldn't possibly relegate to servants,
not even to Mrs. Warren, so that one was on the run
from morning to night.
And then in January, acting on John's advice and
hoping once more against hope, she went to see Philip
Rogerson.
"I hope it's true this time, Philip. I'd like another
149
baby and John thinks it's a criminal mistake to have all
one's eggs in one basket. But I think it's true this time,
don't you?"
"Quite true," Philip Rogerson said. He reached for
his coat, slid into it, smiling at her. "You can go home
and reassure John all right. Middle of August, I'd say,
and I gather it's genuinely a matter of congratulation.'
"Yes," Sheila said. "I wanted another baby terribly.
I'd like half a dozen. There'll never be one who'll
mean what Stephen means-that's impossible-but I
am glad this isn't just another false alarm, Philip."
She went down to Chagford at the end of February
to finish off the cottage. Having a baby didn't worry
her much really. She told herself she was lucky like
that, remembering other women she'd known who'd
been crocks for months and forgetting that she herself
came of a stock that gives little thought to child-
bearing. Mary Kavanagh's mother had had a baby
every year for ten consecutive years, and with it all
had minded the other growing children and kept a
cottage neat and tidy and cheerful for a weary man
coming home after a long day's work in the fields. Men
of her class had no use for ailing sickly wives, and hav-
ing children was only perfectly natural. Nothing to
fuss about. A nuisance it was when they kept on coming
one after another, but what of it? When a decent
woman got married she took a chance on having chil-
dren, and after all life wouldn't be much, come to think
of it, without them. "Proper little nuisances" they were
time and again, but no one really wanted not to have
them at all. And something of the old idea in the blood
that had flowed in the veins of a long line of hard-
working men and women must have still impregnated
the blood of this girl who, of their flesh and blood,
had yet, in her whole make-up, nothing of any of them
150
save perhaps her health and her magnificent vitality,
because neither Stephen nor this second-coming baby
worried her, and the cottage must be finished. She
would have the baby in London, of course. John in-
sisted on that. In Philip Rogerson's nursing-home, and
under his expert eye, no chances would be taken either
with her or with the baby. She'd be watched and
weighed and measured; every reaction would be tabu-
lated, written in Philip Rogerson's neat little hand on
a card that, headed "Ambrose, Mrs. John," he kept
in an index file beside his desk. The card held every-
thing. Her age, her height, her normal weight, her
normal measurements, particulars of all her childish
illnesses, particulars, it seemed, of everything. Her
grandmother might have taken her children as a matter
of course, have been delivered of them with the doubt-
ful aid of the village midwife, or, as the law grew a
little stricter, by the help of the district nurse, but
Sheila's children were people of importance. To be
brought into the world discreetly and wisely and care-
fully, leaving nothing to chance and still less to the
folly of pregnant women. But Philip Rogerson was
never worried about her; he'd looked after her when
Stephen was born and she'd given him no trouble at all.
Done what she'd been told to do, breathed in the an-
æsthetic when she'd been told to breathe it in, been an
exemplary patient all round, and there was no reason
now to look for trouble. With a first baby-well, one
was always a bit anxious. One never knew exactly
what was going to happen with a first baby, but with
a second and with a woman like Sheila-
He let her go to Devonshire without a second
thought and she spent six weeks down there having a
wonderful time. Stephen and nurse were there, of
course. It was cold, and a bit raw up on the moors,
151
but cold didn't hurt Stephen. He thrived, apparently,
in every sort of weather, and when they all came back
to London, even John exclaimed:
"It's splendid to have you back, darling, and you're
looking tremendously fit and well. You and the boy.
I'm glad I bought the cottage if it's going to send you
back to me looking like this, my sweet."
"I adore the cottage," Sheila said, "and I am fit,
John. I don't think really I've ever felt better in my
life than I do now."
All of which made what happened so soon after-
wards such a complete mystery. Because in June, for no
reason to which even Philip Rogerson with all his
knowledge and experience could give an explanation or
a name, Sheila had a miscarriage. The baby was still-
born; Philip Rogerson said unhappily: "We'll have to
operate, of course. That's her only chance," and for
seven days that to John Ambrose, lurking like a ghost
in and out of her darkened room, seemed an eternity
of waiting, she hung so lightly tethered to life that
every now and then it seemed impossible she could get
well again.
But she did get well eventually, although it was ten
weeks before she was allowed even to sit up in bed.
When she asked a question, Philip Rogerson told her
quite nicely and firmly to mind her own business and to
give her attention solely to the matter in hand, which
was to get strong in the shortest possible space of time,
and while the nurses were certainly far more deferen-
tial they gave her no real information.
She'd been up and about nearly a month before,
cornered, John Ambrose told her the true facts of the
case, which were that, as a result of the operation that
had saved her life, she could never have another child.
152
CHAPTER XVI
AFTER that, of course, Stephen became of even
greater importance than before. During the spring and
summer that followed her illness, Sheila couldn't bear
him out of her sight. She gave up parties, gave up her
beloved race-meetings, gave up the week-end trips she
and John Ambrose had so often enjoyed together,
gave up everything, it seemed, that involved leaving
Stephen for longer than an hour at a time. John Am-
brose said, as he had said a hundred times already:
"I wish you wouldn't do this, Sheila. You're laying
up for yourself such stores of trouble. It isn't that I
fail to appreciate your devotion to the boy, my darling,
but for your own sake"
Sheila would break in with a queer, desperate insist-
ence that worried him terribly:
"I can't leave him, John. Not now. Don't you un-
derstand? He's all I've got. If there'd been another
baby-if I could ever have another baby-it would
have been different. But Stephen's all we'll ever have.
If anything happened to him because I wasn't there to
take proper care of him I'd kill myself. I must be at
home to keep an eye on Stephen myself, John."
Her love for the child became in the end an obses-
sion, and once when Stephen was nearly four and he
had a sharp attack of influenza, she had his bed moved
from the night nursery into the big guest-room, and
slept there with him herself. And somehow, after-
wards, she'd stayed on in the guest-room altogether,
153
with Stephen occupying the dressing-room that opened
out of it. She liked to know he was near her, to be able
to get up in the middle of the night without fear of
waking John, and, creeping into the little room next
door, to reassure herself that everything was well with
the boy. John had asked her once if she meant to come
back, but she'd evaded the question then, and he hadn't
pressed her. He was working terribly hard now, burn-
ing at both ends a candle that was already beginning
to show signs of overuse, and about that time Brand
Overton took three months' leave from the clinic at
Colney Head and came over to London. During those
two weeks in the States nearly five years ago, he and
John Ambrose had taken an instant liking to one an-
other. They were made of the same stuff, imbued with
the same ideas, fired with the fire that stamps a fanatic
all the world over, and there was never a time when
they hadn't things they could say to each other on the
subject nearest of all to both their hearts. And in the
weeks they spent in the laboratory, in John Ambrose's
study, here, there, and in any place where they might
perhaps find another clew to the puzzle they were try-
ing so hard to unravel, he hadn't time for anything
that would distract his thoughts. Hadn't time, it
seemed, even for Sheila or for anything outside his
job, so that, coming to bed at night exhausted and
utterly spent, he grew to be almost grateful for the
empty bed beside his own that saved him the trouble
of even having to talk at all.
Perhaps had he been as adept in the diagnosis of
souls and hearts as he undoubtedly was in the diagnosis
of bodies, he might have recognized the wisdom of
looking farther ahead than just into the immediate
enthralling present; might have seen the folly of leav-
ing a girl of Sheila's age and birth and parentage to
154
fend for herself as it were. Perhaps had he been less
engrossed with his job, less involved in his book and
in his beloved laboratory, more of a sensualist than he
was, what happened afterwards might, very probably,
have been avoided altogether. But John Ambrose
wasn't a sensualist. His upbringing and his parentage
-that long line of bigoted, narrow-minded Scotch
forebears who had labeled sex in every form as "sin"
-had seen to that. He had wanted Sheila badly, de-
sired her as he had never desired anything in his life
before, and with a strength that had definitely shocked
him, but he'd condoned desire by the excuse that chil-
dren were the gifts of God, and that a relationship
leading to children couldn't be merely sensualism. And
now that there could be no more children, now that
the things he'd desired could pertain only to sex, and
led to nothing but sheer physical satisfaction, a great
deal of the desire that had pushed him so unceremoni-
ously into marriage died within him. There were, it
is true, times when, uncomfortably, it sprang to life
again; when almost ashamed he went to find Sheila
in the big airy guest-room on the other side of the
corridor; when, for ten brief minutes, both of them
recaptured something of the passion that had swamped
them in the early days of their marriage. But it didn't
last, and apparently neither of them minded that it
didn't. What had died in him had, it seemed, died in
Sheila too. They were good friends, excellent friends,
but in some way neither of them could quite explain
they didn't want any longer the things they had wanted
five years before. John Ambrose, coming up to bed at
one and two in the morning, was too tired for passion,
too tired even to talk. He wanted sleep more than
anything else then; and alone, he didn't have to be
afraid of waking Sheila by coming to bed so late. And
155
Sheila wanted nothing but Stephen. With him in the
room next door, with the knowledge that even a move-
ment of his would wake her, she'd everything she
wanted in life save the one thing she couldn't have.
An older, wiser woman could, of course, have told her
she was heading straight for disaster, but it would
never have entered Sheila's head to discuss her marital
relationships with any one, and if John Ambrose
should have seen it for himself, he was too busy, too
engrossed with his job, too utterly involved in his
laboratory work and in his experiments to do more
than hold up a warning signal and to trust to her
common sense, and a let-up in his own work to pull
them, before it was too late, back on to a level that
was safer and sounder and solider than the slippery
ice-bank on which they were both, now, so precariously
striving after a foothold.
He knew, of course, that if Sheila needed him no
more than he needed her, it was not because another
man had usurped privileges that should have been his,
or even because another man was vaguely interesting
her. Sheila wasn't that sort. She might be Mary
Kavanagh's daughter, but if the desires that had
driven her mother headlong into trouble had ever
sown their seeds in Sheila's soul, environment and
John Ambrose's upbringing had killed them. She'd
friends wherever she went. Men friends as well as
women, as she'd always had. She was frank and clean
and fearless, and with her men knew where they stood.
They knew from the outset that there were certain
things to which, as a married woman, as Stephen's
mother, as John Ambrose's wife, she definitely turned
her back. Petting parties were all right for unmarried
girls if they enjoyed them. She'd liked them herself
once up to a point, but for a married woman it was
156
different. Married, one had a certain amount of dig-
nity to preserve, a certain aloofness that couldn't pos-
sibly run in double harness with indiscriminate petting,
and one could have every bit as good a time without it.
Simon Shepherd, spending a week-end with them on
one of his frequent trips to London, had remarked
once on the little air of dignity that always enveloped
her.
"I like your wife, Ambrose. There's something
about her a lot of these present-day young women
haven't got. Dignity, I suppose it is, and I like it. I've
no use for these modern women, as you've probably
seen for yourself, or for their new-fangled ideas,
either. I like decency and cleanliness. I do more than
like them. They're indispensable to my way of think-
ing, and that's one of the reasons I like and admire
your wife. Most of the women I see about make mar-
riage no more than a key to licensed prostitution; it
doesn't mean a thing to them. They're planning a
divorce before the ink's dry on their marriage lines,
and I don't hold with divorce." He grinned at Am-
brose through the big horn-rimmed glasses that, some-
how, gave to him the expression of an inquiring and
rather puzzled owl. "Guess you're tired of hearing
my views on that subject, Ambrose. I'm apt to run on
once I get started, although it's what I think just the
same. Marriage sets out to be a clean thing, the clean-
est thing in a woman's life, and it ought to stay clean.
I've no use for women who marry a man because it
provides them with an excuse to do things society
won't otherwise let 'em do."
But there was, quite definitely, no other man. On
the rare occasions when she did go to parties Sheila
was always popular; at dances always sure that she
would never be left to make one of the unfortunate
157
wallflowers who protested so valiantly that dancing
bored them; but there was no man with whom she
danced more often than another, no man who came
more often than his fellows to drink the cocktails that
were to be found almost any afternoon between five
and six in her own pretty drawing-room; certainly no
man of whom John Ambrose could possibly entertain
a disquieting doubtful thought in connection with his
wife, or of whom any one of her own set could whis-
per: "What d'you suppose is going to be the end of
that?"
She spent a great deal of her time in the cottage
on the moors in these days of Stephen's little boyhood.
There was something clean and sweet and purifying in
the place, in the winds that swept across its roof, in
the wide open spaces that spread for miles around it.
Nurse, of course, hated it. She'd have thrown up her
job altogether at the end of the first long summer if
she hadn't been quite sure that it might be a long time
before she found another that, apart from the moors,
suited her so well. And M'Ewen, the chauffeur, on
whose presence as guard and general watchman John
Ambrose insisted, didn't really like it. He and his wife,
who acted as cook-housemaid, and nurse, made up the
cottage's entire staff, and even then there wasn't
enough work to enable them to kill time comfortably;
but Sheila loved it, and it certainly suited Stephen.
He had his own pony now, and a fairy cycle, and
was never indoors unless bad weather made it utterly
impossible for him to go out. He and Sheila used to
go for miles across the moors for picnics-he on his
absurd pony, she on a little bay mare John Ambrose
gave her for her birthday; and on the rare occasions
when his father came down they made high festival
of his coming, and staged a proper party just for them-
158
selves, with John Ambrose himself as guest of honor,
and Sheila and the boy in gay attendance on his slight-
est wish. Sometimes people came from London for
week-ends, bringing into the quiet isolation of the cot-
tage high, gay laughter, late nights, dancing on the
polished floor of the big lounge to the music of the
wireless; but for the most part Sheila and the boy
were alone there, and Sheila asked for nothing more
than the joy of being alone with Stephen, of watching
him grow up and develop, of seeing baby hands and
feet stretched out on exploratory expeditions amongst
the never-ending wonders the moor had to produce
for a small boy's happiness and interest.
Mrs. Bellamy, coming down for a fortnight in July,
said:
"You're a constant surprise to me, you know,
Sheila. I've always pictured you as developing into
something so different. When you used to run about
with Jimmy I never thought you'd find all the life you
needed in a moorland cottage with a baby."
"Not all the life I need," Sheila said truthfully.
"There are times when I know I've got to get back to
London; and in any case it isn't just the moors I like,
Mrs. Bellamy. It's" She hesitated, flushing a little.
"John says I'm stupid about it, but I can't help it.
It's Stephen, you see. I'm so afraid. If anything hap-
pened to him—and in London things happen so easily.
There are always epidemics of some sort, and if he
caught anything I'd just go out of my mind. It wor-
ries John, but I can't help myself, and here-he
couldn't pick up anything here, could he? Look out of
that window, or through any window; you can't see a
single house. There can't possibly be epidemics in a
place like this, can there?"
Mrs. Bellamy said slowly:
159
"You're letting Stephen become an obsession, my
dear, and you mustn't. I know it's hard. Doubly hard
in your case, because you wanted children, but-Sheila,
my dear, be wise about this. Love the boy, but don't
idolize him." Her eyes darkened suddenly. "God's a
jealous God. You mustn't make an idol out of Stephen,
Sheila."
"He's all I've got," Sheila said, and Mrs. Bellamy
interrupted her:
"There's your husband, my dear."
"I know," Sheila said. "And you know too, Mrs.
Bellamy. John and I-we're married, you see, but—it
doesn't mean much, to either of us. I suppose some
of it's my own fault. I've let Stephen swamp the
greater part of my life, and somehow-it's difficult to
explain, even to you, Mrs. Bellamy, because lately-
since I was ill something's happened to both of us.
Something's died in both of us. I suppose with me,
perhaps, it's natural, because I've never been in love
with John, but he's got his work and he's tired. He
doesn't want me, and I—” She hesitated a second,
then went on steadily: "I've never wanted him, you
see. I've never loved him that way. I've lived with him
because, as his wife, there was nothing else I could do
and still play the game decently, and I wanted children.
I wanted children terribly, but I've never felt about
John as I felt about Jimmy-as I still do feel about
Jimmy. I've been happy because I've had Stephen, and
I owe Stephen to John. I'd have been living with him
now probably if I could have had more children, but
you see it simply doesn't seem worth while any
longer. Unless he wants it, and he doesn't. He's got
his work and I've got Stephen. Perhaps I'm not made
quite like other women; perhaps when they operated
they did more than make it impossible for me to have
160
children because all that's over and done with. John
and I get on splendidly, as we've got on all our lives.
I don't suppose there's a married couple living who
get on better than we do, and it isn't as though I were
cheating him. If he wanted me-really wanted me-
I'd go back. That's the truth, Mrs. Bellamy; but he
doesn't, and so you see it isn't worth while. We're very
happy as we are; much happier than lots of people
who think that sleeping together is the only thing that
constitutes marriage. And, after all, I've got Stephen.
I couldn't really, want very much more when I've
Stephen."
But all at once she hadn't Stephen any longer. In
the February that followed his sixth birthday a child
in an East End slum was rushed to hospital with some-
thing that was diagnosed twenty-four hours afterwards
as infantile paralysis. He died two days later, by which
time there were eight more cases, and the first scared
whisper of an epidemic. John Ambrose, plunging
into the thick of it all, hailed as an expert in a case
where there are no experts at all but only one or two
who know just a little more than their fellows, came
home at the end of the third day and realized that
Sheila knew. She said, white-faced and terrified:
"I saw it in the paper, and I'm taking Stephen to
Devonshire to-morrow. I've got to get him away where
there's no risk. No possible chance of danger. I'm
right, aren't I, John? I've told M'Ewen to have the
car ready for us in the morning; I won't even take him
by train. But I'm right to take him away, aren't I?"
"There's no danger," John Ambrose said, "not the
slightest. You can be perfectly sure of that, because
for one thing the boy's never been in the East End in
his life, and thanks to your care of him he's never been
in a bus or a tube, either. There isn't any danger,
161
Sheila, but there's no reason why, if it'll ease your mind
at all, you shouldn't go down to the cottage in the
morning. There's always the chance, of course, that
this epidemic may spread"
"It is epidemic, then," Sheila said, and he nodded
gravely.
"Yes. I'm afraid we'll have to face that, but it
can't touch Stephen, darling. Don't you realize how
utterly unlikely it is that, looked after and watched as
he is, kept out of reach of every possible source of
infection, he could pick up the germ of a disease like
this !"
But, incredible, unbelievable, impossible as it seemed,
he had picked it up. He had a bad headache next morn-
ing and a temperature, and he was sick over and over
again, so that, although everything was packed and
ready, he wasn't well enough to go into Devonshire
then. And three days later he died. Just as the child
in the East End slum had died; and nothing-neither
love nor money nor all the hours his distracted father
had spent trying to acquire sufficient knowledge with
which to cheat Death-had the power to save him!
162
CHAPTER XVII
SHEILA went down to Devonshire after Stephen died.
It was cold and raw and bitter on the moors, and day
after day the countryside for miles around was
swamped in the gray-white blanket of fog that lies
thicker on Dartmoor perhaps than on any other part
of England, but somehow the loneliness, the bleakness,
the inexplicable atmosphere of tragedy that hangs over
what can be one of England's most beautiful as well
as most cruel bits of country fitted just then into the
bleakness and utter loneliness of her own soul.
She went alone, because John Ambrose couldn't
leave London. The epidemic that had so ruthlessly, so
unreasonably, killed Stephen wasn't a bad one as it
happened, but John Ambrose had given years of his life
to the study of that particular disease, and if he didn't
know all it was necessary to know, at least he knew
more than most of his contemporaries, and he couldn't
be spared. He hadn't wanted Sheila to go alone.
"Go anywhere, but not there," he'd said. "My
dear-it's too lonely. You need companionship, and if
I can't give it to you at least go where you'll find it.
Or else take some one with you. I can't bear the
thought of your going alone, Sheila."
"I'd rather go alone," Sheila said. "I-I can't ex-
plain, but down there-if I can find peace anywhere
it'll be there, John. Please don't try to stop me. I'd
much rather be by myself, and I shall be all right."
And in the end he had to let her go. He was rushed
163
to death, and to get away himself was out of the ques-
tion. He was needed far too badly in town, and in addi-
tion to the actual practical work of his job, Stephen's
death had lent to the laboratory experiments a double
importance. He gave himself half an hour off duty
when she left, and went with her himself to the station.
"I hate the idea of this, Sheila. Is it too late even
now for you to change your mind, my dear?" And
Sheila smiled at him out of eyes that had lost all their
youth, all their brightness, and were not the eyes of
a girl at all, but of a woman who has seen all the
hopes of her life lifted high above her head and then
smashed to atoms in front of her.
"You mustn't worry about me. I'm really all right,
John," she said, just as she'd said it a hundred times
since Stephen's death. "And I'd rather be alone. I'm
happier alone just now, John. Really I am.”
So he'd kissed her, stood on the platform while the
train steamed out, and then had rushed back to the
hospital, where beds were filling up far too fast, and
where, hour after hour, other fathers and mothers had
to stand by helpless and impotent, suffering as ten
days earlier he and Sheila had suffered, as indeed they
would suffer to a greater or lesser degree throughout
all that was left to them of their lives.
At first, alone in the pretty cottage at the edge of the
moor, Sheila wondered why she'd come there by her-
self; why she hadn't listened to John; why she hadn't
gone anywhere rather than yield to that crazy impulse
to seek peace here, because the whole place brought
back Stephen so clearly. It was full of him everywhere.
There were some of his toys still lying about in the
room that had been his nursery; there was his pony
grazing contentedly in the paddock outside the dining-
room window; there were all the people in the village
164
who'd known him and loved him, whose pitiful, pitying
eyes followed her in silent sympathy, who wanted to
talk to her about him but who, with the innate under-
standing that is so characteristic of country-folk, left it
to her to make the first move. One couldn't, it seemed
in those first hideous days, get away anywhere from the
things that brought back with such tremendous force
the things one wanted so terribly to forget. The way
Stephen had run down the stairs in the morning, clat-
tering because his absurd riding-boots were even then
just a shade big for him; the echo of his voice that
filled every nook and corner of the house.
She told herself desperately: 'I was a fool to come.
John was right. I oughtn't to have come here,' and yet
couldn't tear herself away, held, almost against her
will, by the calm serene gravity of the moor that sur-
rounded her on every side.
Two days after her arrival the Vicar came to see
her, doing his best, conventionally, and piling platitude
on platitude, to break through the bitterness he saw
written so plainly all over.
The Rev. George Dorman was a kindly man, but
he was a dreamer, a bachelor, and a recluse. He could
have answered, clearly and quite definitely, questions
on any theological subject, given chapter and verse for
all his answers; he preached on Sunday morning and
Sunday evening sermons that were solid and learned
and beautiful examples of word-painting and phrasing;
he gave conventional advice at weddings, conventional
consolation at funerals. That was all part of his job,
and there were quite a lot of widows who spoke with
facile tears of the comfort derived from the Vicar's
platitudes. But the stony silence, the dry-eyed tearless-
ness he found in Sheila was something that baffled and
disconcerted him. He didn't know what to say or how
165
to say it because the things he had said to other mothers
and that had always seemed right and fitting suddenly
seemed to lose their efficacy. A more practical man, a
married man with children of his own, might have been
able to help her, because Sheila didn't want platitudes.
She was bitter, her soul was frayed to ribbons, and
when the Vicar quoted to her the little familiar clichés
on which he always fell back in times of stress and
difficulty they left her cold. She didn't then, with
Stephen lying in his tiny grave, want to be told that
all things are for the best and that God is Love, be-
cause obviously the whole statement was ridiculous
and silly and a lie. If God were Love how could He
have seen fit to take away a baby like Stephen? Who'd
never hurt any one; who was the only thing one loved
and wanted and needed. One didn't want to think of
Stephen as a flower plucked by an Almighty Hand for
the decoration of a Heavenly Garden. One wanted
him alive, shrieking with laughter, being naughty and
disobedient and getting into trouble, falling down and
running to one with bleeding knees and cut hands for
comfort and consolation. One wanted him here, warm
and glowing, his little straight lovely body burnt
brown by the sun, his hair ruffled and tousled and un-
tidy, his hands grubby, his voice raised incessantly in
the appeal that meant more to one than anything else
in the world: "Mother-listen. I want you.... Mother,
just a minute, just only a minute, Mother."
The Vicar, Sheila told herself dully, meant well, but
he didn't understand. It was one thing for him to draw
pretty pictures of child angels who would never know
the sufferings of a hard and cruel and irreligious world,
but the child angels whose pictures he drew so graphi-
cally weren't the souls of his own children. He'd never
carried a baby for nine months under his heart, felt it
166
quicken and leap into life, given it birth, nursed it,
watched it grow up day by day, and then had to stand
by, impotent, helpless, powerless to do a single thing
to help, and watch it die. He meant well, one had to
believe that, but he didn't understand. No one under-
stood. No one in the world really understood what
one felt like-inside-remembering that Stephen was
dead. And it stood to sense, knowing what one did
know, that whatever the Vicar said, God couldn't really
be Love, and that all the platitudes and clichés were
just lies, devised and invented by a cunning Church to
blind people to the indescribable cruelty of the God
they set up and worshiped.
And while John did understand up to a point, be-
cause John had really loved Stephen, he didn't feel as
one felt oneself. He didn't feel cold and dead inside,
deaf and dumb and blind and utterly stupid, and he had
his work. If only one had something, anything. Any-
thing at all so that one didn't have to think and think
and go on thinking, unable for a single second to for-
get, incapable even for a day or a night to find any-
thing or any one who would understand what one
felt and be able to help one.
When she'd been at the cottage a week John wrote
her that he was selling his practice. He said, filling
four pages with his scriggly, undecipherable hand:
"I've thought it over very carefully and I hope you
think I'm doing the right thing. I have made a lot of
money these last years, and Stuart Daly, who wants to
buy the practice, is willing to give me all I ask, so that
the question of the wherewithal to live for the future
is not one that need worry us. I have never had ex-
travagant tastes, and if I never make any more, what
we have and what the sale of the practice will bring
will provide you with ample for your needs, and I feel
167
convinced that in giving up my private practice
doing the right thing. The epidemic is abating, but the
fact that there has been an epidemic at all has taught
me my duty, and it is for this reason that I have de-
cided to devote the rest of my life entirely to research
work, in the hope that if I cannot isolate the germ
of which it is the outcome I can, at least, find an anti-
dote, so that what has happened during these past two
weeks, and, in a lesser degree, is still happening
amongst our children, may never have the opportunity
of happening again.
"My plan is to stay on in this house, because for one
thing it is our own, and for another you like it and my
laboratory is here. You will still have the cottage, and
my only hope is that some day you may be happy again.
I think of you so often, my Sheila...."
She wrote back by return:
"It isn't for me to say 'yes' or 'no.' If you think
you can do better work in the laboratory it seems to
me the answer is there. Quite plainly. And in any case
I shouldn't interfere, although I envy you your work
so much. Sometimes it seems to me that I have nothing
-nobody—and then it comes over me that a great
deal of the loneliness is my own fault. I had Stephen
and I let you go. Perhaps if I'd tried, if I hadn't let
Stephen take up all my time and all my thoughts, I
could have kept you. Perhaps you didn't even want to
be kept, and now in any case it is too late for both of
us, because you are too busy and I am dead. D'you
think I'll ever come to life again? I wish I could, John.
I wish, so terribly, terribly much, that we could go
back, and, if we can never again find Stephen or what
gave us Stephen, at least find the lovely thing we used
to share before everything went smash and ruined
our lives."
168
When John Ambrose read that letter he made up
his mind to go down to Devonshire that night. There
were things he could say that could never be written,
and if Sheila needed him He would go down there,
and alone, in the peace and quietness of the moor, they
would find each other again. They would face the
truth, calmly and steadily. Life could never be the
same again. They had outgrown the old days, outlived
passion, found themselves bereft of all the things that,
at the beginning of their married life, had seemed
so all-important, so tremendously all-engrossing, but
surely, surely there was something on which they could
get a grip, to which, desperately, they could hold fast
until after a time the current that had swept them into
its stream would leave them high and dry on a bank of
at least companionship and good fellowship. Surely,
even if passion in both of them were dead-as passion
so inexplicably can and does die-there must be some-
thing left somewhere that would be worth while and
that would help once again to make life worth living.
But when he was ready to go, when his suitcase was
packed and the car actually at the door, Brand Overton
arrived. His ship had docked that afternoon and he'd
come over specially to investigate the cases that were
still in hospital. He said: "There are a lot of things
you and I can learn from each other, Ambrose, and
correspondence is no good. I thought if I came over
and we thrashed things out together there might be
something I could do"-and in the five hours that
followed, when they sat comparing notes and cases,
John Ambrose forgot Sheila and forgot the letter.
He wrote a week later, but it wasn't the same thing,
and he hadn't then time to go to Devonshire. Brand
Overton and he were on the verge of big things, of
169
what might be the biggest things they'd ever done yet,
and he hadn't a moment to spare.
"But you must never say again you have no one,
my Sheila. Whatever happens, surely we have each
other, and my thoughts are always with you. When you
come back we must find time to see more of each other
than we have had time to see in the past rather hectic
five years."
Sheila put the letter away with the other. She was
sorry for John-sorrier for herself, of course, because
John had his work-but sorry for him too. Between
them they'd made such a hash of life when it might
have been such a success. If she hadn't had an over-
developed sense of duty, if ideals on the subject of love
and loyalty and gratitude hadn't played such a big part
in her make-up, everything would have been all right.
She'd have let common sense play her hand for her,
have turned her back, as undoubtedly common sense
would have told her to turn it, on all the things about
which John Ambrose had raved in delirium in that
hospital room, and in the end he'd have forgotten his
need of her, drowned it in his work, outgrown it as
he had outgrown it since, because he didn't want her
any longer. He had wanted her for a time. That was
true, but now-all that was over. They were both
exactly where they'd been seven years earlier, except
that because of those hours in the hospital Jimmy had
had to be side-tracked, and if Jimmy hadn't been side-
tracked-if Jimmy were here...
Alone in the silent house, Sheila put that thought
aside swiftly. Thoughts like that wouldn't do. Ever.
Least of all now, when Jimmy would have been such
a help.
If John had needed her it would be different. If
he'd been dependent on her for anything, been like
170
Philip Rogerson, for example, who was always need-
ing Joan for something-"Where's my shooting-stick?
Have you seen my fishing-tackle? I say, Joan, what
the hell d'you suppose is wrong with the car this morn-
ing?"-it would have been different. She wouldn't
have felt so lost, have had time to sense the emptiness
of life when no one wanted her help, because Stephen
had been for ever wanting something, but John never
needed help for anything. He was utterly independent,
knowing exactly where everything was, despising, a
little, men who had to appeal to women for assistance.
All his gear was where he himself had put it, where he
knew where to find it, and Roberts or M'Ewen looked
after the car. He'd certainly never have thought for a
moment of asking Sheila what was wrong with it even
if it went wrong, which, thanks to Roberts' care, it
never did. He rather prided himself on his complete
independence, on his ability to fend for himself, and on
not having to "bother" Sheila about a lot of silly trifles.
He'd have been surprised and quite shocked to realize
that his ability to look after himself was one of the
things that made Stephen's death and, indeed, life
itself less bearable than it might, perhaps, otherwise
have been.
Jimmy, of course, would have been quite different-
always losing things, always wanting something, hope-
lessly incompetent, utterly inadequate to take care of
even the smallest part of his belongings. And work
and he had nothing in common at all. If Sheila had
wanted him to play, he'd have flung aside whatever
he was doing and gone, blithely, to play any game she
had in mind. "Work-what's work? It's a mistake,
you know, darling, to let work upset pleasure. And
while I make a lot of mistakes I don't make one of
that sort."
171
If, Sheila told herself for the hundredth, thousandth
time, now that Stephen was gone and didn't want her
any more, John had found her a necessity even to his
comfort, life would have been tolerable. There'd have
been less time in which to be sorry for herself, less
time in which to grieve for Stephen, to agonize over
his death; and what she suddenly realized with a
nerve-shaking jolt was even more important still, less
time to think of Jimmy.
Alone at the cottage, without John, without Stephen,
it was disconcerting-terribly disconcerting-to realize
how badly, all over again, even after all these years
when one had felt so sure the whole thing was dead-
one wanted Jimmy.
But at the end of a month the moor did what she'd
hoped it might do. With the coming of spring there
came, too, if not forgetfulness, at least something that
was a sort of resigned acceptance of the inevitable, so
that one could look back without actual agony on the
days of Stephen's life and look forward with less sheer
dread to a future that didn't hold him. One day, tramp-
ing across the moor, she'd come across a shepherd. He
was stooping down over something that lay on the
ground, and when she got nearer Sheila could see that
two baby lambs lay there. Beside him, puzzled, bleat-
ing, a ewe stood watching him, and as Sheila passed
him he looked up, the friendly smile that is so char-
acteristic of all the Devon countryfolk creasing a
beautiful weather-baked face.
"'Tis a proper fine marnen', ma'am," he said; and
Sheila stopped for a second, smiling back.
"Baby lambs?" she said, and he nodded.
"Ay, ma'am. 'Tis babies they be fur sure, though
one be dead." He stood up, the living lamb in his arms,
his hard brown hand on the funny bright-eyed baby
172
head, the ewe watching with wary eyes his every move-
ment. “'Tis as well may be. 'Twill gie the livin' one
a better chance, an' 'tis the way o' things, ma'am.
Seems hard that a li'l thing like that's got to die, but
I reck'n 'tis the good God knows t'ewe can't feed 'em
both, and one goes to make life a bit easier for t'other.
There do always be a reason even fur a cruel thing
same as death ef so be 'ee stops to think it out, ma'am.”
On her way back his words stuck in her mind.
What if perhaps the old shepherd were right? What
if there had been a reason for Stephen's death? Not
the silly selfish sort of fairy tale reason the Vicar had
tried to tell her about Heavenly Gardens and angel
flowers, but the simple, practical reason the old shep-
herd had given her. What if, instead of the sheer
wanton waste she had thought it, Stephen had died
to make life easier for the other children that would
come after him; to encourage by the very reason of his
death the work his father was doing? To point the
urgent, desperate necessity of all the experiments and
the work that went on behind the closed laboratory
door. If that were true-and it could be true, because,
living close to Nature as that old shepherd lived,
taught men truths kept hidden from the eyes of other
less privileged men-then perhaps Stephen's life
hadn't been wasted after all. Perhaps it had been a
sacrifice that had been asked of her a great and terri-
ble sacrifice, certainly, but still a sacrifice. One could go
on, one could bear life more easily, if one could think
of Stephen as laying down his life that others might
live. The world would never be the same again, never
the light, gay thing it had been during his lifetime,
but at least to remember what the old shepherd had
said brought the only comfort she had known since
he died. At least one could look back and know that
173
Stephen's life-his beautiful, sunny, baby life-hadn't
been altogether wasted. It mightn't make up for all
one suffered, nothing in the world could ever do that,
but at least, to believe that Stephen hadn't just been
wasted was better than nothing.
She went back to London in early June. It was
lovely on the moor, getting lovelier every day, but one
couldn't just stay on there indefinitely. Sooner or later
one would have to go back, to take up one's life where
one had dropped it, to push the dead past into the
background and to face people again. And there was
John. Slaving from morning till night in the laboratory,
snatching sleep when he could get it, working like a
galley slave so that the world might be a little wiser,
its babies a little safer. Mrs. Warren wrote that John
was "getting real troublesome, Miss Sheila, what with
no regular meals and all that sort of rubbish what
can't possibly do him nor no one else any good." Sheila
gathered he ate when he felt hungry, which was seldom
if Mrs. Warren were to be believed, and slept only
when Nature refused point-blank to let him stay awake
any longer.
"He just lives in that nasty-smelling room of his
up in the roof, and if he was left to himself I think he'd
sleep there too, which isn't no good for any man. I'd
be the last to get you to come back before you're feel-
ing like you ought to come, but just the same I can't
help thinking I'll be glad when you're here. To see a
man working the way the doctor's working these days
is plain unreasonable, Miss Sheila, and if he'd only
give me time to get the words out I'd tell him so
myself."
But, back at home, there wasn't much she could do
after all, because John refused point-blank to be fussed
over or babied. He said:
174
"I'm absolutely fit, my dear, and there isn't a thing
wrong with me, so please don't worry. And I don't
think you'd better count on me for to-night either.
Carteret and I are up to the eyes in work, so if you
don't mind I think you'd better go without me."
After a bit she gave up even suggesting he should
go to the parties to which he was asked with her, be-
cause he never went. He never even wanted to go, was
never happier apparently than when he was shut up
there in the laboratory with young Carteret and the
little ginger-headed secretary, with his bottles and
tubes and his books, and after a time people got used
to seeing her alone, to asking her by herself to dances
and dinners and night-clubs, to look upon her as a
useful unattached woman who could always be counted
upon to be a credit to any dinner-table.
If at eighteen Sheila Kavanagh had been a pretty
girl, Sheila Ambrose at twenty-five was beautiful. She
had poise and dignity and that indefinable quality
which for want of a better definition we call charm;
she knew how to wear her clothes, when to talk, and,
which is a much rarer attribute, when to say nothing
and to listen while a man talked instead. She had the
reputation among women of being good-tempered and
decorative, which reputation is more easily talked about
than earned, and all men liked her, though she took
care to let none of them like her too much. She was
very cautious in what she did in those early days after
Stephen died, because, lonely as she was, it would have
been perilously easy to have grabbed with both hands.
the companionship men were only too ready to offer
her. And John never interfered. On the rare occasions
when they met he said, as he'd said for years on end:
"You're looking very pretty to-night, my dear," and
left it at that. Only rarely did he ask her where she
175
was going or with whom, and after a time even the
hope that he might want her, that there would be
something she could do for him that would fill the
gap left by Stephen, died a natural death. She was
popular, and back in London there was plenty to do,
and, recognizing her loneliness, people included her in
everything that went on, so that little by little life
slid back almost into the groove in which it had run
so smoothly and so happily prior to her marriage and
to Stephen's birth. John had his job, she had her
friends; and if sometimes she began to wonder what
was the use of having married John at all since he
required of her so little, she pushed the query back out
of sight and refused to think of it.
And then at the end of October something happened.
Jimmy Bellamy, growing rubber out in the Malay
Straits, had his fourth bad shot of malaria inside
seven months, developed blackwater fever, from which
he nearly died, and, given a year's leave, came home
to England to recuperate.
176
CHAPTER XVIII
THAT she was still in love with him she knew from
the moment when at one of Fay Rogerson's over-
crowded cocktail parties Mrs. Bellamy told her he was
coming home.
"You and Jimmy were such friends, Sheila. I only
had the news just before I came out. He cabled me,
so I wanted you to know at once-unless you've heard
from him...."
"Jimmy and I don't write," Sheila said. She was
conscious of something that flicked sharply against
her ribs, that stood still and then began to hammer so
that she thought Mrs. Bellamy must hear it, even in
the overcrowded noisy room. She told herself, forcing
herself to listen calmly and quietly: 'He's going to be
married, of course. That's what it is, and it's only
right. It's unreasonable to suppose that some day he
wouldn't marry. Even in spite of what he said.'
She spoke to Mrs. Bellamy, steadying her voice so
that there was in it not so much as a flicker of emotion.
"We've never written to each other, Mrs. Bellamy.
We thought-oh, what we thought doesn't matter
much, really, does it? But I'd like, terribly, to hear his
news."
And then Mrs. Bellamy had told her. Jimmy had
been ill, terribly ill, and he was coming home, was
indeed already on his way. "He's due on the sixteenth,"
she said, and suddenly her eyes had filled with tears
and her lips had quivered. "I don't ever say very much,
177
Sheila, but I can hardly believe can hardly believe
God has been good enough to send Jimmy home to me
after all these years.
99
Sheila went straight home after that party. Fay
said: "Why, darling, you're not going, are you? Oh,
Sheila, listen, don't be silly, darling. We're going on
to Tommy's show-ten of us, and you've got to come.
Sheila-listen- -" And Douglas Martin had added
his own appeal: "You promised you'd let me drive you
to the theater, Mrs. Ambrose. I can't believe you're
going to let me down," but she'd shaken her head,
sticking to her guns.
"I've got to go home. I'd forgotten-there's some-
thing I've got to do." Somehow she'd steadied her
voice, pulled herself together, nodded to Fay Roger-
son and young Martin. "I'm sorry, but I must go,
Fay."
She walked back to Wimpole Street because she
wanted time to think, and as she walked through the
crisp October air a voice hammered ceaselessly in her
brain. Jimmy was coming home... coming home...
Jimmy... he was coming home.
She tried to think quietly and coherently, to remind
herself that Jimmy's home-coming now could mean no
more than it would have meant six years ago. There
wasn't Stephen, certainly, but there was still John.
Still her wedding ring, her marriage vows, and John's
blind, unquestioning faith in her. John might, engrossed
as he was in his work, forget her for weeks at a time,
but he had, as he had always had, the sure and certain
knowledge that under no circumstances whatever would
she let him down, so that even if people were quite
ready to sympathize with her, to regard her as a
neglected wife, to hint, indeed, as Pauline Dexter and
Rosemary Oliver had already hinted quite plainly, that
178
if she found means of amusing herself that were not,
perhaps, altogether orthodox no one would really
blame her, there was still John to be remembered and
taken into consideration. And there was something
else, too, to be remembered, which was harder even to
consider than John, because eight years, after all, was
a long time, a long time in the life of any man, but in
the case of Jimmy, who had never been known to be
faithful to any one woman for longer than a year at a
time, an eternity.
It was all very fine for Jimmy to swear eternal
fidelity, to insist there could never be another woman.
Men did things like that under the stress of emotion
and a tropic sky, and if one were honest with oneself—
no matter how desperately one wanted to believe them
-one took them with much more than a grain of salt,
especially when the men who said them were men like
Jimmy. And out there, in the Malay Straits.... One
read stories of things that happened out there-where
women were scarce and for that reason of far greater
value. And if one weren't altogether mad one knew—
beyond all shadow of doubt-one knew a man like
Jimmy couldn't be faithful to a girl he'd never even
seen once for eight long, desperately crowded years.
"Oh," Sheila told herself furiously, "I'm behaving
like a fool, like a stupid, sentimental, love-sick school-
girl, and there's no sense in it. I'm not a child. I'm not
even very young any more. I'm twenty-seven, and he's
been away nearly eight years. The whole thing's ridic-
ulous, because he's probably forgotten all about me.
He will have forgotten me, of course. Men like Jimmy
don't remember married women for eight years when
they've nothing to remember them by. Not even a
thing to help them. And in any case there's John.
Jimmy's sure to have forgotten me, but even if he
179
hasn't there's still John. I've got to remember, I've
got to remember, even if he hasn't forgotten, that
there's always John."
And he hadn't forgotten. He hadn't forgotten any-
thing. He landed at Southampton at five in the after-
noon of a miserable foggy November day, and at a
quarter to eight, when Sheila was dressing to go out to
dinner with the Rogersons, the telephone on the table
beside her bed rang shrilly, twice in quick impatient
succession, and then, as though the caller couldn't even
wait the thirty seconds it took her to put down her
hair-brush and cross the room, it rang a third time.
Sheila knew who was calling her long before she
reached the telephone and lifted the receiver. It was
quite a usual time for people to call her up, because,
as a general rule, they could count on finding her at
home between seven and eight, but instinctively, with
utter and complete certainty, she knew whose voice
would greet her when she spoke. And then, picking up
the receiver with hands that refused to stop shaking,
she heard Jimmy speaking.
"Hullo, Angel," he said, and at the sound of his
voice something that was warm and vital and tre-
mendously reassuring and comforting swept through
her whole body. "Jimmy speaking. I got in ten minutes
ago. Listen, darling. Mother says you're dining with
the Rogersons, and in any case I'll have to dine in
to-night. She'd break her heart if I didn't, so I'll be
round about eleven. See? Yes, about eleven. I say,
Angel-did you hear?"
"Yes!" Sheila said. The room was swinging round
her head and she wondered stupidly if, over the wire,
her voice sounded as choked and stifled to Jimmy as it
did to herself. "I heard. I-heard quite well, and
180
that's quite all right. I mean, I'll be here, Jimmy. I'll
be back here just about eleven."
"I should jolly well think you would," Jimmy said.
His voice had the little arrogant, cocksure note it had
always had, a note that said as clearly as though he'd
spoken: 'I know exactly where I am and what I want,
and I'm going to have it too.' He had always had that
little cocksure note in his voice; always, Sheila re-
membered, been utterly certain of his welcome, utterly
certain of his place in the sun. "Haven't I been away
the best part of eight solid years? I should jolly well
think you would be home at eleven. And not about
eleven either, darling. At eleven-sharp." And then
softly, tenderly, so that the blood singing through her
heart almost suffocated her: "Are you glad I'm home
again, Angel?"

CHAPTER XIX
THAT winter, for the first time in her life, people
began to talk about Sheila. It didn't, at the beginning,

That, at least, was true. It was lovely to have Jimmy

183
of the old days, the gay mad days before life had had

They said all sorts of things that at one time would

And Jimmy hadn't forgotten, either. He hadn't

184
"You knew I'd come back some day, didn't you,

It was only natural, however, the world being what
it is, that people should talk, and that, for the same
reason, the things they said weren't always kind, or
even charitable.
John Ambrose didn't hear them because, with every
day that passed, he knew less and less of what went on
in the world outside the little ring that encircled his
laboratory and his study, and when Sheila heard them
she didn't care. Jimmy helped her to forget, and that

didn't, whatever people might have thought or said,

and women who made up their own particular set, but
of what they did they made no secret, and they were
together everywhere.
191
For whatever Jimmy hoped to gain, he could, ap-

had badly worried his mother, and Carl Bronford, the

had to take care. After the first time when he'd col-
lapsed at a dance, he'd given up dancing altogether,
and when he and Sheila went to the "Silver Slipper" or
to the "Blue Hawk," or to any of the night clubs at
which they were both already well known, it was to
sit at a table on the fringe of the dance floor to watch
others, but never any more to dance themselves. Carl
Bronford had been very straight with him.
"You've got to go steady, my lad. I'm no alarmist,
but what I say is true, and you can't play tricks with
a heart in the state yours is in. Until I give you leave
there's to be no excitement of any kind. Of any kind—
neither mental nor physical. D'you understand that?"
Jimmy had understood perfectly well. The last at-
tack had frightened him a little, and he knew Bronford
was right. So he'd given up dancing, given up all games,
given up everything, apparently, save his pursuit of
Sheila. And even that he took quietly. He'd waited
for her eight years and now-well, it wouldn't hurt
him much to wait three or four months longer. Sheila
could talk and argue, but in the end she'd give way. He
felt sure of that, and it was because he was so sure
that he could afford to mark time and to wait until
such time as she herself took the first decisive step
forward.
In the meantime they were both happy. When the
192
days grew longer and warmer they went out into the

he'd stayed at Chagford in the hotel and they'd spent

by day in the laboratory amongst his bottles and test-
tubes, by night in the study with his books, and with
the pages of closely written manuscript that one day
was to be the greatest authority on the disease for
which he was struggling so hard to find an antidote.
At first he'd missed Sheila. A little, but not much,
because he had so little spare time in which to miss

to spare, he took her absence for granted, thankful
from the bottom of his heart for any diversion that
could take her out of herself, and if any one had told

Nature they had slipped back to their original rela-

bitterly the hours she would have taken from the work

CHAPTER XX
WASHING his hands in one corner of the laboratory,
scrubbing them with pumice stone in an effort to re-
move from them at least some of the stains left there
by chemicals and acids, Basil Carteret watched his
Chief, still at work under the hard white glare of the
electric bowl above his head, and wondered how much
longer he could keep it up without cracking. The man
was tired to death, played out, and he looked it. It
showed in his face, in the lines round his mouth,
sounded in his voice when he spoke. From where he
stood in the shadowed end of the room young Carteret
could see him quite plainly. He was standing in front
of a table littered with bottles, with racks of test-tubes
and with Bunsen burners, watching a queer green
liquid as it bubbled over the flame below it. Once he
lifted it, holding it high against the electric bulb over-
head, and the reflection of the light on the liquid threw
a greenish glow over a face that, despite the eager an-
ticipation of the man who is always hoping against
hope, was utterly exhausted, completely spent. Drying
hands on which the stains still offended him young
Carteret thought, 'He can't go on like this. No man
could. One of these days he'll crack—and crack badly!'
He spoke over his shoulder, turning to hang the towel
over its gleaming nickel rail: "Are you sure there's
nothing else I can do, sir?"
For a moment John Ambrose didn't answer. He
was turning the tube that held the green liquid round
195
and round against the light in those nervous, beautiful
fingers of his, staring at it. Then he set it in a rack and
spoke without taking his eyes from his job.
"No-nothing, thanks. I've got to stop a bit longer.
There's just a chance-the faintest chance" his
voice trailed off uncertainly, and young Carteret spoke
again-urgently.
"You can't keep this up, sir. When did you have
your last decent meal?"
"Breakfast," John Ambrose said and turned sud-
denly with one of his quick, unexpected grins. "You're
developing into a hen, Carteret, and I decline to play
the part of the customary feeble-minded chicken to suit
your idiosyncrasies. Get on home, boy, and leave me
to work out my own salvation. Good Lord, you've been
here long enough this week, haven't you?"
"I'd like to see you slacking up a bit," Basil Car-
teret said doggedly. "Our job needs men like you, and
they've got to be watched. At the rate you're
going..
"I know," John Ambrose said. "You remind me-
you and Miss Rogers-of a housekeeper I used to
have years ago. She had a Scotch conscience, and she
spent most of her time foretelling the evils that'd
befall me if I didn't better my ways. But I didn't better
my ways, and I've come out of the mess extraordinarily
well. Get on home, boy, and don't croak. If this is what
I think it is..." the banter dropped out of his voice.
"God, Carteret, we've worked, you and I. We've not
left a stone unturned for the last two years. We've
found things no one else has found and yet we can't
find what we're looking for."
"We've made headway," young Carteret said, and
John Ambrose broke in.
“Fifty yards over a distance of half a mile," he said.
196
"Every time we think we've got it-that the winning
tape's actually in sight and then-there's a snag and
we're back at the beginning. Sometimes I wonder if it's
worth while; worth all the sweating and toiling and the
heartbreak, and then I remember my son and that a
little more work on my part might have saved him;
that we may, every minute, be on the very edge of what
we're trying to find, and I know it's worth while. I'll
hunt this thing down if I die doing it, Carteret, and
I'll find it. I shall find it in the end, and when I do"-
he came across the room, laid an affectionate hand for
a second on the younger man's shoulder-"I'll know
who I'll have to thank for it. Without you and that
little Rogers girl—I think sometimes I'd have given
up in despair long ago if it hadn't been for you

a failure I've had astonishing luck, you know. I haven't
done yet what I meant to do, but at least in my failure
I've had the support of the three people I value above
all others—my wife and you and little Miss Rogers.
Most men, knowing that, would count me lucky in
these days when loyalty and honor go for so little."
He dropped his hand, went back to the littered table.
"It's nearly two o'clock," he said. "For the last time,
Carteret, my lad, will you go home."
Outside the laboratory the house was utterly still.
A light burned on each landing and in the hall, but all
the servants were in bed. Sheila was thoughtful and
considerate where her servants were concerned, and
when she was coming home late she never let them sit
up to wait for her. If she wanted anything she got it
herself, but she wouldn't have the servants sitting up.
The door of her room was open as Carteret went past
it. It was early January, and cold, and a coal fire burnt
cozily in the grate, flickering warmly and gayly on
197
polished walnut, on apple-green draperies, on a huge
four-poster bed covered with an apple-green eiderdown,
heaped high with lace-edged pillows, and turned down
only on one side; a room that was oddly personal and
disturbing, that made so perfect a setting for the
woman who slept in it. He paused for a second, a cyni-

was empty of everything approaching luxury; a nar-

held brushes of heavy, plain silver, two chairs, a bed-

Ovaltine and a tin of dry biscuits; the only ornaments
in the entire room a studio portrait in a big silver
frame of a woman kneeling beside a divan on which

Passing the two rooms young Carteret wondered if
the things people said of his Chief's wife were true,
and, if they were true, whether John Ambrose knew,
or, if he did know, whether he cared. He thought of
nothing apparently but his work, his nearly completed

198
had told him, of what was obviously common gossip.
Miss Rogers was jealous, of course. John Ambrose
was blind to it, but young Carteret knew. She was batty
on the subject of John Ambrose; had been batty about

"Miss Rogers-me? Oh, for God's sake-" But

had made no secret of the fact that her Chief had

Basil Carteret had never believed that story, what-

to make people believe she didn't, and flaunted her

Going into the cloak-room for his hat and coat, Basil

he didn't gossip much, but Mrs. Denham would. Sheila
knew exactly what she'd say: "My dear, it's perfectly
true. I saw her myself letting him out at three in the
morning. I really wonder some one doesn't tell that
poor deluded husband of hers"-and one day some
one would tell John. She wondered herself why they
hadn't told him already, putting their own construction
on something that hadn't really the basis for any con-
struction at all. Perhaps if they did tell him it would
help. If he heard the stories some of those women
were ready to tell him at least he couldn't ignore them,
at least he'd have to come out of the laboratory long
enough to thrash them out and get to the bottom of
them, so that, whatever else he did, he'd be bound,
for a time at all events, to remember her existence.
She wasn't in love with him, but then she'd never been
in love with him, so that made no real difference. But
210
if he'd just remember her for a minute out of the day;
if he'd let her feel that she was of any importance in

job-even if the job were to avenge Stephen's death-

now-he'd put up an ultimatum now, and she'd got to

and honorable thing, sent Jimmy back to the tropics
alone and stayed on with John, making the best, since
John was what he was, of a bad job, but at least living
up to what he expected of one. But if Jimmy went-
if he went and left her...
She shut the door, stood for a second in the silent,
dimly lighted hall, then picked up her coat and went
upstairs. Past the open door of her own firelit room;
up the second flight of stairs that led to the guest-
rooms and the big sunny room that had been Stephen's
day nursery, up the third flight and to the landing from
which branched off the servants' quarters. Faintly,
from behind a closed door, came the sound of gentle,
comfortable snoring. That was Mrs. Warren, who
didn't approve of the way things were going, but
didn't like to say so. She was getting old now—soon
she'd have to be pensioned off-but meantime—she'd
been so happy when Stephen came, almost idolatrous
2II
in her worship of him, terribly upset when, after the
operation, she'd learnt there could be no more children.
"Children keep married folks together," she'd said.
"An' it's right an' natural they should. 'Tis a thousand
pities there isn't going to be any more babies, Miss
Sheila."
Passing her door now, Sheila remembered what
she'd said. It was true, probably. If there had been
other children—and perhaps she'd been wrong to move
into Stephen's room like that, although John had
never questioned her decision, had never even asked
her to come back, and after a bit he obviously hadn't
even wanted her to come back. If only he'd wanted her
-if only now he'd make her feel he needed her, even
if it were only in the old, happy, care-free manner of
her childhood when his first thought had always been
of her, at least there would have been something on
which to get a grip. If she could feel that just for one
single moment John Ambrose thought more of her
than he thought of his work, if only there had been
Stephen making upon her time and her daily life his
eternal demands for companionship and attention,
Jimmy's appeal wouldn't have meant what it did now.
But John didn't want her any more. He'd got his work
and he hadn't time even to miss her, to know what she
was doing, to ask her how she filled up her time, and
Stephen was dead. And Jimmy was alive and hadn't
any work. He hadn't anything at all to do except to
want her and to love her, to make of her the indis-
pensable companion she'd once been to John and wasn't
any longer. And if John didn't want her and Jimmy
did, if Jimmy could just make her feel that there was
something left to make life worth living-then why
not go to Jimmy and make something of what was
left?
212
After all, every one thought she was Jimmy's mis-
tress. Even Mrs. Bellamy herself, although she'd been
sweet always, and when people, one after the other,
had said horrid things, she'd always defended her.
But deep down Sheila knew Jimmy's mother thought
she and her son were lovers, and so why not? If she
went with Jimmy she'd let John down, fail him hope-
lessly, so that when he'd time to remember it he'd be
terribly hurt and grieved and disappointed, but at
least there'd be something. At least, one would know
that, out of the whole miserable, heart-breaking world,
there was one person to whom one was of vital, para-

neither career nor money nor position, nor all three
put together, mattered so much as a single damn.
She went up the last flight of stairs that led to the
roof and to the laboratory John Ambrose had built on
it, pushed open the door, and went in. John Ambrose
was still working, standing just where Carteret had
left him at the long narrow table, with over his head
the glaring white light that showed up so vividly every
line and every wrinkle in a face far too old for a man
of his age. He was stirring over a Bunsen burner some-
thing that gave off a bitter acrid smell, and for a

spoke. Then as she crossed the room to the table he
looked up and saw her.
"This is very nice of you, Sheila, my dear. But isn't
it time you were in bed and asleep? I'm sure Carteret
said something about it's being very late."
"Yes," Sheila said. "It is late, but I wanted to
talk to you, and Dr. Carteret said you were here. I
shan't keep you very long, only... are you even lis-
tening, John?"
"Yes, of course," John Ambrose said. "But this—
213
I've got to give my attention to this for a few mo-

you'll understand-although I can listen quite well at
the same time. Is it anything important, my dear?"
"Yes," Sheila said. She put down her coat over a
bench and came to stand beside him at the table. The
smell from whatever he was stirring over the burner
made her feel a little sick, but she stood there quite
quietly, and as quietly dropped her bomb into the
middle of it all: "Will you give all this up and take
me away, John ?"

CHAPTER XXI
AT first, for a moment after she'd spoken, she thought
he hadn't heard her, because he just stood there as
quietly as she herself was standing and went on stirring
the smelly liquid over the burner. Then he picked it

"You can't mean that, my dear," he said. "You

"But I do mean it, John. I mean every word of it.
We can't go on like this. Surely you've seen for your-

me. I can't go on... if Stephen hadn't died...'
99
"I know," John Ambrose said. He came a step

ing. I know why you're doing it and what the end of

John Ambrose said gently:

216
you? And from what?" Awkwardly, uncertainly, he

abroad... or anywhere. You'd rather stay on here,

be something to do. Women ought to have occupation.

he minded must have struck him as silly. "Although

If at that moment John Ambrose, fussing about with
his tubes, hadn't seen something in the green liquid
that gave him food for serious and terribly exciting
hope, he might have given a second thought to those

and persuade her to go to bed a little earlier.

"Jimmy-listen. Yes, it's me. I'm sorry to wake you

you this day next week, and I'll go with you anywhere
you like."
