CHAPTER XXII
IT was cold on the moor. There'd been snow the day
before, and though old Mrs. Penrith, who came over
every day to "give an eye" to the place while it was
unoccupied, had lighted huge fires in every room, even
the cottage itself felt chilly and raw.
Mrs. Penrith said:
"'Ee do say 'ee come all alone, Ma'am," in blank
astonishment when Sheila, following her telegram,
drove up to the gate. "Wi' no servant even to mind
'ee. What be 'ee goin' to do by 'eeself then?” And
Sheila laughed.
"Manage alone," she said. "Don't fuss, Mrs. Pen-
rith, there's a darling. I've run away from home on
purpose to be alone, and I'm going to have a lovely
time. I'm tired of people."
"'Ee look tired o' more than people," Mrs. Penrith
said shrewdly." "Tis all the thngs 'ee do to London,
I've no doubt. I do hear all manner o' stories o' the
things ladies do to London." She lifted a log and flung
it on to the fire in the pretty drawing-room. "Do 'ee be
sure 'ee'll not want me to come a bit an' do fur 'ee,
Ma'am?"
"Quite sure, thanks," Sheila said. "I'll come over
and tell you if I do want you." She looked round the
big room, spoke again with the warm appreciation of
service that endeared her to all her servants. "The
cottage is lovely to come home to, Mrs. Penrith. I
220
don't know how you manage to keep it so beautifully
always."
But it was lonely, just the same, after Mrs. Penrith
went. She'd made the tea, waited till Sheila had
finished, had washed up the cups and saucers, and then
had gone. Reluctantly, but finding no inducement to
stay, she'd said:
"If 'ee do find it too lonely fur 'ee do 'ee come right
over an' I'll come back an' bide here wi' 'ee, Ma'am,"
but Sheila had assured her she'd be all right.
"I like being alone, Mrs. Penrith. Honestly, you
mustn't worry about me," she'd said, but after Mrs.
Penrith had actually gone, with the darkness of an
early January night crowding round the cottage, it was
certainly lonely, although she didn't really mind. If she
wanted anything there was the car and the telephone,
and while people talked a lot about the things that
happened to women living alone on isolated moors they
seldom, if ever, really came to pass. Far more existed
in imagination than ever had existed in actual life and
substance, and the cottage was very comfortable. It
was getting warmer now with all the doors shut and
the curtains drawn. There were great roaring fires in
every room and after Mrs. Penrith left Sheila went all
round the house, piling on logs, applying bellows
to heaps of graying ash so that flames shot up in lovely
red tongues and splashed great daubs of dancing red
on walls and furniture and on all the pretty hangings
over the choosing of which she'd spent so many happy
hours.
Sitting in the low-ceilinged sitting-room with a lamp
at her elbow and another dispelling the shadows of
distant corners, she let her thoughts run back: to the
drive across the moor that had resulted in the purchase
of the cottage in the first place; to the hours she'd
221
spent in its furnishing; to the long, happy days she
and Stephen had romped and played through together;
to the month following Stephen's death, when in its
peace and grandeur and silence she had come there
with a breaking heart in a desperate endeavor to find
something to which she might perhaps anchor a soul in
terrible danger of drifting; to the days when, with
John wrapped up in his books and in his laboratory,
she and Jimmy had looked for the elusive thing the
world calls happiness and had only succeeded, after all,
in digging up a sort of spurious imitation to which
their own particular corner of the world had given
a very different name altogether. And now she'd come
alone. Without John, without Stephen, without even
Jimmy. With for company only the disconcerting
ghosts of her own disquieting thoughts.
It had been a silly idea really. She'd have been much
wiser to have thrown up the sponge right away; to
have struck while the iron still glowed and to have
gone with Jimmy then and there. If she'd done as he'd
wanted her to do, the wrench would have been over
now. She'd have burnt her boats so that there'd have
been no turning back, no possible means of getting
back. It would have been much wiser really to have
made one clean straight slash instead of snipping at it,
and yet... She'd go away with Jimmy, of course. She'd
given him her word and she'd keep it, and in any case,
even if going with him didn't bring happiness, at least
it would bring companionship. And companionship—
even if it involved only the physical companionship
of one body calling to another-was surely better than
nothing, and Jimmy was a dear. He'd be very good to
her. That was certain. He'd be utterly dependent on
her, as his type of man was always dependent on his
women. He was one of the helpless kind who could
222
never find things; who expected his mother to look
after his clothes; who wanted a woman to "baby" him,
and that would make life a little less barren. John had
never wanted any of the things Jimmy would want.
He'd been taught very early to fend for himself and
he'd done it ever since. He didn't like being fussed
over, knew instantly and quite accurately where every-
thing was, liked to feel that he was independent of the
help of any one so that there'd never been the things
to do for him there would be for Jimmy. All the thou-
sand and one little intimate things that are in them-
selves such trifles and are yet the jam that goes so far
to make the stale bread of marriage the manna from
heaven which some women have the good luck to find.
In a week-in less than a week now-she'd be meet-
ing Jimmy. Not for just lunch or tea or dinner or to go
to a theater or a night club, but for good. He'd come
to the house directly after breakfast, following her
telephone call.
"Sheila-Angel-I'll be good to you. I swear I'll
be good to you."
He'd looked much younger and he'd wanted to kiss
her, to make love to her even then, and she'd held him
off.
"Not now, Jimmy. Please, not now. After Monday
-I'll do what you like after Monday, but until then-
I don't want you to kiss me until after Monday,
Jimmy."
Jimmy had acquiesced agreeably enough. He'd
laughed at her certainly, but he hadn't tried to force
the point.
"You're so sweet," he'd said, "and it's all so exactly
like you. This week's grace and all that. I'm not going
to try to make you change your mind, but I'd give a
223
good deal to know just why you won't come with me
now, instead of next week, Angel."
Sheila hadn't tried to explain, although she herself
knew well enough why she'd stipulated for that inter-
vening week. The "funeral" to which she'd referred
wasn't only a figure of speech. There were things that
had to be done, a past to be murdered and buried so
deeply that it could never be unearthed again, for one
thing. Secrets shared that must be tucked away out of
sight where glimpses of them couldn't hurt. Memories
that must be banished once and for all so that they
could never again poke ugly inquiring noses into things
that didn't concern them, as memories had an un-
pleasant trick of doing unless they were very definitely
exiled. And one couldn't go straight from John's house,
from the very bed one had once shared with John, to
the arms of another man. There had to be something
of decency in the affair, and the week's grace had been
the only means of providing it. A week spent alone
with the memory of the laboratory and the study would
do wonders. One could just sit there and, in front of the
fire, remind oneself steadily, implacably, that one had
asked John to take one away. That one had actually
warned him, and that he'd put his work first and re-
fused to come. One had said, not in words, perhaps,
but certainly in effect: "I'm afraid of being alone be-
cause, if you don't keep me company, Jimmy most cer-
tainly will, and I shan't have the courage to lock him
out," but it hadn't had the slightest effect on John. It
was doubtful whether he'd even heard, and so... what
was the use? One was twenty-seven-and at twenty-
seven life had done with one altogether. It had done
its best to finish things but there was still something
left, and if John couldn't produce even that small un-
satisfactory something it was only right, only fair and
224
reasonable, that some one else should find it for one.
One could even bring up, too, the argument of Jimmy's
health, because if by being unselfish one could keep
him from going straight to what Carl Bronford had
assured him would be death, and if by unselfishness in
one direction one were not causing trouble in another,
surely one could be quite sure one was doing the right
thing. If going away with Jimmy had hurt John, that
would be different. He'd be grieved, of course, but
only because she'd lost the social status with which his
teaching and upbringing had imbued her and had re-
verted to type, but in the work of the laboratory, with
the little Rogers girl and young Carteret to help him,
he'd soon forget her. Soon forget, buried as he was in
the work he'd chosen, that she'd ever been in his life
at all.
But she'd had to have the week. Death always
brought with it a pause in one's daily life. Things, the
usual everyday routine, the trivial round and common
task, always stood aside for a discreet interval when
Death put his nose round the door. The interval gave
one a chance to catch up with oneself as it were; to
bury one's dead in peace and quietness and complete-
ness; to dry one's eyes so that, at its close, one could
face the world again with one's sorrows tucked away,
decently, out of the sight of inquiring eyes. And so
she'd insisted and Jimmy had agreed, ridiculing her a
little, but being sweet about it as, in fact, he was sweet
about everything.
"All right," he'd said. "I've waited eight years, and
seven days longer won't kill me! I can afford to wait,
knowing what's coming to me afterwards. There's
never been any one in the world loved as I'm going to
love you, Angel. And I don't just want your body
either."
225
Sheila said, stung into loyalty:
"That's not fair, Jimmy. You're implying John only
wanted my body and that's not true."
"He wanted children, didn't he?" Jimmy said
logically. "Let's be straight with each other, Angel.
I'm not running Ambrose down, but-that's true,
isn't it? You told me yourself since your operation
you've never lived together-or only rarely. Be fair,
darling. I'm not abusing Ambrose, but it is true, isn't
it ?"
"John's different," Sheila said. "He isn't like us,
Jimmy, not like any of us. He's got a code, and he
believes exactly what the Prayer Book says, that mar-
riage is for the procreation of children and you see—
I can't have any more children. He thinks that to live
with me now is pandering to sex, although even then
if I'd made a point of it I think he'd have squashed all
his inhibitions for my sake. Only, you see, I didn't—
why should I when I didn't love him and there wasn't
any use? But perhaps I'm like that. Perhaps I'll be
the same with you. That, at heart, it's only the ad-
venture that counts, and we can't even have children
to help us unravel the tangle. That's a risk, you know,
Jimmy."
"It's a risk I'm perfectly willing to take," Jimmy
said confidently.
Sheila thought: 'He's so sure-of me as well as of
himself, and yet I've failed once. Suppose I fail him

marvelous goes wrong.' She said with a sort of des-

"I don't want you to be so sure, Jimmy. You see
I've been a failure as a wife, once. Only I do love you,
and that ought to help, oughtn't it? Because I didn't
love John and love must make a difference. If I'd
226
loved John I couldn't have even thought of leaving
him, could I? I couldn't leave a man I really loved,
could I?"
And Jimmy had laughed at her.
"Oh, my Angel!" he'd said. "You're sweet, and I
adore you, but you've got to stop asking yourself ques-
tions, because they're not going to get either of us
anywhere. You love me and I love you, and that's the
only thing that matters. Get that into your head and
we'll be as right as rain. And you do love me, don't

"Oh yes!" Sheila said. That at least was true, and
in the middle of all the muddle it was at least a relief
to be able to answer one question honestly and straight-

sure of, but that isn't one of them. When I think how
much I love you I wonder sometimes what I'd have
done if you hadn't come home. I love you all right,
Jimmy."
And so he'd waited there in her own little sitting-
room while she'd finished her packing for Devonshire;
while she'd run upstairs to say "good-by" to John;
while M'Ewen, who was thankful he wasn't expected
to go too, slid into the back of the Riley Nine the two
suitcases and her dressing-case, and gave her a few
warning hints: "There's ice here and there, Madam,
the A.A. says. Best watch out over tram lines and
across the Plain; and you've all the petrol you'll need.
I hope you'll have a pleasant drive, Madam." And
then Jimmy had stood on the pavement and watched
her go, with the sun on his sleek uncovered head and
in his black-circled eyes the light of a great anticipation.
And now she was here. Alone in the cottage John
had given her, with for company the corpse she had
come to bury. For four days she was free to bury her
227
dead as effectively, as completely as she knew how. On
the fifth she would take the car and drive to South-
ampton, and on the sixth-the promised, anticipated
sixth-Jimmy would meet her, and the past would be
over.
228
CHAPTER XXIII
JIMMY telephoned her every night she was away.
He'd said to her before she left: "I shall ring you at
ten o'clock every night, and God help you, woman, if
you're not there"; and at ten every night the telephone
bell had shrilled through the silence of the little cur-
tained house, and she'd listened while Jimmy talked.
There wasn't much to say, really. Most of the conver-
sation consisted of just gossip, or the sort of nonsense
Jimmy talked when life was treating him kindly. But
always at the end when the London operator had re-
minded him for the umpteenth time of the inevitable
passing of minutes and was beginning to sound a little
bored with the affair, he said the same things:
"Another day's over, Angel. Are you happy about
it, darling?"
The local operator took a sentimental interest in
those nightly telephone calls. After the first two nights
she'd say: "Here's your home call, Mrs. Ambrose,"
convinced that in the lady at the cottage she'd dis-
covered the heroine of one of those marriages found
probably more often in heaven than on earth. Once,
waiting for the final phase of the connection, she'd
forgotten to put up the key, so that Sheila had heard
her talking to some one in the office: "He calls her this
time every night. And Mrs. Penrith says they've been
married quite a time. It must be lovely to have a hus-
band" and there the London operator had cut in
229
""
briskly, "Here you are, Chagford, here's your party,
and she'd faded out. And Jimmy had been there in-
stead, with his warm, eager voice, his hopes, his cer-
tainty of the happiness that, after Southampton, would
be waiting for them wherever they went.
Mrs. Bellamy, too, had written. A letter that was
typical of her.
"Jimmy has told me, Sheila. I guessed because he
was, all at once, so happy and so gay, and when I
asked him he told me your plans. He has asked me-
and I have promised to say nothing until after you
have both gone away on Sunday, but this I must say
to you, as I have already said it to Jimmy. If this
is for your happiness and for his, then I give you my
blessing, such as it is, and for what it is worth. I am
not, I think, what the world calls a good woman. There
are things that to my mind count for more-for much,
much more than laws and legality-and the fact that
'God gives us Love and lends us one to love' must mean
something, so that we can't turn our backs on love—
ever. Only-be good to him, Sheila, for what is left of
his time. Bronford says his heart is no better, that
there are several things he doesn't like, and sometimes
I feel he should not have asked you, with his health as
it is, to throw in your lot with his. Your life together
may, you see, be so short, and then you will be lonelier
than you have ever been, because people aren't always
kind. You must remember that and take it into con-
sideration, because unless you're sure you can bear what
it may be your lot to bear when he is not there to stand
beside you, it would be better for you, even now, not to
go with him next week. Of one thing I am glad and I
ask your forgiveness, because for months I have be-
lieved to the contrary. Jimmy tells me that when you
stay together on Sunday night it will be for the first
230
time, and of that I am glad. The thought that my son
was making love to a woman under her husband's roof
hurt me terribly. There are things that under any cir-
cumstances can't be done, and that, to my mind, heads
the list. If there is any time when you need me, Sheila,
my dear, I am here. Jimmy knows that, and I should
like you to know it too. I should like you both to feel
that in spite of everything the world may say-and
will say Jimmy's mother is behind you to help when-
ever and wherever necessary.'
99
And once she'd telephoned to John, putting the call
through almost involuntarily, driven to doing it by
something stronger than herself. But Miss Rogers had
answered the telephone, primly, business-like, forcing
herself to the politeness she so patently didn't feel.
"Dr. Ambrose is engaged, I'm afraid, Mrs. Am-
brose.... I'm sorry, but he said particularly that he
was not to be disturbed.... Yes, thank you, I think
he's as well as he can be. Of course he's working ter-
ribly hard.... Yes, I'll certainly tell him you rang up.
... Yes...of course.... Good-by, Mrs. Ambrose..
yes, I'll remember.... Good-by."
In the daytime, of course, she was busy. Doing her
own housework, there was always something to be done
-meals to be cooked, plates and dishes to be washed up,
and the house to be kept tidy—and when there wasn't
work to be done she went for walks on to the moor
or into Chagford. One day it was foggy and she nearly
got lost. The mist came down quite suddenly and
silently, wrapping the world in a white deathly-cold
almost impenetrable blanket, and a shepherd brought
her home. He was a great strapping shy lad of perhaps
twenty, and when, at last, they found themselves at
the cottage gate she insisted on his coming in while she
made him tea and found a cake she'd brought that
231
morning from the village. While he ate the cake and
drank the tea he told her bits of his life. He'd been
born on the moor, his people had been moorland folk
for six generations back, shepherds all of them, and
while he didn't even attempt to dramatize his life she
learnt then something of the hardships that go to the
making of a Dartmoor shepherd's life, of fogs that
lasted for days, of white snow blankets, of searing, bit-
ing wind, and of loneliness. He told her, too, simply and
so graphically that she could see the word-pictures he
drew for her, bits about the town that, lying out there
on the lonely desolate moor, is feared by law-breaker
and law-keeper alike; of the siren that, flinging its
warning to the four winds of heaven, is at the same
time a signal of a criminal at large and a call for help
in a man hunt.
"And do you help?" Sheila asked. "If you hear it, I
mean. Would you go out and help without having any
other invitation than just the siren? Or would you
shut your eyes and give that poor devil a chance ?"
The boy smiled at her over the teacup. He'd eyes
that were the color of gentians, set in a leather-brown
face, and teeth so strong and white as to be almost
startling.
"There do be no chance fur 'ee, Miss," he said,
"not on the moor. A prisoner don't get fur. Maybe
a mile, maybe five, but there do be no chance fur much
more, although if I did see 'im I'd shut my eyes." He
went on to tell her: "My father an' mother, when the
siren do blow an' they do know there's a lad abroad,
they do set out vittles fur 'im. The lad's got to eat, do
'ee see, Miss, an' maybe 'ee never knows. None on
us do be in that place yet, but my mother says if I was
to be there an' I got out maybe, fur what she'd done,
somebody 'd do it fur me." He set down the cup and
232
saucer, stood up so that with his height and breadth
he completely dwarfed the bright cozy kitchen, and
thanked hor for his tea. "I do be proper grateful to
'ee, Miss, fur the kindness, though the moor be no
place fur a young lady to be abroad in alone. Noways
a lady so pretty." She could see his admiration for her
in his eyes, frank and unabashed. "Do 'ee bide to home,
a bit, while the mist's down. 'Tis wiser, Miss."
Sheila wondered what he'd say if she told him the
truth, that she'd no use for wisdom and had turned her
back on it, once and for all. But she didn't tell him.
What was the use? He wouldn't understand, and she
would have hated to see the admiration in his eyes die
and give place to a puzzled distrust. So she smiled at
him, thanked him gravely in return.
"If it hadn't been for you I'd have been on the
moor still, and I've loved all the things you've told me.
Perhaps if I'd been staying longer we'd have met again,
but I'm afraid we shan't now. I'm going away on
Saturday." She held out her hand. "I'm going on a
journey that's important too, and somehow-will you
wish me luck? I think I'd like you to wish me luck
before I start."
He took her hand, held it for a second in a hard
brown fist.
"If so be as 'tis fur happiness then I do most sartinly
wish 'ee luck, Miss," he said. "An' I'll be gettin' on
now. 'Tis late an' the mist's not risin' any. I'll say
good-night to 'ee, Miss, an' I'm proper grateful to 'ee
for the tea an' the cake."
After he'd gone the kitchen seemed oddly empty
and lonely. The boy had been so big and his voice had
a nice, heartening, friendly sound. It was funny, when
she'd asked him to wish her luck the way he'd almost
quoted Mrs. Bellamy. "If so be as 'tis fur happiness,"
233
he'd said. Oh-well, one would know soon enough, and
if it weren't for happiness, if she and Jimny couldn't
find happiness in spite of everything, it just couldn't be
helped, that's all. And whatever happened, wherever
one went, whatever one did, one couldn't ever be
lonelier than one had been since John had forgotten
her in favor of his research work and since Stephen
had died.
It began to rain on Friday morning, steadily, as it
does rain sometimes on Dartmoor, with a dogged per-
sistence worthy, as a general rule, of a better cause.
She spent the morning tidying up. There were things
to be put away, to be burnt, to be locked up now that
she wouldn't be coming back, and then after lunch she
walked into Chagford to buy stamps and a daily paper.
After tea she would write the letters that had to be
written. One to the Bank about an address for for-
warding mail, and one to John, and then she'd finish
packing. There wasn't much to be done, because most
of her things had been sent direct to Southampton to
await arrival there. She'd seen about that before leav-
ing home, so there wasn't much here to do. Just the
two suitcases she'd brought with her and a box that
held some things of Stephen's she couldn't bear to
leave behind.
Walking down the lane to the village with the rain
beating against her mackintosh and on to her oilskin
cap she was sorry she'd ever come back to the cottage.
She'd had some happy days there, some of the happiest
days of her life, and they were over. After to-morrow
so much more would be over too. She'd be starting out
on a life that had no connection with any life she'd ever
known, that couldn't have any connection ever, learn-
ing new lessons, seeing new places, meeting new people.
There'd be people, too, she wouldn't want to meet yet
234
at all events, who perhaps wouldn't know her any
longer when they did meet. Until John divorced her
she'd be an outcast to people who counted in the social
scheme, avoided uncomfortably, only welcomed by
those who'd skeletons of their own in cupboards of
their own history, who had reached the point where
the opinions of other people couldn't either make or
mar very much more reputations that had pretty well
reached rock bottom. But one would get used to that
in time, to looking the other way, tactfully, so that the
people who didn't want to see one might be spared too
much painful embarrassment; to meeting as social
equals men and women one would never have thought
of knowing at home; to living abroad because, out of
England at least, there were fewer people who would
know the truth and consequently fewer who would
whisper among themselves and point one out as a light
woman, the mistress of a man to whom one wasn't
married.
Later, with the divorce over, it would be easier. Not
much, because she'd flaunted her indiscretion too
flagrantly in people's respectable faces. If she and
Jimmy had been lovers on the side, meeting under
fictitious names in shady Paris hotels, all well and good.
The world doesn't mind what it doesn't see, shuts
discreet ears when it doesn't want to hear things that
are better kept as secrets. But she and Jimmy hadn't
made a secret about anything, and the world could be
cruel-terribly cruel-to people who paid no attention
to the things that in its opinion were or weren't done.
She wished dully that Jimmy hadn't forced her hand.
Even loving him as much as she did, as all these years
she had loved him, to go away like that, to face criti-
cisms, slights, and open ostracism wasn't going to be
easy. One had grown accustomed to popularity now;
235
to being asked everywhere, and it wasn't going to be
easy, even with Jimmy to counteract it all, to give
it up. To know that in future one must get used to
being left out and either tactfully not seen, or openly,
palpably snubbed. But one couldn't have one's cake
and eat it. Years ago even John had taught one that,
so that either Jimmy or respectability had to go by the
board. And one did love Jimmy. The thought of him,
even mixed up as he had to be with so much that was
horrid and unpalatable and unpleasant all round, sent
little floods of warm happiness running all over one's
body, so that, as long as one could forget duty and
Stephen, who was so indissolubly bound up with John,
one was almost completely happy again. And, of course,
in time one would forget John. Time was kind like
that, and sooner or later every one forgot. That, at
least, was true, because every one said so. One heard
it on every side. Mrs. Shepherd, writing from Colney
Head when Stephen died, had put it actually into black
and white. "Time, my dear, is a great healer," she'd
written, so that it must be true. And if it were true,
if the time ever came when one could forget John and
the disappointment one had been to him, the blow one
had struck at his pride and his faith and the love that
one knew still existed somewhere amongst the tubes
and bottles and smells, life could be very beautiful.
One could, Sheila told herself for the thousandth time,
be terribly happy with Jimmy if one could only forget
John and all the things for which John stood.
She reached the village, bought her stamps, went
across the square to the newspaper shop. There were
half a dozen bill-boards propped up round the doors; a
yellow board that proclaimed in letters a foot high:
"Shocking Scandal on a Baby Farm"; a racing paper
setting forth the successes of its own particular tipster
236
-"Wiseacre gives 20-1 winner"; The Times, discreet,
guarded; the Morning Post, a little less discreet, per-
haps; the Daily Telegraph, the Daily Mirror, and in-
side, on a long trestle, the morning papers that hadn't
been already sold.
The woman in the shop saw her coming, beamed at
her.
"We wondered if we'd be seeing you, Mrs. Am-
brose," she said. "Or, perhaps I ought to say Lady
Ambrose now. We're all proud of the doctor, as I'm
sure you must be too. 'Tis a great day for you, and a
crying shame the dear little lad couldn't have lived
to see where his daddy's got. I'm sure all the village'll
wish to congratulate you and the doctor, Mrs. Am-
brose, though it'll be a loss to London, no doubt, when
he goes."
Dumbly, stupidly, Sheila held out her hand for the
paper, muttered something, stood staring blindly, un-
seeingly, till the mists cleared and she could read the
staggering, amazing thing that blinked up at her from
the printed page, because John's name was in it twice.
He was in the New Year's Honors List as one of the
baronets created in recognition of "valuable services
rendered to the community in connection with research
work in the campaign against infantile paralysis"; and
below that notice was another, which said that Dr.
John Ambrose, now, as the Honors List would show,
one of the year's new baronets, had been offered and
had accepted the post of Head of the Research De-
partment of Mr. Simon Shepherd's world-famous
Clinic in Colney Head, U.S.A., on the retirement of
Dr. Joseph Mallory, and went on to give further de-
tails. It gave, in the first place, a short sketch of the
new baronet himself, then of the Clinic, and finally of
the millionaire whose name it bore, who was con-
237
spicuous in his native America and in England, as well,
indeed, as throughout the scientific world for the work
he had done in the interests of his own country and
those of other nations, but was also famous for the
ceaseless battle he had waged and continued to wage
in the social world against the steadily growing menace
of divorce and domestic immorality.
"It is," the paper said in conclusion, "a well-known
fact in this and in other countries that Mr. Simon
Shepherd will employ no one who has figured in any
way in the Divorce Court of his own or of any other
country; and one can only assume, without, we trust,
being guilty of impertinence, that the exceptionally
happy married life of the new Superintendent who, it
will be remembered, married some eight years ago his
ward, Miss Sheila Kavanagh, in conjunction with his
very high scientific qualifications, has been of infinite
assistance in making him eligible for an appointment
that, while he has so ably deserved the honor it brings
with it, could in the curious circumstances only have
been offered to one who fulfills both halves of the con-
ditions Mr. Shepherd expects from those who work
both with and for him.'

CHAPTER XXIV
AFTERWARDS, looking back on it all, Sheila could
never remember very accurately what happened after
she read those amazing paragraphs in the morning
paper.
At first she hadn't believed it. Things like that, fairy
stories like that, weren't true. They couldn't be true,
because if they were John would certainly have told
her something. He might be busy, but a thing like that
-a huge colossal thing that affected his whole life, his
whole career he couldn't treat a thing like that even as
he treated her. No one, not even men buried, absorbed,
soaked in their work as John was, could be made a
baronet and appointed Superintendent of the Research
Department in a place like Colney Head, which was
the job of all others he'd longed for ever since he'd
first been inside the silver birch doors of Simon Shep-
herd's magnificent laboratory, and, in the interests of
his work, just forget it, think no more about it, push
it into the background of their minds like that and
never say a word about it, even to his wife. So that it
stood to reason it couldn't be true. The papers must
have made a mistake; must have got the names wrong,
and yet it was only rarely, only terribly rarely, that
papers made mistakes. Mistakes were made by people,
certainly, but it wasn't likely, not really likely, that a
reliable paper, and not just one but four or five of
239
them, would make a mistake over a thing as important
as that.
And if it weren't a mistake? If what the papers said
were true? What then? Dear God, what then? Because
if it were true, if John had been offered-and had ac-
cepted the office of Superintendent of the Research
Department at Colney Head, what about Jimmy? And
herself? And the divorce from John which would set
her free to marry Jimmy? Simon Shepherd would have
nothing to do with divorced people. He wouldn't even
employ them if he knew. He'd said so again and again,
and even the paper had drawn attention to what in
these modern days, when one took divorce, so to speak,
in one's stride, paying no attention to it, giving it not
so much as a second thought as one went on one's way,
amounted almost to an idiosyncrasy. And if she went
away with Jimmy-as she was going, not in the dim
future, but actually to-morrow-then either John must
divorce her and lose his job, or for all that was left
of her life she must live in what the world called "sin."
Away from all the friends who helped to make life so
pleasant; out of England probably, since England is so
small when people have something to hide; and always
on the alert, on the qui vive, even in foreign countries
for people who might recognize them and who, recog-
nizing them, would point them out to others, would
whisper or snigger according to their ideas of morality.
Or who might be people that cared nothing at all for
morality, cared nothing either for honor or even de-
cency, who prided themselves on "taking life as they
found it" and were the people John had always said
one couldn't ever know and could never, under any
conditions, under any circumstances, ask to the house
or make friends of. If John didn't divorce her those
people would be her friends-or else there'd be no
240
friends to have, save perhaps, here and there, a rare
treasured being who had the courage to stand by her.
And if John did divorce her, if she went away with
Jimmy and he divorced her, as, of course, she would
ask him to divorce her and as he would feel in honor
bound to do, then what about the job at Colney Head?
That, next to the discovery of that mysterious germ
and its antidote, was the aim and goal and ambition
of his whole life. He'd made no secret of it.
"If I could just get my fingers on Joseph Mallory's
job." He'd said the same thing over and over again
in those early days when they came back from the
States and he had time to talk of his work. "Sheila,
think of it, think of what one could do. With the
money he's got behind him. When I look round at the
pettifogging hole I've got to work in, and the tools
that are all I can afford, and then remember that
laboratory-God, if only I could get the job Joseph
Mallory's got."
And now, if what those papers said was true, he'd
got it. He'd got a baronetcy, too, but that didn't mat-
ter. The baronetcy was an honor, of course. A great
honor that was coveted tremendously, but it wouldn't
mean to John Ambrose what that job at Colney Head
meant. And if he were divorced, mixed up in a divorce
case-the job would go. Simon Shepherd wouldn't have
him. He might be the only man in the world really
qualified to hold it, but once he'd been in a divorce
court nothing would ever make Simon Shepherd break
through the rules by which he so rigidly governed his
life and give him the job in spite of it.
It was raining harder now, coming down with a
steady, relentless insistence that in some way was oddly
in keeping with the depression that, deepening with
every step she took, seemed to be closing round her.
241
There were little streams of water running down the
gullies, pools of water lying all over the road, and the
few villagers who had to be out hurried from door to
door, clutching umbrellas over their heads against a
rising wind and grabbing mackintoshes about their
scurrying bodies. A small boy, soaked to the skin, sat
on a stone by the wayside, dabbling saturated shoes in
an entrancing puddle, and was screamed at shrilly from
a doorway by his exasperated mother. A flock of
patient sheep huddled together under a leafless tree in
the vain hope of shelter, two red cows, whose horns
gave them a curiously raffish expression, chewed their
cud defiantly, staring at her as she passed. Up at the
cottage it would be warm and cozy. She'd made up all
the fires before she came out, forecasting the falling
barometer, left the tea-tray ready on the hearth in the
pretty sitting-room, but quite suddenly, seeing the gate
ahead of her, Sheila knew she couldn't go back there
yet. Her mind, just then, wasn't in tune with comfort,
with warmth, with pretty gay chintzes and a kettle
bubbling cheerily on a glowing hearth, because, even
while she deliberately refused to face the truth, she
knew perfectly what she'd got to do. That, with John's
job dependent upon her, with his whole future de-
pendent upon what she did within the next forty-eight
hours, she couldn't go with Jimmy. John might not
need her personally. He might forget her very exist-
ence, as so palpably he had forgotten it since he could
leave her to learn news like that from a casually pur-
chased daily paper, ignore the fact that she was his
wife, and, as his wife, entitled to more than just a
passing thought, neglect her so that she might just as
well have no husband at all, but there were certain
things that, in spite of it all, one couldn't do and keep
to oneself even a torn rag of decency. Years ago John
242
had taught her the meaning of "noblesse oblige." It
was funny how things stuck in one's mind. Silly little
bits of things that, at the time, had seemed of no
importance at all, because that incident of "noblesse
oblige" was as clear to-day as though it had happened
yesterday, as though it were something vitally out-
standing when, as a matter of fact, it had been nothing
but a trifling occurrence.
Old Colonel Egerton's bailiff had died and because
Colonel Egerton himself was laid up with gout and
unable to attend the funeral, Ronnie, who was only
eleven, but who was his heir, and who, in the absence
abroad of his uncle, was the representative of the
family, had had to go instead. He hadn't wanted to go
either. John Ambrose had arranged a pantomime party
that afternoon to celebrate Sheila's tenth birthday. He
himself had to go to the funeral, but Miss Ellesmere
was taking all the children, and Ronnie was to have
been one of the party. And then, at the last moment,
old Colonel Egerton had crocked up with gout, and
Ronnie was told off to go to the bailiff's funeral. He'd
come himself to see John Ambrose and to explain;
furious with his grandfather, obviously resentful, and
after he'd gone, Sheila had wanted to know why, and
John Ambrose had told her, sorry for Ronnie but
recognizing the force of the old man's dictum.
"People in Ronnie's shoes have duties, my child.
You can't, you see, when you occupy an important
position in the world, always please yourself. It's one
of the disadvantages of being important, and is often,
as you have seen, a great nuisance. But it's there just
the same. If you've got a position in the world you've
got to live up to it, whether you like it or not, and the
necessity for living up to it is called 'noblesse oblige.'
The chances are that neither you nor I will ever reach
243
such a peak of national eminence that we shall be called
upon to exercise the prerogative, but still it's just as
well you should know what it means, which, boiled
down, amounts simply to this, my child. If you're a
person of any sort of importance whatever you've got
to do your job, and it doesn't matter a twopenny damn
whether you like it or not. See?"
With poor Ronnie as an illustration, Sheila had seen
clearly enough, though it had all seemed curiously para-
doxical, because, being rich and important was surely
all the more reason for being able to please oneself.
But Ronnie had had to give up the party and go to the
funeral, and now, for the first time in her life, she was
being called upon to exercise the same prerogative
herself.
She hadn't perhaps reached what John had so ex-
travagantly called "a peak of national eminence."
She wasn't, in the eyes of the world, a person of any
importance at all, but the parallel held just the same.
By marrying her, John had made her a lady, and there
were things a "lady" had to do, duties she must per-
form whether she liked it or not, and, because of that
appalling handicap of being a "lady," perform them
with at least an outward and visible show of the
graciousness and courtesy and dignity that all the world
over are the attributes of birth and breeding and social
standing.
So when she came back from that tramp in the rain
she knew what she had to do. By that time she'd looked
the future full in the face, unflinchingly, bravely, cour-
ageously. Jimmy and all the love, all the possible hap-
piness, all the joy that were part of a life that held
Jimmy, must go. To-night, instead of writing to John,
she would write to Jimmy, tell him the truth, say
"good-by" and never see him again. And to-morrow
244
she would go back to London, to take the place to
which duty had called her, to listen with a smile while
people hurled congratulations at her feet, to be the
charming decorative wife of a man who was so
wrapped up in his work that he'd forgotten even her
bare existence, to take up, in fact, as years ago Ronnie
had been called upon to take it up, the exhausting,
depressing burden of "noblesse oblige."
The gate of the little drive that led to the cottage
was open when she reached it. She thought: "I sup-
pose the baker's been while I've been out and forgotten
to shut it," and then, turning into the drive herself,
saw the car that stood in front of the door. It was a
big blue car and it had evidently traveled a long dis-
tance, and moreover traveled fast, because it was so
splashed with mud from top to bottom that for an
appreciable moment she didn't recognize it at all. Then
a man came out of the front door and stood in the
porch for a second staring in front of him at the
streaming rain, turned and saw her, came to meet her,
bareheaded even in the rain, with both hands out-
stretched to take hers.
"I had to come," John Ambrose said. He sounded,
Sheila thought dazedly, like some schoolboy who has
just been unexpectedly granted a reprieve from well-
merited detention. "I tried to telephone you last night
after Shepherd went, but the damned line was engaged
for so long that I couldn't wait. And then I thought
I'd come instead. I wanted the news to be a surprise
to you, but I wanted to tell you myself, so I started
right away. I thought I'd make the trip by breakfast-
time, and then I broke a spring and got held up for
five hours in Salisbury. I knew the baronetcy was com-
ing, of course. But I only saw Shepherd at eight o'clock
last night. He came over especially to offer me the job.
245
He thought, poor deluded fool, that I might need per-
suading. Sheila, sweet, d'you realize what it means?
I've got the job I've wanted more than I've ever
wanted anything in my life except you. And d'you
realize that I owe it all to you? Shepherd said that
himself. He said: 'I want you. I want you badly, but
I want your wife just as much. She's a very charming
lady, and she's the sort we want.' Sheila, my dear, my
dear, d'you understand what it means? I've owed a
lot to you in my life. All the happiness I've ever
known. And now-this. Are you as proud of your hus-
band as I am of my wife, Lady Ambrose?"
"I'm terribly proud," Sheila said. She slid a hand
through his arm, walked with him out of the rain into
the sheltered warmth of the little cozy hall. The after-
noon post had come while she'd been out, and a letter
lay on the chest just inside the door. It was addressed
in a heavy, sprawling hand and was from Jimmy—the
letter he wrote her every day. She picked it up as she
passed, and with it in her hand went with John Am-
brose into the firelit sitting-room. The tea-tray was just
where she'd left it and the kettle was boiling. She
thought dully, stupidly: 'I put all this ready myself,
before I went out, when I'd left John altogether, when
in my soul I'd already been unfaithful to him. And it
was less than two hours ago. And now-it's all over.
In two hours the whole world's turned upside down.
John is here. When he goes I'll go back with him.
Life'll be just what it's been during these last two
years. And I'll have to live up to it-to pretend-never
to let him know-never to spoil things by letting him
know, but just to go on. Alone always. Without
Stephen-without Jimmy. Being a good woman and a
lady.' She went over to the fire, dropped into it, un-
246
opened and unread, the letter she held, lifted the
kettle, and made the tea.
"It was dear of you to come, John, all this way,
just to tell me. You'll stay now, won't you? I mean
there's no hurry, is there, to get back again just for a
day or two?"
"I'm afraid there is," John Ambrose said. He took
the tea she held out to him, drank it thirstily. "I've
got to get back right away. I'd no business even to
take the day off like this, because now-with this job
at Colney Head-it's doubly important I should make
good. I brought M'Ewen with me. He's down in the
village sending off some telegrams and cables, but he'll
take the little car back if you'll come with me. I'd like
you to come with me if you will, Sheila. It isn't often
we get the opportunity of a trip and a chat together.
And at Colney Head I'm afraid there'll be even less."
He said, with sudden wistfulness: "I wish Stephen
hadn't died, Sheila. I'd like him to have lived to see
me in charge of Colney Head."
"Yes," Sheila said. She drank her tea quickly. It
was very hot, but it provided a perfectly valid excuse
for the tears that drenched her eyes. And she mustn't
cry. If she began to cry now-if once she began to cry
now "I wish that too-terribly. But I'd like to
come with you, John. I was-leaving here-to-morrow,
anyway, so it won't take me ten minutes to get ready.
I'm sorry you can't stay, but-I can be ready any time
you like."
"You're splendid," John Ambrose said. He had
taken a magazine from the attaché-case he'd brought
in from the car, was turning its pages, and already his
attention, she knew, was wandering. "I can't think
what I did before I found you, Sheila." He settled
down more comfortably in front of the fire, his long
247
legs sprawling across the rug. "I can spare an hour,
and then I think we'll have to go. We can stay over-
night in Salisbury and go on first thing in the morning.
Will that be all right for you?"
"Perfectly," Sheila said. She got up, began gather-
ing up the tea-tray. An hour would give her plenty of
time. She'd only her suitcase to finish, to wire to
Jimmy: "Don't telephone to-night-letter in post,"
because she couldn't let him call her and be told curtly
and briefly that there was "no reply," and the letter
wouldn't take long. All she had to say could be said
in a very few words-the fewer the better really-and
then she'd be ready.
Carrying the tea-tray, she went across the room to
the door that led to the hall and thus to the kitchen.
John was already deep in the medical magazine he'd
brought with him from London, but as she reached the
door he looked up for a second.
"Oh, Sheila," he said, "there was a letter for you
on the floor when I came in just now. The postman
came when you were out, I suppose. I picked it up and
put it on the chest. Did you see it?"
"Yes, thank you," Sheila said. Her voice was quite
steady, devoid of any kind of expression. "I found it."
She added levelly: "It was from Jimmy. But there's-
no answer, John."
